Equivariant critical point theory and bifurcation of 3 ​ d 3d gravity-capillary Stokes waves
Tommaso Barbieri, Massimiliano Berti, Marco Mazzucchelli

Abstract




Abstract.

We establish novel existence
results of
3d
gravity-capillary periodic traveling waves.
In particular we prove the
bifurcation of multiple, geometrically distinct truly
3d
Stokes waves
having the
same
momentum of
any
non-resonant

2d
Stokes wave.
This unexpected
clustering phenomenon of Stokes waves,
observed in physical fluids, is
a
fundamental consequence of the Hamiltonian nature of the water waves
equations, their symmetry groups, and novel topological arguments.
We employ a variational Lyapunov-Schmidt reduction combined with
equivariant Morse-Conley theory for a functional defined on a joined
topological space invariant under a
2
-torus action.
Although the reduction is a priori singular
near the hyperplanes of
2d
-waves, we circumvent this difficulty by exhaustive use of the symmetry groups. This approach yields a complete bifurcation picture of
3d
gravity-capillary Stokes waves.

MSC 2020
: 76B15, 58E05, 35C07, 37K50, 58E07.

Keywords
: Bifurcation of Stokes waves, equivariant Morse-Conley theory, gravity-capillary water waves, Variational methods for Hamiltonian PDEs.

1
Introduction

In 1847 Stokes
[
52
]
discovered the nonlinear
bifurcation of
2d
-space
periodic traveling water waves solutions

u(x-ct)
, nowadays referred to as Stokes waves,
that are
constant along the direction
orthogonal to the propagation speed.
These waves
were the first global-in-time solutions ever discovered for dispersive PDEs.
Their first rigorous mathematical existence
proof
was then achieved by Nekrasov
[
44
]
, Levi-Civita
[
37
]

and Struik
[
53
]
almost one century ago, in
[
25
,
19
]
with vorticity and in
[
59
]
including the effects of capillarity.
Since then, numerous results
have been obtained using a variety of innovative
approaches, including global bifurcation theory, see e.g.

[
1
,
46
,
14
,
56
,
36
]
and references therein.
The literature
regarding traveling water waves solutions
is immense and we
refer to
[
31
]
for extended presentations.

Mathematically rigorous results about genuinely
3d
-Stokes waves (namely non constant along any direction)
are less numerous; we refer to
[
29
]
for striking experimental realizations in laboratory.
The first bifurcation theorems of
3d
gravity-capillary Stokes waves are due to Reeder-Shinbrot
[
48
]
for symmetric diamonds, under non-resonance
assumptions on the linear wave vectors, extended by Groves-Mielke
[
27
]
,
Groves-Haragus
[
26
]
,
Bagri-Groves
[
3
]
, Nilsson
[
45
]
, via a spacial dynamics approach, also for non symmetric diamonds. We mention that
Theorem 5 in

[
26
]

deals with also a
resonant case, applying the Weinstein-Moser center theorem.

The general resonant case
when multiple, arbitrarily many,
linear wave vectors correspond to the same resonant speed
c_{*}

has been addressed by
Craig-Nicholls
[
15
,
16
]
by
a variational approach, demonstrating the occurrence of small amplitude

3d
gravity-capillary Stokes waves
with
momentum
non
collinear to any resonant wave vector;
this elegant work constitutes the most relevant precursor to our results as we comment below. We also quote
the recent bifurcation results
[
38
,
28
]
of doubly periodic gravity-capillary non-resonant Beltrami flows
and with small non-zero vorticity in
[
50
]
.
Bifurcation of
3d
Stokes waves in the pure gravity case is a small divisor problem and has been addressed in Iooss-Plotnikov
[
32
,
33
]
.

In this paper we prove new
bifurcation results
of
3d
gravity-capillary Stokes waves periodic over
an arbitrary
2
-dimensional lattice
\Gamma
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
The following bifurcation result remained unnoticed so far:

For any value of
gravity
g>0
, surface tension
\kappa>0
, depth

{\mathtt{h}}\in(0,+\infty]
, any
arbitrary lattice

\Gamma\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
,
and any resonant speed
c_{*}
,
there exist multiple genuinely

3d
Stokes waves spatially
\Gamma
-periodic, having the
same
momentum
of a non-resonant
2d
Stokes wave,
thus
collinear
to some linear wave vector;
see Theorem
1.1
for a precise formulation.

The emergence
of such traveling waves follows by purely topological arguments based on equivariant Morse-Conley theory for an Hamiltonian invariant under the action of the group
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{\Gamma}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}

where
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}:={\mathbb{R}}^{2}/\Gamma
.
Such symmetry is induced by the group of translations and the Hamiltonian and reversible nature of the water waves equations.
Theorem
1.2

improves

[
15
]
about existence of
Stokes waves with momentum non-collinear with any linear wave vector; actually,
in this case, Theorem

1.3
-(
\mathfrak{I}
)
implies that
any Stokes wave is
3
-dimensional.
Theorem
1.3
provides non-existence results of

2d
or
3d

small amplitude Stokes waves, according to the value of its momentum.
Altogether these results
provide the complete
bifurcation scenario
of
3d
gravity-capillary Stokes waves, as we
detail
in Section
1.2
.

The topological principle behind the
proof of
Theorem
1.1

is roughly the following.
Via a variational Lyapunov-Schmidt procedure –which is potentially singular near the

2d
-waves hyperplanes– the problem is reduced
to the search of stationary points of a reduced Hamiltonian function

H:\mathbb{M}_{a}\to{\mathbb{R}}

defined on the
level sets
\mathbb{M}_{a}

of the “reduced momentum”.
For values of the momentum
a
collinear with a resonant wave vector, we show that

\mathbb{M}_{a}
is equivariantly homeomorphic to
the compact
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}
-topological space

(1.1)

\mathbb{M}_{a}\cong\big(\,\underbrace{S^{2d_{-}-1}\times S^{2d_{+}-1}}_{3d-\text{waves}}\,\big)\star\underbrace{S^{1}}_{2d-\text{waves}}\,,\quad d_{-},d_{+}\in{\mathbb{N}}\,,

where
S^{2d_{\pm}-1}

are unit spheres,

\star
denotes the topological
join

product, and
the circle

S^{1}
corresponds to the celebrated non-resonant
2d
Stokes orbit.
The waves on
S^{2d_{-}-1}\times S^{2d_{+}-1}
are
3d
.
The compactness of
\mathbb{M}_{a}
and its rich equivariant topology under the group
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}

imply a lower bound
for the number of geometrically distinct

{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-Stokes waves which are truly
3
-dimensional.
Several topological and analytical difficulties are posed
by the coexistence of
2d
- and
3d
-waves inside
\mathbb{M}_{a}
, overcome
in Theorem
3.14
.
On a more technical side
we mention that, since
\mathbb{M}_{a}
in (
1.1
) is not a manifold,
we
develop a Conley type approach
to equivariant Lüsternik-Schinerlman theory
for
the search of rest points of a gradient-like flow for
H
on
\mathbb{M}_{a}
,
as we explain in Section
1.3
.
Theorem
1.2
is simpler since in the non-collinear case
(as in
[
15
]
) the space

\mathbb{M}_{a}

is diffeomorphic to the manifold
S^{2d_{-}-1}\times S^{2d_{+}-1}
which consists of only
3d
-waves.

Variational bifurcation theory with symmetry originated in the seminal works of
Weinstein
[
57
]
, Moser
[
42
]
, Fadell-Rabinowitz

[
21
]
, and Fadell-Husseini
[
20
]
with main applications to the
multiplicity of periodic
orbits of finite dimensional Hamiltonian systems. Later Floer-Zehnder
[
24
]
reinterpreted Fadell-Rabinowitz’s work in the context of Conley theory and Bartsch
[
6
]
generalized
[
57
,
42
]
under weaker assumptions. Most of these works deal with
S^{1}
symmetries. More general group actions were also considered by Bartsch and Bartsch-Clapp
[
7
]
.
In the present work,
we encounter the natural actions of 2-tori
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}
, and semidirect products
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
.

We expect
these ideas and techniques may lead to many other important bifurcation results in Hamiltonian PDEs.
For this reason,
Part
I
provides a rigorous and self-contained
presentation
of equivariant critical
point theory in metric spaces.
The Lüsternik-Schinerlman arguments are somehow originally introduced via the annihilator of the local cohomology.
We now rigorously present the results.

1.1
Formulation of the problem

We consider the motion of a
3d
incompressible and inviscid fluid in irrotational regime under the action of gravity and capillarity forces
at the free surface. The fluid occupies the time dependent region

(1.2)

{\mathcal{D}}_{\eta(t,\cdot)}:=\big\{(x,y)\in{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}\times{\mathbb{R}}\ |\ -{\mathtt{h}}\leq y\leq\eta(t,x)\big\}\,,\quad{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}={\mathbb{R}}^{2}/\Gamma\,,

where
{\mathtt{h}}\in(0,\infty]
is the, possibly infinite, bottom depth, and
\Gamma\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
is the
2
-dimensional lattice

(1.3)

\Gamma=\big\{m_{1}2\pi{\bf v}_{1}+m_{2}2\pi{\bf v}_{2}\,,\ m_{1},m_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}\big\}

with linearly independent generators

2\pi{\bf v}_{1}
,

2\pi{\bf v}_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.

The
irrotational and divergence free velocity field is the gradient
\nabla_{x,y}\Phi(t,x,y)
of a scalar velocity potential
\Phi(t,x,y)
which is harmonic in
{\mathcal{D}}_{\eta}
.
Inside
{\mathcal{D}}_{\eta}
the velocity field evolves according to
the Euler equations and the water waves
dynamics is
determined by
two boundary conditions at the free surface.
The first condition is that the pressure of the fluid
plus the gravity and capillary forces at the free surface is equal to the constant
atmospheric pressure (dynamic boundary condition).
The second one is
that the fluid particles at the free surface remain on it along the evolution
(kinematic boundary condition).
Denoting by
\psi(t,x)
the value of
\Phi
on the free surface
(x,\eta(t,x))
, the water waves equations are governed by
the Zakharov-Craig-Sulem system
[
58
,
17
]
of quasi-linear non-local equations

(1.4)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\eta=G(\eta)\psi\\
\displaystyle{\partial_{t}\psi=-g\eta-\frac{|\partial_{x}\psi|^{2}}{2}+\frac{(\partial_{x}\eta\cdot\partial_{x}\psi+G(\eta)\psi)^{2}}{2(1+|\partial_{x}\eta|^{2})}+\kappa\,\text{div}\Big(\frac{\partial_{x}\eta}{\sqrt{1+|\partial_{x}\eta|^{2}}}\Big)}\end{cases}

where

g>0
is gravity,
\kappa>0
is the surface tension and
G(\eta)
is the Dirichlet-Neumann operator

G(\eta)\psi:=(\partial_{y}\Phi-\partial_{x}\eta\cdot\partial_{x}\Phi)|_{y=\eta(x)}\,.

Hamiltonian structure.

The equations (
1.4
) are the Hamiltonian system

(1.5)

\partial_{t}u=X_{{\mathcal{H}}}(u)\,,\quad u=\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
\psi\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad X_{{\mathcal{H}}}(u)=J\nabla^{L^{2}}{\mathcal{H}}(u)=\begin{pmatrix}\nabla^{L^{2}}_{\psi}{\mathcal{H}}\\
-\nabla^{L^{2}}_{\eta}{\mathcal{H}}\end{pmatrix}(\eta,\psi)\,,

where
J
is the Poisson tensor

(1.6)

J:=\begin{pmatrix}0&\text{Id}\\
-\text{Id}&0\end{pmatrix}\,,

\nabla^{L^{2}}=(\nabla^{L^{2}}_{\eta},\nabla^{L^{2}}_{\psi})
denotes the
L^{2}
gradient and the Hamiltonian

(1.7)

{\mathcal{H}}(u):=\frac{1}{2|\Gamma|}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}}(\psi\,G(\eta)\psi+g\,\eta^{2})\,{\rm d}x+\frac{\kappa}{|\Gamma|}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}}\sqrt{1+|\partial_{x}\eta|^{2}}\,{\rm d}x

is the sum of
kinetic, potential and
capillary energies.
Introducing the symplectic form

(1.8)

\boldsymbol{\Omega}\big(u,u_{1}\big)=\big(J^{-1}u,u_{1}\big):=\frac{1}{|\Gamma|}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}}\big(\eta(x)\,\psi_{1}(x)-\psi(x)\,\eta_{1}(x)\big)\,{\rm d}x

where

(f,g):=\frac{1}{|\Gamma|}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}}f(x)\cdot g(x)\,{\rm d}x

denotes the
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{\Gamma},\mathbb{R}^{2})
real scalar product,
the Hamiltonian vector field

X_{{\mathcal{H}}}(u)
is the symplectic gradient of
{\mathcal{H}}
, namely

(1.9)

{\rm d}_{u}{\mathcal{H}}(u)=\boldsymbol{\Omega}(X_{\mathcal{H}}(u),\cdot)\,.

{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
and
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}
-symmetry.

The water waves system
(
1.4
) possesses both a

{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
and a
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}
-symmetry.
Indeed (
1.4
) is
reversible
, namely

(1.10)

{\mathcal{H}}\circ\rho={\mathcal{H}}\qquad\text{where}\qquad\rho\begin{pmatrix}{}\eta\\
\psi\end{pmatrix}(x):=\begin{pmatrix}{}\eta(-x)\\
-\psi(-x)\end{pmatrix}

is an involution (
\rho=\rho^{-1}
),
and
since the bottom of (
1.2
) is flat, it is also space invariant, namely

(1.11)

{\mathcal{H}}\circ\tau_{\theta}={\mathcal{H}}\qquad\text{where}\qquad(\tau_{\theta}u)(x):=u(x-\theta)\,,\quad\forall\theta\in{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}\,.

According to Nöether theorem the
Hamiltonian system (
1.5
)
possesses
two prime integrals
associated to the translation invariance, notably the momentum

(1.12)

\mathcal{I}(u):=\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{I}_{1}\\
\mathcal{I}_{2}\end{pmatrix}(u)\quad\text{where}\quad\mathcal{I}_{i}(u):=\frac{1}{|\Gamma|}\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}}\psi\,\partial_{x_{i}}\eta\,{\rm d}x\,.

Actually the Hamiltonian vector field
generated by
{\mathcal{I}}_{i}(u)

is
the generator
\partial_{x_{i}}

of the translations

(1.13)

X_{{\mathcal{I}}_{i}}(u)=J\nabla^{L^{2}}\mathcal{I}_{i}(u)=\partial_{x_{i}}u\,,\quad i=1,2\,.

Traveling waves.

We seek for

\Gamma
-periodic traveling solutions

u(x-ct)
,

u:{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}\to{\mathbb{R}}
,
of the water waves equations (
1.5
),
for some speed vector
c\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
Thus

-c\cdot\partial_{x}u=X_{\mathcal{H}}(u)

and, recalling (
1.13
),
we are reduced to
solve
the equation

(1.14)

\mathcal{F}(c,u)=0\qquad\text{where}\qquad{\mathcal{F}}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\times X\to Y

is the nonlinear map

(1.15)

\mathcal{F}(c,u):=X_{{\mathcal{H}}+c\cdot{\mathcal{I}}}(u)=c\cdot\partial_{x}u+X_{{\mathcal{H}}}(u)=J\nabla^{L^{2}}({\mathcal{H}}+c\cdot{\mathcal{I}})(u)\,.

The domain
X
is the dense subset
of
L^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma})\times L^{2}_{0}({\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma})
defined in (
1.18
) below, and

Y\subset L^{2}_{0}({\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma})\times L^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma})
is the space in (
1.20
).
Here
L^{2}_{0}({\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma})
is the subspace of
L^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma})
consisting of zero average functions.
Since
J
is invertible, the equation (
1.14
) is equivalent
to search
critical points,
i.e. equilibria, of
the Hamiltonian

(1.16)

\Psi(c,\cdot):X\to{\mathbb{R}}\,,\quad\Psi(c,u):=\big({\mathcal{H}}+c\cdot{\mathcal{I}}\big)(u)\,,

for some value of the
speed
c\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.

By the group symmetries (
1.10
),
(
1.11
),
if
u
is a Stokes solution of
(
1.14
) then each translated

\tau_{\theta}u
and each reflected

\rho u
are solutions as well.
Two Stokes waves
are
geometrically distinct
if they are not obtained
by translations and reflections of the other one, namely if they are not in the same
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-orbit.

Functional setting.

Let
{\bf A}:=({\bf v}_{1}\,|\,{\bf v}_{2})
be the

2\times 2
invertible matrix of
the lattice
\Gamma
in (
1.3
), so that
\Gamma=2\pi{\bf A}{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
.
The
dual lattice
associated to
\Gamma
is the
2
-dimensional lattice

(1.17)

\Gamma^{\prime}:={\bf A}^{-\top}{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}=\Big\{k_{1}{\bf v}_{1}^{\prime}+k_{2}{\bf v}_{2}^{\prime}\,,\ k_{1},k_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}\Big\}

where
{\bf v}_{1}^{\prime}:={\bf A}^{-\top}{\bf e}_{1}
and

{\bf v}_{2}^{\prime}:={\bf A}^{-\top}{\bf e}_{2}

and

{\bf e}_{1},{\bf e}_{2}
denote the canonical
basis of
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.

Any
\Gamma
-periodic function
f:{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}\to{\mathbb{C}}
can be expanded in Fourier series
f(x)=\sum_{j\in\Gamma^{\prime}}f_{j}\,e^{{\rm i}j\cdot x}
. We define for any
\sigma>0
and

s\in{\mathbb{R}}

the Hilbert space of
\Gamma
-periodic
real analytic functions

(1.18)

X:=H^{\sigma,s}\times H^{\sigma,s}_{0}:=H^{\sigma,s}({\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma})\times H^{\sigma,s}_{0}({\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma})

where

(1.19)

H^{\sigma,s}:=H^{\sigma,s}({\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}):=\Big\{f\in L^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma},{\mathbb{R}})\ \big|\ \|f\|_{\sigma,s}^{2}:=\sum_{j\in\Gamma^{\prime}}|f_{j}|^{2}\,\langle j\rangle^{2s}e^{2\sigma|j|_{1}}<+\infty\Big\}

with
\langle j\rangle:=\max(1,|j|_{1})
,
|j|_{1}:=|j_{1}|+|j_{2}|
, and

H^{\sigma,s}_{0}:=H^{\sigma,s}_{0}({\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma})
is the subspace of zero average functions.
For any
s>1

each space
H^{\sigma,s}({\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma})

is an algebra with respect to the product of functions.
We assume that

s\geq 7/2
with
s+\tfrac{1}{2}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, to directly apply the analyticity result

[
11
]
[Theorem 1.2]
for the Dirichlet-Neumann operator.
This is actually
not restrictive since
\sigma>0
is arbitrary.

The target space in (
1.15
) is

(1.20)

Y:=H^{\sigma,s-1}_{0}\times H^{\sigma,s-2}:=H^{\sigma,s-1}_{0}({\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma})\times H^{\sigma,s-2}({\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma})\,.

Note that
{\mathcal{F}}(c,0)=0
for any
c\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
We are going to prove
new existence and multiplicity bifurcation results of
3d
-Stokes waves according to the following definition.

Definition

(
2d
and
3d
-Stokes waves)

A Stokes wave solution
u(x)

of (
1.14
) is
2d
if
it is constant along one direction, namely

u(x)=u\big((x\cdot j)j\big)

for some

j\in\Gamma^{\prime}
.
In this case
we say that
u(x)

is
2d
-along the direction
j
.
Otherwise
we say that

u(x)
is

truly
3
-dimensional
.

Note that for a
2d
-Stokes wave
u(x)

it results

(1.21)

u(x-ct)=u(x-c^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}\hat{\jmath}\,t)\qquad\text{where}\qquad c^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}:=c\cdot\hat{\jmath}\,,\ \ \hat{\jmath}:=j/|j|\,,

and therefore its “observed velocity”
reduces to only the parallel component

c^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}\hat{\jmath}
.

1.2
Main results

As we show at
the beginning of Part
II
,
all the possible speeds of bifurcation, namely the vectors
c_{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
such that
{\cal L}_{c_{*}}:={\rm d}_{u}{\mathcal{F}}(c_{*},0)
is not invertible,
form the web

(1.22)

\mathsf{C}:=\bigcup_{j\in\Gamma^{\prime}\setminus\{0\}}{\mathsf{R}}_{j}\qquad\text{where}\qquad{\mathsf{R}}_{j}:=\big\{c\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\ |\ \omega(j)=c\cdot j\big\}

and

(1.23)

\omega(\xi):=\omega(\xi;g,\kappa,{\mathtt{h}}):=\sqrt{(g+\kappa|\xi|^{2})|\xi|\tanh({\mathtt{h}}|\xi|)}\,,\qquad\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\,,

is the
dispersion relation

(if
{\mathtt{h}}=+\infty
then

\tanh({\mathtt{h}}|\xi|)
is replaced by
1
).
Since
\omega(j)>0
, each straight line
{\mathsf{R}}_{j}
in (
1.22
) lies in the half plane
\{c\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\ |\ c\cdot j>0\}

at a distance
\omega(j)|j|^{-1}
from the origin.

For any speed

c_{*}\in\mathsf{C}

we denote

(1.24)

{\mathcal{V}}:=\big\{j\in\Gamma^{\prime}\setminus\{0\}\ |\ \omega(j)=c_{*}\cdot j\big\}

the set of
resonant wave vectors
.
The corresponding
\ker{\cal L}_{c_{*}}

is the span of the resonant plane waves

(1.25)

v=\sum_{j^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{V}}}\alpha_{j}\begin{pmatrix}M_{j}\cos(j\cdot x)\\
M_{j}^{-1}\sin(j\cdot x)\end{pmatrix}+\beta_{j}\begin{pmatrix}-M_{j}\sin(j\cdot x)\\
M_{j}^{-1}\cos(j\cdot x)\end{pmatrix}\ ,\quad\alpha_{j},\beta_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}\,,

where
M_{j}
are constants defined in (
4.2
).
Due to capillarity,

\ker{\cal L}_{c_{*}}

is always finite dimensional, cf.
(
4.10
).
A wave
v
in (
1.25
) is genuinely
3d
if there are at least two
non parallel resonant wave vectors
j\not\parallel j^{\prime}
with non-zero
amplitudes
(\alpha_{j},\beta_{j}),(\alpha_{j^{\prime}},\beta_{j^{\prime}})\neq 0
; the

j,j^{\prime}
are called
“active frequencies”.
The number of collinear vectors in
{\mathcal{V}}
is at most
2
,
as well known for

2d
-fluids, cf. Lemma
4.2
.
If
\dim\ker({\mathcal{L}}_{c_{*}})=2
the problem reduces to the classical bifurcation of
2d
Stokes waves,
usually
tackled by the
Crandall-Rabinowitz bifurcation theorem, see e.g.
[
55
,
39
]
.
When
\dim\ker({\mathcal{L}}_{c_{*}})=4
and the two vectors in
{\mathcal{V}}
are parallel,
this is the resonant bifurcation problem of
2d
-Stokes waves discussed e.g. in
[
34
,
35
,
47
,
4
]
.
If the
2

wave vectors in
{\mathcal{V}}

are not-parallel,
bifurcation of
smooth families of
3d
Stokes
waves has been
proved in
[
48
,
27
,
3
,
45
]
;
we discuss the
non-resonant case
in (
4.49
)-(
4.52
).

In the resonant scenario when
\dim\ker({\mathcal{L}}_{c_{*}})\geq 6
the situation is much more complex and
the only rigorous known results are due to Craig-Nicholls
[
15
,
16
]
and Groves-Haragus
[
26
]
[thm 5].
In particular
[
15
]
proves, using variational methods, that any
c_{*}
is a
bifurcation speed,
constructing
3d
-Stokes waves,
if the momentum

{\mathcal{I}}(u)=a
is
not
parallel to any resonant wave vector

j\in{\mathcal{V}}
(and sufficiently small).

•

Question:

Are there
3d
-Stokes waves if
a
is parallel
to some resonant wave vector

j_{0}\in{\mathcal{V}}
?

Figure 1:
The closed convex cone
\mathcal{C}
in (
1.26
) associated to three resonant wave vectors
j_{1}
,
j_{2}
and
j_{3}
. According to Theorem
1.3

bifurcation of Stokes waves
u

with momentum
{\mathcal{I}}(u)=a
occurs if and only if
a\in{\cal C}
, while
3d
-Stokes waves emerge when
a\in\interior({\cal C})

(if
a\in\partial{\cal C}
then
any
u
is
2d
).
The scenario
a
is not collinear with any resonant wave vector was addressed in
[
15
]
, see the improved Theorem
1.2
. The case
a
is collinear with exactly
one resonant wave vector is the subject of
Theorem
1.1
.

This is a subtle question because, in this case,

2d
Stokes waves with momentum
a

always exist.
The first main result of this paper is to actually prove bifurcation of multiple
other truly
3d
-Stokes waves
u
having the same momentum

{\mathcal{I}}(u)=a
,
collinear
with some

j_{0}\in{\mathcal{V}}
.

To state precisely
the result we introduce
the closed cone

(1.26)

{\mathcal{C}}:=\Big\{\sum_{j\in{\mathcal{V}}}\tau_{j}\,j\ \big|\ \tau_{j}\leq 0\Big\}

generated by the resonant wave vectors
{\mathcal{V}}
, see Figure
1
.
Note that the cone
{\mathcal{C}}
is convex
and proper (its angle is
<\pi
),
because
c_{*}\cdot j=\omega(j)>0
for any
j\in{\mathcal{V}}
, and

(1.27)

{\mathcal{C}}\setminus\{0\}\subset\big\{a\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\ |\ a\cdot c_{*}<0\big\}\,.

Theorem 1.1
.

(Multiplicity of
3d
-Stokes waves: collinear case)

Assume that the number of resonant wave vectors is
\#{\mathcal{V}}\geq 3
(resonant case).
Then there is
\varepsilon>0
such that

(1.28)

\forall\,a\in\text{int}({\mathcal{C}})\,,\ |a|\leq\varepsilon\,,\ \text{collinear
to a unique resonant wave vector}\ j_{0}\in{\mathcal{V}}\,,

there exist,
in addition to a unique

S^{1}
-orbit of

{2d}
Stokes wave
u_{*}

along the direction
j_{0}
with momentum
{\mathcal{I}}(u_{*})=a
,
at least

N:=\#{\mathcal{V}}-{2}

geometrically distinct
truly
3d
analytic Stokes waves

u_{1},\ldots,u_{N}\in X\,,\quad\text{with
momentum}\quad{\mathcal{I}}(u_{i})=a\,,

having size
\|u_{i}\|_{X}={\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{|a|})

and
speeds

c_{i}(a)=c_{*}+{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{|a|})
.

Note that
N\geq 1
since

\#{\mathcal{V}}\geq 3
.
Theorem
1.1
is proved in Section
8
.
Let us make some comments:

(i)

(
2d
-Stokes wave)
The
2d
-Stokes wave

u_{*}:=u_{*}(a)
with momentum
{\mathcal{I}}(u_{*})=a

(whose existence follows by Crandall-Rabinowitz bifurcation theorem)
has the form

(1.29)

u_{*}=\varepsilon\begin{pmatrix}M_{j_{0}}\cos(j_{0}\cdot x+\vartheta)\\
M_{j_{0}}^{-1}\sin(j_{0}\cdot x+\vartheta)\end{pmatrix}+O(\varepsilon^{2})\,,\ \vartheta\in S^{1}\,,\ M_{j}:=(g+\kappa|j|^{2})^{-\frac{1}{4}}(|j|\tanh({\mathtt{h}}|j|))^{\frac{1}{4}}\,,

with amplitude

\varepsilon=\sqrt{\frac{2|a|}{|j_{0}|}}(1+{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{|a|}))

and speed
c_{*}^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}_{0}}}(a)=c_{*}\cdot{\hat{\jmath}_{0}}+{\mathcal{O}}(|a|)
.

(ii)

(
3d
-Stokes waves shape)

The
3d
-Stokes
waves
u_{i}:=u_{i}(a)
,
i=1,\ldots,\#{\mathcal{V}}-{2}
, are

({\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
-orbits of the form

u_{i}=v_{i}+{\mathcal{O}}(\|v_{i}\|_{X}^{2})\qquad\text{where each}\qquad v_{i}:=v_{i}(a)\in\ker({\mathcal{L}}_{c_{*}})\setminus\{0\}\,,\ \text{cf.}\ \eqref{vkern}\,,

is a
3d
wave
obtained by variational/topological arguments,
and the higher order term depends analytically on
v_{i}
.
A central difficulty
is proving that each

v_{i}
is genuinely
3
-dimensional, i.e. it contains at least two non parallel “active frequencies”, unlike the first order term of
u_{*}

in (
1.29
).
The dependence of the
v_{i}
, thus
u_{i}
,
with respect to their momentum
a
, may vary rather irregularly.

(iii)

(
3d
-Stokes waves speeds)
The velocity
c_{i}(a)
of each

3d
traveling Stokes wave
u_{i}

has
component

c^{\bot_{\hat{\jmath}_{0}}}_{i}(a):=c_{i}(a)\cdot\hat{\jmath}_{0}^{\bot}=c_{*}\cdot\hat{\jmath}_{0}^{\bot}+{\mathcal{O}}(|a|)\,,\quad\text{in the direction orthogonal to}\ j_{0}\,.

If
c_{*}\cdot\hat{\jmath}_{0}^{\bot}\neq 0
,
the motion of the

3d
-Stokes wave
u_{i}(x-c_{i}(a)t)
looks rather different from the

2d
-Stokes wave
u_{*}(x-c_{*}^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}_{0}}}(a)\hat{\jmath}_{0}\,t)
whose
“observed velocity” is

c_{*}^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}_{0}}}(a)\hat{\jmath}_{0}
, accordingly to comment
(
1.21
).

(iv)

(Necessity of assumption
a\in\text{int}({\mathcal{C}})
)

As shown in Theorem
1.3
below, the assumption
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
is a necessary condition for the existence of Stokes waves with momentum
a
,
cf. (
1.30
); while the assumption
a\in\interior({\mathcal{C}})
is necessary for the existence of
truly
3d

Stokes waves having momentum
a
since, by
(\mathfrak{B})
, if
a\in\partial{\mathcal{C}}
any
Stokes wave with momentum
a
is
2
-dimensional.

(v)

(Collinearity assumption)

The assumption that

a
is collinear to a
unique
resonant wave vector
j_{0}\in{\mathcal{V}}
forces the
existence
of
3d
-Stokes waves by purely topological arguments, as we explain in Section
1.3
.
If there are
two
distinct

j_{0},j_{1}\in{\mathcal{V}}
parallel to
a
then the
existence of other
3d
-Stokes waves seems to depend on the nonlinearity and specific properties of the resonant wave vectors
{\mathcal{V}}
.

In the noncollinear case we improve the result of
[
15
]
as follows.

Theorem 1.2
.

(Non-collinear case)

For any
a\in\text{int}({\mathcal{C}})\cap\overline{B_{\varepsilon}}
non-collinear to any
j\in{\mathcal{V}}
there exist at least
\#{\mathcal{V}}-{1}

geometrically distinct
truly
3d
analytic Stokes waves
(u_{i})_{i=1,\ldots,\#{\mathcal{V}}-{1}}
in
X
with momentum
{\mathcal{I}}(u_{i})=a

having size
\|u_{i}\|_{X}={\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{|a|})

and
speeds

c_{i}(a)=c_{*}+{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{|a|})
.

Theorem
1.2
requires the uniform bound on
|a|\leq\varepsilon
while
[
15
]
requires to take
a
smaller and smaller, as
a
aligns to some resonant wave vector. In addition
Theorem
1.2

proves the existence of
\#{\mathcal{V}}-1

({\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
-critical orbits,
not just
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}
-orbits,
improving by a factor
2
the number of geometrically distinct Stokes waves of
[
15
]
.

We complete the bifurcation picture
of
3d
-Stokes waves of
Theorems
1.1
and

1.2

by the
following
result valid for
any
small amplitude
Stokes wave
u

with speed
c

near
c_{*}
.

Theorem 1.3
.

There exists
\delta>0

such that if
u\in X
is an analytic
Stokes wave solution
of
{\mathcal{F}}(c,u)=0

with speed

c\in B_{\delta}(c_{*})
and

\|u\|_{X}<\delta
,
then its
momentum

(1.30)

{\mathcal{I}}(u)=a\in{\mathcal{C}}

belongs
to the convex cone
{\mathcal{C}}
defined in (
1.27
), and

(\mathfrak{B})

(Boundary
\partial{\mathcal{C}}
)

If
a\in\partial{\mathcal{C}}
then the Stokes wave
u
is
2
-dimensional;
if
a=0
then
u=0
.

(\mathfrak{I})

(Interior
{\mathcal{C}}
)

If
a\in\interior({\mathcal{C}})

is not collinear to any resonant wave vector
j\in{\mathcal{V}}
, then the Stokes wave
u(x)
is
3
-dimensional.

Before describing the main ideas of proof we mention that in the last years also the existence of quasi-periodic traveling Stokes waves –which are the nonlinear superposition of Stokes waves moving with rationally independent speeds–
has been proved by means of KAM methods: in
[
9
,
22
,
10
]
for
both
2d
gravity-capillary and pure gravity waves.
Existence of
3d
-quasi-periodic
pure gravity Stokes waves has been
recently achieved in
[
23
]
. The gravity-capillary case is still completely open.

1.3
Ideas of the proof

We first perform in Section
4
a variational Lyapunov-Schmidt decomposition for (
1.14
) along the Kernel

V:=\ker({\mathcal{L}}_{c_{*}})
and its symplectic orthogonal
W:=V^{\bot_{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}}
. The Kernel (
1.25
) is the symplectic subspace

V:=\ker({\mathcal{L}}_{c_{*}})=\bigoplus_{j\in{\mathcal{V}}}V_{j}\qquad\text{where}\qquad V_{j}:={\rm span}\{v_{j}^{(1)},v_{j}^{(2)}\}

are the
2
-dimensional real subspaces in (
4.3
) with basis

v_{j}^{(1)},v_{j}^{(2)}
in (
4.1
),
and
{\mathcal{V}}
are the resonant wave vectors
in (
1.24
).
Decomposing the space

X=V\oplus(W\cap X)

in (
1.18
),
we first solve in Lemma
4.3
the range equation

\Pi_{W}{\cal F}(c,v+w)=0
finding

w(c,v)

for any
|c-c_{*}|<r
and any

v\in B_{r}^{V}
for some
r>0
. In this way problem (
1.14
) is reduced to solve the bifurcation equation

\Pi_{V}{\cal F}(c,v+w(c,v))=0

which amounts to look for critical points of the
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-invariant functional

\Phi(c,\cdot):B_{r}^{V}\to{\mathbb{R}}\,,\quad\Phi(c,v):={\mathcal{H}}(c,v+w(c,v))\,.

In particular
critical points of each restricted functional

\Phi(c,\cdot):V_{\hat{\jmath}}:=\oplus_{j^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{V}},j^{\prime}\parallel j}V_{j^{\prime}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
are critical points on the whole
V

and give rise to

2d
-Stokes waves along
the direction
j
.

Construction of
c(v)
.

In Section
5

we construct
the speed
c(v)
solving
the system

(1.31)

{\rm d}_{v}\Phi(c,v)[\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}(v)]=\begin{pmatrix}{\rm d}_{v}\Phi(c,v)[\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}_{1}(v)]\\
{\rm d}_{v}\Phi(c,v)[\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}_{2}(v)]\end{pmatrix}=0\,,\quad\forall v\in B_{r^{\prime}}^{V}\,,

for some
0<r^{\prime}<r
.
The difficulty in solving (
1.31
)
is that it becomes singular as
v
approaches the
hyperplanes

{\bf V}^{2d}=\cup_{j\in{\mathcal{V}}}V_{j}

of
2d
-waves. Nevertheless, in Proposition

5.1
, exploiting the

{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}
- symmetry of
\Phi(c,v)
, we are able to define
c(v)

in a full neighborhood of
v=0
,
unlike
[
15
]
which determines
c(v)
only if
v\notin{\bf V}^{2d}

and, if
v\to{\bf V}^{2d}
, requires to take
v\to 0
.
The speed
c(v)

is real analytic
in
B_{r^{\prime}}\setminus{\bf V}^{2d}
,
but
may be not continuous at
{\bf V}^{2d}
, as simple examples show.

Variational principle.

Stokes waves
are critical points of the Hamiltonian

H(v):={\mathcal{H}}(v,w(c(v),v))

restricted to level sets

I^{-1}(a)
near
v=0

of the reduced momentum

(1.32)

I(v):={\mathcal{I}}(v,w(c(v),v))={\mathcal{I}}(v)+{\mathcal{O}}(\|v\|^{3})\,.

Let us understand the topology of the level sets
I^{-1}(a)

as
a\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
varies.

Straightening of the momentum.

As first step we
“straighten”

I

into the quadratic momentum
{\mathcal{I}}
.
Theorem
6.2
actually proves the existence
of an equivariant homeomorphism
\zeta

such that

(1.33)

(I\circ\zeta)(v)={\mathcal{I}}(v)\,,\quad\zeta(0)=0\,,\quad\forall v\in B_{r^{\prime\prime}}^{V}\,,

for some
0<r^{\prime\prime}<r^{\prime}
.
This
result is also the key of the non-existence results of
Theorem
1.3
.
The main difficulty to prove such vector valued
Morse type result
is when
v
approaches the
hyperplanes
{\bf V}^{2d}
.
We employ a Moser trick argument and fully exploit the
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}
-symmetries.

Topology of
\mathbb{M}_{a}
.

In view of (
1.33
),
we are reduced
to understand the topology of
the level sets
{\mathcal{I}}^{-1}(a)

which is not empty iff

a\in{\mathcal{C}}
, since

{\mathcal{I}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})={\mathcal{C}}
by (
4.8
).
The
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}

space

\mathbb{M}_{a}:=\big\{v\in U_{2}:=\zeta(B_{r^{\prime\prime}}^{V})\ |\ I(v)=a\big\}

is compact but it may not be
a manifold, which is the case
if
a

is parallel to a unique resonant wave vector
j_{0}\in{\mathcal{V}}
.
In this case

\mathbb{M}_{a}
is the disjoint union

\mathbb{M}_{a}=\mathbb{M}_{a}^{3d}\sqcup\mathbb{M}_{a}^{2d}

where

\mathbb{M}_{a}^{3d}:=\mathbb{M}_{a}\setminus{\bf V}^{2d}

is a manifold,

V=\underbrace{T_{v}\mathbb{M}_{a}^{{3d}}}_{=\ker{\rm d}_{v}I(v)}\oplus\,\text{span}(\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}_{1}(v),\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}_{2}(v))\,,\quad\forall v\in\mathbb{M}_{a}^{3d}\,,

and

\mathbb{M}_{a}^{2d}=\mathbb{M}_{a}\cap V_{j_{0}}=\{v\in V_{j_{0}}\ |\ \|v\|=\sqrt{2}|a|\}

consists of the
2d
Stokes orbit with momentum
a
. In Theorem
7.3

we also prove that
\mathbb{M}_{a}
is equivariantly homeomorphic to the join topological space

(1.34)

\mathbb{M}_{a}\cong(\underbrace{S^{2d_{-}-1}\times S^{2d_{+}-1}}_{\rm product\,of\,spheres})\star S^{1}\quad\text{where}\quad d_{\pm}\geq 1

are the number of resonant wave vectors of
{\mathcal{V}}

at the right/left of
j_{0}
respectively.

Existence and Multiplicity.

On
\mathbb{M}_{a}
the function
H
attains maximum and minimum, and since the
2d
-Stokes wave orbit

\mathbb{M}_{a}^{2d}\subset H^{-1}(\ell_{*})
is contained in a level set of

H
, there exists at least
one critical point
\bar{v}
of
H
in
\mathbb{M}_{a}^{3d}
.
Thus

\bar{u}=\bar{v}+w(c(\bar{v}),\bar{v})
is a
3d
-Stokes wave
solution with momentum
{\mathcal{I}}(\bar{u})=a
.
Multiplicity of
({\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
-orbits of
3d
-Stokes waves follows by the
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}
-invariance of
H
,
implementing the
equivariant Conley-Morse theory
of Section
3
, which is developed in a
G
-metric space.
We first construct in Proposition

8.4

a gradient like flow for
H
whose
stationary points in
\mathbb{M}_{a}^{{3d}}

are critical points of

\Phi(c(v),\cdot)
and thus give rise to Stokes waves.
Then computations in equivariant cohomology for the space
(S^{2d_{-}-1}\times S^{2d_{+}-1})\star S^{1}

enable to prove in Theorem
3.14

the existence
of at
least

\mathrm{CL}_{{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}}(S^{2d_{-}-1}\times S^{2d_{+}-1})+1\geq d_{-}+d+-2=\#{\mathcal{V}}-3

critical values
of
H

different
from the critical level

\ell_{*}=H(\mathbb{M}_{a}^{2d})
, and thus

(\mathbb{T}^{2}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
-orbits
(the definition of equivariant cuplenght is in (
3.24
)).
The proof of Theorem

3.14
faces a serious difficulty: unlike the stabilizers of the action at any point in

\mathbb{M}_{a}^{3d}
are finite, the stabilizers at any point of the
2d
-Stokes wave
S^{1}

are infinite.
This requires a precise
understanding of the annihilator of the graded
module
H^{*}_{{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}}\big((S^{2d_{-}-1}\times S^{2d_{+}-1})\star S^{1}\big)

as addressed in
Lemma
2.25
.
Part
I

contains a rigorous and self-contained exposition of these ideas and techniques.

Acknowledgments.

We thank Thomas Goodwillie for his help with the
computations of Proposition
2.23
, and Bernard Deconinck, Alberto Maspero
and Eric Wahlén for useful comments.

Part I
Equivariant critical point theory

The main result of this part is Theorem
3.14
which provides a lower bound for the number of critical orbits for a continuous Lyapunov function defined on a
singular
topological space
(as (
1.34
))
invariant under the action of a
d
-torus.
After recalling basic definitions concerning graded modules over a graded ring,
we report
in Section
2
the notion of equivariant cohomology, we investigate some properties of its annihilator, and compute it for some spaces
needed later:
products of spheres, and joint products with spheres. In Section
3
we provide a self-contained account of equivariant Morse-Conley theory for continuous Lyapunov functions on compact metric spaces in a form needed to prove Theorem
3.14
. These topics are present in literature, e.g.,
[
41
,
40
,
2
,
13
,
8
,
49
]
in ordinary or equivariant form. The arguments involving the annihilator of the local cohomology of invariant sets build on the works of Fadell-Rabinowitz

[
21
]
, Fadell-Husseini
[
20
]
, Bartsch

[
6
]
, and Bartsch-Clapp
[
7
]
.

Modules and algebras.

We consider a ring
R
with unit
1\in R
, without non-trivial zero divisors (i.e.
r_{1}r_{2}\neq 0
for all
r_{1},r_{2}\in R\setminus\{0\}
), and that is commutative up to sign (i.e. for each
r_{1},r_{2}\in R
, we have
r_{1}r_{2}=r_{2}r_{1}
or
r_{1}r_{2}=-r_{2}r_{1}
, where the sign depends on the elements
r_{1},r_{2}
). We denote the multiplication in the ring simply by juxtaposition. An
R
-module

M
is an abelian group equipped with a scalar multiplication

R\times M\to M
,
(r,m)\mapsto r\cdot m
,
such that, for any
x,y\in M
and any

r,s\in R
, we have

(r+s)\cdot x=r\cdot x+s\cdot x\,,\quad r\cdot(x+y)=r\cdot x+r\cdot y\,,\quad r\cdot(s\cdot x)=(rs)\cdot x\,,\quad 1\cdot x=x\,.

An
R
-module
M
is finitely generated, if there exist finitely many
x_{1},...,x_{n}\in M
such that any element of
M
can be written as a linear combination
r_{1}\cdot x_{1}+...+r_{n}\cdot x_{n}
for some
r_{1},...,r_{n}\in R
.
The
torsion submodule
of
M

is defined as

\displaystyle M_{\mathrm{tor}}:=\big\{x\in M\ \big|\ r\cdot x=0\mbox{ for some }r\in R\setminus\{0\}\big\}.

When
M=M_{\mathrm{tor}}
, we say that the
R
-module is purely torsion, whereas when
M_{\mathrm{tor}}=0
we say that it is torsion-free. The quotient abelian group

(1.35)

M_{\mathrm{free}}:=M/M_{\mathrm{tor}}

inherits an
R
-module structure, and it is called the
torsion-free quotient
module, as
(M_{\mathrm{free}})_{\mathrm{tor}}=0
.
An ideal
I\subseteq R
is an additive subgroup of the ring such that
IR\subseteq I
(since the ring
R
is commutative up to sign, we have
IR=RI
).
The
annihilator
of a subset
S\subseteq M
is the ideal

(1.36)

S_{\mathrm{ann}}:=\big\{r\in R\ |\ r\cdot S=0\big\}\,.

Note that a purely torsion module
M=M_{\mathrm{tor}}
may still have trivial annihilator
M_{\mathrm{ann}}=0
. Nevertheless, if
M
is finitely generated, then
M=M_{\mathrm{tor}}
if and only if
M_{\mathrm{ann}}\neq 0
. If
M
and
N
are
R
-modules, a
homomorphism

\phi:M\to N
is a
R
-linear map, meaning that

\phi(r\cdot x+y)=r\cdot\phi(x)+\phi(y)
,
for all
r\in R
,

x,y\in M
.
We denote by
\mathrm{im}(\phi):=\phi(M)
its image and by
\ker(\phi):=\{x\in M\ |\ \phi(x)=0\}
its kernel.
If
P
is another
R
-module, a sequence of two homomorphisms

M\toup^{\phi}N\toup^{\psi}P

is
exact
when
\mathrm{im}(\phi)=\ker(\psi)
.
The following important elementary properties hold.

Proposition 1.4
.

(i)
Each
R
-module homomorphism
\phi:M\to N
satisfies
\phi(M_{\mathrm{tor}})\subseteq N_{\mathrm{tor}}
.

(
ii
) For each exact sequence of
R
-modules

\displaystyle M\toup^{\phi}N\toup^{\psi}P
,
we have
M_{\mathrm{ann}}P_{\mathrm{ann}}\subseteq N_{\mathrm{ann}}
.

(
iii
)
For each exact sequence of
R
-modules

\displaystyle M\toup^{\phi}N\toup^{\psi}P
,
starting at a purely torsion
R
-module
M=M_{\mathrm{tor}}

(
equivalently,
M_{\mathrm{free}}=0
)
, the homomorphism
\psi
induces an injective homomorphism

\psi:N_{\mathrm{free}}\hookrightarrow P_{\mathrm{free}}
.

Proof.

(i)
For each
x\in M_{\mathrm{tor}}
there exists
r\in R\setminus\{0\}
such that
r\cdot x=0
, and therefore
r\cdot\phi(x)=\phi(r\cdot x)=0
, which proves that
\phi(x)\in N_{\mathrm{tor}}
.

(
ii
) For each
r\in P_{\mathrm{ann}}
and
x\in N
, we have
\psi(r\cdot x)=r\cdot\psi(x)=0
. Therefore, using the exact sequence, there exists
y\in M
such that
\phi(y)=r\cdot x
. For each
s\in M_{\mathrm{ann}}
, we have
sr\cdot x=s\cdot\phi(y)=\phi(s\cdot y)=0
, and therefore
sr\in N_{\mathrm{ann}}
.

(
iii
) By (
i
) the map

\psi
induces an homomorphism
\psi:N_{\mathrm{free}}\to P_{\mathrm{free}}
between torsion free quotient modules, cf. (
1.35
). If by contradiction the induced homomorphism
\psi:N_{\mathrm{free}}\to P_{\mathrm{free}}
is not injective, there is
x\in N\setminus N_{\mathrm{tor}}
such that
\psi(x)\in P_{\mathrm{tor}}
, and therefore there is
r\in R\setminus\{0\}
such that
0=r\cdot\psi(x)=\psi(r\cdot x)
. By the exact sequence, we infer that
r\cdot x\in\mathrm{im}(\phi)
. However
r\cdot x
is not a torsion element, and so item (
i
) implies that
r\cdot x
cannot lie in the image of
\phi
whose domain is purely torsion.
∎

We also consider a special class of modules. An
R
-algebra

A
is a ring with unit
1\in A
, equipped with a ring homomorphism
\pi_{A}:R\to A
, which is a map such that
\pi_{A}(1)=1
,
\pi_{A}(r_{1}r_{2})=\pi_{A}(r_{1})\pi_{A}(r_{2})
and
\pi_{A}(r_{1}+r_{2})=\pi_{A}(r_{1})+\pi_{A}(r_{2})
for all
r_{1},r_{2}\in R
. Notice that
A
is in particular an
R
-module with the scalar multiplication

\displaystyle r\cdot x:=\pi_{A}(r)x\,,\qquad\forall r\in R\,,\ x\in A\,.

A homomorphism of
R
-algebras
\phi:A\to B
is an
R
-linear ring homomorphism, meaning that
\phi(r\cdot x+y)=r\cdot\phi(x)+\phi(y)
and

\phi(x\cdot y)=\phi(x)\cdot\phi(y)
, such that we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{B}}

Lemma 1.5
.

The annihilator of an
R
-algebra
A
is

A_{\mathrm{ann}}=\ker(\pi_{A})
.
For each
R
-algebra homomorphism
\phi:A\to B
, we have

A_{\mathrm{ann}}\subseteq B_{\mathrm{ann}}
.

Proof.

Clearly
\ker(\pi_{A})\subseteq A_{\mathrm{ann}}
.
Conversely, if
r\in A_{\mathrm{ann}}
, we have

0=r\cdot 1=\pi_{A}(r)1=\pi_{A}(r)
,
thus
\ker(\pi_{A})=A_{\mathrm{ann}}
. Since

\pi_{B}=\phi\circ\pi_{A}
, we infer that

A_{\mathrm{ann}}=\ker(\pi_{A})\subseteq\ker(\pi_{B})=B_{\mathrm{ann}}
. ∎

Gradings.

In algebraic topology, we need rings and modules equipped with gradings. We denote by
{\mathbb{N}}=\{0,1,2,...\}
the natural numbers (including zero). A ring
R^{*}
with unit
1\in R^{*}
and without non-trivial zero divisors is
graded
when it can be written as the direct sum of abelian groups

\displaystyle R^{*}=\bigoplus_{d\in{\mathbb{N}}}R^{d}\qquad\text{such that}\qquad R^{d_{1}}R^{d_{2}}\subseteq R^{d_{1}+d_{2}}\,,\ \forall d_{1},d_{2}\in{\mathbb{N}}\,.

A non-zero element
r\in R^{*}\setminus\{0\}
is called homogeneous when
r\in R^{d}
for some
d:=\deg\left(r\right)
, called the degree of
r
.
In the sequel, whenever we consider a non-zero
r\in R^{*}\setminus\{0\}
we always tacitly assume that it is homogeneous.
Note that the degree of the unit is
\deg\left(1\right)=0
. The ring
R^{*}
is
graded-commutative
when

r_{1}r_{2}=(-1)^{\deg\left(r_{1}\right)\,\deg\left(r_{2}\right)}r_{2}r_{1}
, for any
r_{1},r_{2}\in R\setminus\{0\}
.

An
R^{*}
-module
M^{*}
is graded when it can be written as the direct sum of abelian groups

\displaystyle M^{*}=\bigoplus_{d\in{\mathbb{N}}}M^{d}\qquad\text{such that}\qquad R^{d_{1}}\cdot M^{d_{2}}\subseteq M^{d_{1}+d_{2}}\,,\ \forall d_{1},d_{2}\in{\mathbb{N}}\,.

Homogeneous elements

x\in M^{*}\setminus\{0\}
of
\deg\left(x\right):=d

are defined as for a graded ring. A homomorphism of
R
-graded modules
\phi^{*}:M^{*}\to N^{*}

preserves the degree of homogeneous elements, i.e.
\phi^{*}(M^{d})\subseteq N^{d}
.
An
R^{*}
-algebra
A^{*}
is graded when it is an
R^{*}
-graded ring with unit, equipped with a ring homomorphism
\pi_{A^{*}}:R^{*}\to A^{*}
which preserves the degree of homogeneous elements.

2
Equivariant cohomology

Throughout this article,
G
will denote a compact Lie group.

Classifying spaces.

For any Lie group
G
(and indeed even general topological groups) there exists a contractible space
EG
on which
G
acts freely.
The quotient
BG:=EG/G
is called the
classifying space
of
G
. While
EG
is not unique,
BG
is unique up to homotopy equivalence. We will denote by
H^{*}(\cdot)
the singular cohomology with coefficients in the ring of rational numbers
{\mathbb{Q}}
, and we will simply refer to it as the cohomology.

Example 2.1
.

For
G=S^{1}={\mathbb{R}}/2\pi{\mathbb{Z}}
, we can choose
ES^{1}
to be the unit sphere in
{\mathbb{C}}^{\infty}
, equipped with the free
S^{1}
action
\theta\cdot z=e^{{\rm i}\theta}z
. The classifying space is the infinite dimensional complex projective space
BS^{1}={\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}^{\infty}
. Its cohomology is the ring of rational polynomials in one variable

H^{*}(BS^{1})={\mathbb{Q}}[u]

where
u
is a generator of
H^{2}(BS^{1})\cong{\mathbb{Q}}
.

Example 2.2
.

Given two compact Lie groups
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
and their product
G:=G_{1}\times G_{2}
, we can choose
EG=EG_{1}\times EG_{2}
equipped with the product action
(g_{1},g_{2})\cdot(e_{1},e_{2})=(g_{1}\cdot e_{1},g_{2}\cdot e_{2})
, and obtain the classifying space
BG=BG_{1}\times BG_{2}
. By the Künneth theorem, its cohomology is the tensor product

(2.1)

H^{*}(BG)=H^{*}(BG_{1})\otimes H^{*}(BG_{2})\,.

We infer that the torus

{\mathbb{T}}^{d}:=S^{1}\times...\times S^{1}

(
d
-fold product) has classifying space

B{\mathbb{T}}^{d}={\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}^{\infty}\times...\times{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{P}^{\infty}
,
whose cohomology is the ring of rational polynomials in
d
variables

(2.2)

H^{*}(B{\mathbb{T}}^{d})={\mathbb{Q}}[u_{1},...,u_{d}]\,,

where
u_{1},...,u_{d}
are generators of
H^{2}(B{\mathbb{T}}^{d})\cong{\mathbb{Q}}^{d}
.

Example 2.3
.

If
F
is a finite group, its singular cohomology with
{\mathbb{Q}}
coefficents is

H^{0}(BF)={\mathbb{Q}}

and

H^{d}(BF)=0

for any
d\geq 1
.

Any compact Lie group
G
contains a maximal subgroup isomorphic to a torus
{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
, where maximal means that such a subgroup is not contained in a torus subgroup of larger dimension. The dimension
d
is called the
rank
of
G
. For any compact Lie group
G
the classifying space
BG
has cohomology ring

H^{*}(BG)={\mathbb{Q}}[u_{1},...,u_{d}]

with
\deg\left(u_{i}\right)=d_{i}\in 2{\mathbb{N}}
,
where each
u_{i}\in H^{d_{i}}(BG)
has
even
degree, see e.g.
[
43
, Theorem 6.22]
. In particular,
H^{*}(BG)
is a ring without non-trivial zero divisors
.

Remark 2.4
.

If the compact Lie group
G
is connected, the classifying space

BG
is simply connected.
This follows from the long exact sequence of the homotopy groups associated with the principal
G
-bundle
EG\to BG
, see e.g.
[
30
, Theorem 4.41]
.

Borel quotient.

Let
X
be a
G
-space, meaning a topological space equipped with a continuous
G
action.
If the
G
action is not free, there are no general techniques to compute the cohomology of the quotient
X/G
,
and it is convenient to consider the
Borel quotient

(2.3)

X_{G}:=X\times_{G}EG\,.

Here, with common notation,
G
acts diagonally on the product
X\times EG
, i.e.
g\cdot(x,e):=(g\cdot x,g\cdot e)
, and
X\times_{G}EG:=(X\times EG)/G
. The Borel quotient

X_{G}

is a natural substitute of the ordinary quotient as
X\times EG
is homotopy equivalent to
X
. The advantage is that
G
acts freely on
X\times EG
. The quotient projection
X\times EG\to X_{G}
is a principal
G
-bundle, and the topology of
X_{G}
can be studied via well established techniques in algebraic topology.

Equivariant cohomology.

Let
X
be a
G
-space.
Throughout this paper, all
G
-spaces are assumed to be Hausdorff.
A function
f:X\to Y
is called
G
-
invariant
if
f(g\cdot x)=f(x)
and
G
-
equivariant
if
f(g\cdot x)=g\cdot f(x)
for any
x\in X
.

Let
Y
be a
G
-subspace of
X
. The
G
-equivariant cohomology
of the pair
(X,Y)
is defined as the relative cohomology of their Borel quotients

\displaystyle H^{*}_{G}(X,Y):=H^{*}(X_{G},Y_{G})\,.

Throughout this article
H^{*}(\cdot)
denotes the singular cohomology with coefficients in the ring of rational numbers
{\mathbb{Q}}
.
We denote

H^{*}_{G}(X):=H^{*}_{G}(X,\varnothing)\,.

The equivariant cohomology inherits many of the properties of the ordinary cohomology,
including the compatibility with the
G
action. More specifically, a continuous map between pairs of
G
-spaces
\phi:(X,Y)\to(W,Z)
that is
G
-equivariant, meaning that

\phi(g\cdot x)=g\cdot\phi(x)
,
for any
g\in G
,

x\in X
,
induces a continuous map between the corresponding pairs of Borel quotients, still denoted with the same letter
\phi:(X_{G},Y_{G})\to(W_{G},Z_{G})
,
\phi([x,e])=[\phi(x),e]
, and therefore a ring homomorphism

(2.4)

\displaystyle\phi^{*}:H^{*}_{G}(W,Z)\to H^{*}_{G}(X,Y)\,.

Two
G
-equivariant continuous maps
\phi_{0}:(X,Y)\to(W,Z)
and
\phi_{1}:(X,Y)\to(W,Z)
induce the same homomorphism
\phi_{0}^{*}=\phi_{1}^{*}
in equivariant cohomology provided they are interpolated by a
G
-equivariant homotopy, namely by a family of continuous
G
-equivariant maps
\phi_{t}:(X,Y)\to(W,Z)
depending continuously on
t\in[0,1]
.

There is a crucial extra property that is specific to equivariant cohomology.
While the cup product
\smallsmile
gives a graded ring structure to the ordinary cohomology, the equivariant cohomology
H^{*}_{G}(X)
is actually a
graded
H^{*}(BG)
-algebra
, with the canonical homomorphism

(2.5)

\displaystyle\pi_{X}^{*}:H^{*}(BG)\to H^{*}_{G}(X)

induced by the projection map

(2.6)

\pi_{X}:X_{G}\to BG\,,\quad\pi([x,e]):=[e]\,.

Thus, by Lemma
1.5
, for any
G
-space
X
,

(2.7)

\ker(\pi_{X}^{*})=H^{*}_{G}(X)_{\mathrm{ann}}\,.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
X
be a
G
-space with a fixed point
x=G\cdot x
of the action. Then
H^{*}_{G}(X)_{\mathrm{ann}}=0
.

Proof.

Since
x=G\cdot x
, the map
\pi_{x}:\{x\}_{G}\to BG
is a homeomorphism, and therefore induces an isomorphism in cohomology. The inclusion
i:\{x\}\hookrightarrow X
induces a homomorphism which fits in the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{G}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{G}(\{x\})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}(BG)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{X}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{x}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Since the diagonal isomorphism factorizes as
\pi_{x}^{*}=i^{*}\circ\pi_{X}^{*}
, we infer that
\pi_{X}^{*}
is injective, and by (
2.7
)
we conclude that
H^{*}_{G}(X)_{\mathrm{ann}}=0
.
∎

The relative equivariant cohomology does not have an algebra structure, but nevertheless
H^{*}_{G}(X,Y)
is a
graded
H^{*}(BG)
-module
with scalar product

(2.8)

r\cdot h:=\pi_{X}^{*}(r)\smallsmile h\,,\qquad\forall r\in H^{*}(BG)\,,\ h\in H^{*}_{G}(X,Y)\,.

Note that this is indeed well defined, as the cup product of a cohomology class in
H^{*}_{G}(X)
with a relative cohomology class in
H^{*}_{G}(X,Y)
is still a relative cohomology class in
H^{*}_{G}(X,Y)
. Clearly (
2.4
) is an
H^{*}(BG)
-module homomorphism, and even an
H^{*}(BG)
-algebra homomorphism if
Y=\varnothing
, meaning that it commutes with the scalar product, i.e.

\phi^{*}(r\cdot h)=r\cdot\phi^{*}(h)
.
We will often need the long exact sequences induced by a triple of
G
-spaces.

Proposition 2.6
.

Any triple of
G
-spaces
Z\subseteq Y\subseteq X
induces a long exact sequence of graded
H^{*}(BG)
-module homomorphisms

(2.9)

...\toup^{\delta^{*-1}}H^{*}_{G}(X,Y)\toup H^{*}_{G}(X,Z)\toup H^{*}_{G}(Y,Z)\toup^{\delta^{*}}H^{*+1}_{G}(X,Y)\toup...

where all the arrows are induced by the inclusions,
except
\delta^{*}
.

Proof.

The statement follows from the long exact sequence for the triple of Borel quotients
Z_{G}\subseteq Y_{G}\subseteq X_{G}
, and since the inclusions induce graded
H^{*}(BG)
-module homomorphisms in equivariant cohomology. The connecting homomorphism
\delta^{*}
is a graded
H^{*}(BG)
-module homomorphism as well, as follows by the definition of the scalar product (
2.8
) and since
\delta^{*}
satisfies

\delta^{*}(i^{*}(k)\smallsmile h)=k\smallsmile\delta^{*}(h)
,
for any
k\in H^{*}_{G}(Y)
,

h\in H^{*}_{G}(X,Y)
,
where
i:Y\hookrightarrow X
is the inclusion.
∎

2.1
Orbits of the action

We recall that the compact Lie group
G
acts freely on the contractible space
EG
, and therefore any compact Lie subgroup
H\subseteq G
acts freely on
EG
as well. This implies that we can choose
EH=EG
and classifying space
BH=EG/H
. Notice that
BH
is still a
G
-space, and
BG=BH/G
. Up to homeomorphism, the classifying space
BH
depends only on the conjugacy class of
H
. Indeed, the quotient group
G/H
, endowed with the quotient topology, is a
G
-space whose Borel quotient admits a homeomorphism

(2.10)

\displaystyle(G/H)_{G}\toup^{\cong}BH\,,\qquad[gH,e]\mapsto[g^{-1}e]\,.

If
K=gHg^{-1}
is another subgroup conjugate to
H
, there is a
G
-equivariant homeomorphism

G/H\toup^{\cong}G/K\,,\qquad hH\mapsto hHg^{-1}=hg^{-1}K\,,

and we infer by (
2.10
) that
BH\cong BK
are
G
-homeomorphic.

Let
X
be a
G
-space (which, as everywhere else in this paper, is assumed to be Hausdorff). Each point
x\in X
has
stabilizer

(2.11)

G_{x}:=\big\{g\in G\ |\ g\cdot x=x\big\}\,,

also referred to as
isotropy group
,
which is a compact subgroup of
G
.
By the elementary orbit-stabilizer theorem, the orbit
G\cdot x
is homeomorphic to the quotient
G/G_{x}
via the
G
-equivariant homeomorphism

(2.12)

G\cdot x\toup^{\cong}G/G_{x}\,,\qquad g\cdot x\mapsto gG_{x}\,.

The projection

\pi_{G/G_{x}}

in (
2.5
)
factorizes as

\pi_{G/G_{x}}=q_{x}\circ\nu_{x}
,
where
\nu_{x}:(G/G_{x})_{G}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\toup}}BG_{x}

is the canonical homeomorphism
defined in (
2.10
), and
q_{x}
is the quotient map

q_{x}:BG_{x}\to BG=BG_{x}/G\,.

The homeomorphism

\nu_{x}
together with (
2.12
)
induces a canonical isomorphism

(2.13)

H^{*}(BG_{x})\cong H^{*}_{G}(G\cdot x)\,.

Overall, we have the commutative diagram

(2.14)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}(BG)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{G}(G\cdot x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}(BG_{x})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{G\cdot x}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{x}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

By (
2.7
) we have

(2.15)

H^{*}_{G}(G\cdot x)_{\mathrm{ann}}=\ker(\pi^{*}_{G\cdot x})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{cd:q pi}}}{{=}}\ker(q_{x}^{*})\,.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
T={\mathbb{T}}^{d}
be a torus, and
X
a
T
-space.
If
x\in X
is not a fixed point of the action, then
H^{2}_{T}(T\cdot x)_{\mathrm{ann}}\not=0
.

Proof.

The stabilizer
T_{x}
is a closed abelian Lie-subgroup of
T
. Since
x
is not a fixed point of the whole
T
action,
T_{x}
has rank
m<d
. Since
H^{2}(BT)\cong{\mathbb{Q}}^{d}
and
H^{2}(BT_{x})\cong{\mathbb{Q}}^{m}
, the homomorphism
q_{x}^{*}:H^{2}(BT)\to H^{2}(BT_{x})
has a non-trivial kernel. This, together with (
2.15
), implies
H^{2}_{T}(T\cdot x)_{\mathrm{ann}}\not=0
.
∎

Remark 2.8
.

If
x
has finite stabilizer
G_{x}
then, by
(
2.13
) and
Example
2.3
, we
deduce that

H_{G}^{0}(G\cdot x)\cong{\mathbb{Q}}
and
H_{G}^{d}(G\cdot x)=0
for any degree
d\geq 1
.

2.2
Annihilator of the equivariant cohomology

The following are basic properties of the annihilator of pairs of
G
-spaces.

Proposition 2.9
.

Let
X,Y
be
G
-spaces. The following holds:

(i)

Monotonicity:
If there is a continuous
G
-equivariant map
\phi:X\to Y
, then

H^{*}_{G}(Y)_{\mathrm{ann}}\subseteq H^{*}_{G}(X)_{\mathrm{ann}}
.

(ii)

Union subadditivity:
for each
G
-space
X
and for each pair of
G
-subspaces
Y,Z\subseteq X
whose interiors cover
X
, i.e.
X=\interior(Y)\cup\interior(Z)
, we have

H^{*}_{G}(Y)_{\mathrm{ann}}\smallsmile H^{*}_{G}(Z)_{\mathrm{ann}}\subseteq H^{*}_{G}(X)_{\mathrm{ann}}\,.

(iii)

Pair monotonicity:
For each pair of
G
-spaces
Y\subseteq X
, we have

H^{*}_{G}(X)_{\mathrm{ann}}\subseteq H^{*}_{G}(X,Y)_{\mathrm{ann}}
.

(iv)

Triple subadditivity:
For each triple of
G
-spaces
Z\subseteq Y\subseteq X
, we have

(2.16)

H^{*}_{G}(X,Y)_{\mathrm{ann}}\smallsmile H^{*}_{G}(Y,Z)_{\mathrm{ann}}\subseteq H^{*}_{G}(X,Z)_{\mathrm{ann}}\,.

Proof.

(
i
) By (
2.4
)
a continuous
G
-equivariant map
\phi:X\to Y
induces a
H^{*}(BG)
-algebra homomorphism
\phi^{*}:H^{*}_{G}(Y)\to H^{*}_{G}(X)
that fits into the commutative diagram,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{G}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{G}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}(BG)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{Y}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{X}^{*}}

where
\pi_{X}^{*},\pi_{Y}^{*}

are induced by the canonical projections (
2.6
).
This diagram implies
H^{*}_{G}(Y)_{\mathrm{ann}}=\ker(\pi_{Y}^{*})\subseteq\ker(\pi_{X}^{*})=H^{*}_{G}(X)_{\mathrm{ann}}
.

(
ii
)
Consider the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{G}(X,Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{G}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{G}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}(BG)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{X}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{Y}^{*}}

where the homomorphisms in the exact horizontal line are induced by the inclusions

(Y,\varnothing)\hookrightarrow(X,\varnothing)\hookrightarrow(X,Y)
.
By the exactness, for any
r\in H_{G}^{*}(Y)_{\mathrm{ann}}=\ker(\pi_{Y}^{*})
, the cohomology class
\pi_{X}^{*}(r)
is in the image of the homomorphism
H^{*}_{G}(X,Y)\to H^{*}_{G}(X)
. Analogously, for any
s\in H_{G}^{*}(Z)_{\mathrm{ann}}=\ker(\pi_{Z}^{*})
, the cohomology class
\pi_{X}^{*}(s)
is in the image of the homomorphism
H^{*}_{G}(X,Z)\to H^{*}_{G}(X)
induced by the inclusion.
Since
X=\interior(Y)\cup\interior(Z)
, the pair
\{Y,Z\}
is excisive and
the cup product
\smallsmile:H^{n}_{G}(X,Y)\smallsmile H^{m}_{G}(X,Z)\to H^{n+m}_{G}(X,Y\cup Z)
is well defined.
By the naturality of the cup product we deduce that the cohomology class
\pi_{X}^{*}(r\smallsmile s)=\pi_{X}^{*}(r)\smallsmile\pi_{X}^{*}(s)
is in the image of the homomorphism
H^{*}_{G}(X,Y\cup Z)\to H^{*}_{G}(X)
induced by the inclusion. But
H^{*}_{G}(X,Y\cup Z)=H^{*}_{G}(X,X)=0
, and therefore
r\smallsmile s\in\ker(\pi_{X}^{*})=H_{G}^{*}(X)_{\mathrm{ann}}
.

(
iii
) For each
r\in H^{*}_{G}(X)_{\mathrm{ann}}=\ker(\pi_{X}^{*})
and
h\in H^{*}_{G}(X,Y)
, we have
r\cdot h=\pi_{X}^{*}(r)\smallsmile h=0
.

(
iv
) The inclusion
(
2.16
) follows by Proposition
1.4
-(
ii
) applied to the exact sequence (
2.9
).
∎

We now provide a more technical lemma involving the torsion-free quotient and the annihilator of the equivariant cohomology in the presence of a fixed point of the group action. Hereafter, for any fixed point
x=G\cdot x
of a compact Lie group action, we will tacitly
identify

H^{*}_{G}(\{x\})
with
H^{*}(BG)
through the isomorphism
\pi_{x}^{*}
induced by the canonical homeomorphism
\pi_{x}:\{x\}_{G}\to BG
.

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
X
be a
G
-space containing a fixed point
x=G\cdot x
of the action, and a
G
-invariant subspace
Y\subseteq X
. If
H^{*}_{G}(Y)_{\mathrm{free}}=H_{G}^{*}(X,\{x\})_{\mathrm{free}}=0
and the inclusion
Y\hookrightarrow X
is
G
-equivariantly homotopic to the constant map
Y\to\{x\}
, then the inclusion of pairs
(\{x\},\varnothing)\hookrightarrow(X,Y)
induces an isomorphism

H^{*}_{G}(X,Y)_{\mathrm{free}}\toup^{\cong}H^{*}_{G}(Y)_{\mathrm{ann}}\,.

Proof.

Consider the commutative diagram

(2.17)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{G}^{*-1}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{G}^{*}(X,Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{G}^{*}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{G}^{*}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}(BG)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{*-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{X}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{Y}^{*}}

where the horizontal line is the exact sequence of the pair of
G
-spaces
Y\subseteq X
,
so that
i^{*}
is induced by the inclusion
i:(X,\varnothing)\hookrightarrow(X,Y)
.
The homomorphism
\pi_{X}^{*}
is injective according to Lemma
2.5
. We have

\ker(\pi_{Y}^{*})=H^{*}_{G}(Y)_{\mathrm{ann}}
, cf. (
2.7
), and the exact sequence in (
2.17
) implies

(2.18)

\pi_{X}^{*}(H^{*}_{G}(Y)_{\mathrm{ann}})\subseteq\mathrm{im}(i^{*})\,.

Since, by assumption, the inclusion
j:Y\hookrightarrow X
is
G
-equivariantly homotopic to the constant map

Y\to\{x\}
, we have the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{G}^{*}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{G}^{*}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}(BG)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{Y}^{*}}

where the diagonal homomorphism is induced by the inclusion
l:\{x\}\hookrightarrow X
.
This, together with the exact sequence in (
2.17
), implies that
\mathrm{im}(l^{*}i^{*})\subseteq H^{*}_{G}(Y)_{\mathrm{ann}}
.
Since
l^{*}\pi_{X}^{*}=\mathrm{id}
, we also have the opposite inclusion
H^{*}_{G}(Y)_{\mathrm{ann}}=l^{*}\pi_{X}^{*}(H^{*}_{G}(Y)_{\mathrm{ann}})\subseteq\mathrm{im}(l^{*}i^{*})
by (
2.18
), and therefore

(2.19)

\mathrm{im}(l^{*}i^{*})=H^{*}_{G}(Y)_{\mathrm{ann}}\,.

Using
the first part of the
exact sequence in the horizontal line in (
2.17
)
and the assumption
H_{G}^{*}(Y)_{\mathrm{free}}=0
, Proposition

1.4
-(
iii
) implies that
i^{*}
induces an injective homomorphism

i^{*}:H_{G}^{*}(X,Y)_{\mathrm{free}}\hookrightarrow H_{G}^{*}(X)_{\mathrm{free}}\,.

Similarly, by Proposition

1.4
-(
iii
),
the exact sequence of the pair
\{x\}\subseteq X
,

H_{G}^{*}(X,\{x\})\toup H_{G}^{*}(X)\toup^{l^{*}}H^{*}(BG)\,,

and the assumption

H_{G}^{*}(X,\{x\})_{\mathrm{free}}=0
,
we get that
l^{*}
induces an injective homomorphism

l^{*}:H_{G}^{*}(X)_{\mathrm{free}}\hookrightarrow H^{*}(BG)
.
Thus, the inclusion
i\circ l:(\{x\},\varnothing)\hookrightarrow(X,Y)
induces
an injective homomorphism
H^{*}_{G}(X,Y)_{\mathrm{free}}\hookrightarrow H^{*}(BG)
,
namely an
isomorphism

(i\circ l)^{*}=l^{*}i^{*}:H_{G}^{*}(X,Y)_{\mathrm{free}}\toup^{\cong}H^{*}_{G}(Y)_{\mathrm{ann}}.\qed

2.3
The Euler class and the Gysin sequence

In our applications to the multiplicity of Stokes waves, we need to compute the equivariant cohomology of spheres, products of spheres, and certain join products. The fundamental tool is the Gysin exact sequence for oriented sphere bundles, which we report in
Theorem
2.19
, see Milnor and Stasheff
[
40
]
or Spanier
[
51
]
.
While everywhere else in the paper we employ singular cohomology with coefficients in
{\mathbb{Q}}
, in this section we will sometimes employ singular cohomology with coefficients in
{\mathbb{Z}}
, specifying the employed coefficients ring
R
writing
H^{*}(\,\cdot\,;R)
.

Let
\pi:V\to B
be an oriented real vector bundle of rank
r
, i.e. each fiber
V_{x}:=\pi^{-1}(x)
is
a real oriented vector space of dimension
r
, and the orientations vary coherently with
x
. Any complex vector bundle is also a real oriented vector bundle with a canonical orientation, see e.g.
[
40
, Lemma 14.1]
.
A complex vector bundle of complex rank one is called a complex line bundle. With a common abuse of terminology, we will often refer to its total space
V
as the “vector bundle”, at least when the projection
\pi
on the base space
B
is implicit from the context. We denote by
0_{V}\subseteq V
the zero-section of the bundle, which is the subspace intersecting every fiber
V_{x}
at the origin.
Note that
0_{V}
is homeomorphic to
B
.
The orientation of any fiber
V_{x}
fixes a generator
m_{x}\in H^{r}(V_{x},V_{x}\setminus\{0\};{\mathbb{Z}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}
.
The following is a fundamental result on the cohomology of vector bundles (see e.g.
[
40
, Theorem 9.1]
).

Theorem 2.11

(Thom isomorphism)
.

There is a unique cohomology class
m\in H^{r}(V,V\setminus 0_{V};{\mathbb{Z}})
, called the
Thom class of
V
,
that for any
x\in B
projects to

m_{x}\in H^{r}(V_{x},V_{x}\setminus\{0\};{\mathbb{Z}})

under the homomorphism induced by the inclusion

i_{x}:(V_{x},V_{x}\setminus\{0\})\to(V,V\setminus 0_{V})
.
Furthermore the map

(2.20)

H^{*}(V;{\mathbb{Z}})\ttoup^{\cong}H^{*+r}(V,V\setminus 0_{V};{\mathbb{Z}})\,,\qquad h\mapsto m\smallsmile h\,,

is an isomorphism.

Definition 2.12
.

The
Euler class

e(V)\in H^{r}(B;{\mathbb{Z}})
is the image of the Thom class
m
under the homomorphism induced by the compositions

(B,\varnothing)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle l}}{{\hookrightarrow}}(V,\varnothing)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\hookrightarrow}}(V,V\setminus 0_{V})\,,

where
l
sends any

x\in B

to the corresponding origin

0
in
V_{x}
and
i
is the inclusion.

We list properties of the Euler class which we will employ later on:

(i)

If
\pi:V\to B
is an oriented vector bundle and
\psi:C\to B
is a continuous map between topological spaces, then the Euler class of the
pull-back vector bundle

\psi^{*}V\to C
is
e(\psi^{*}V)=\psi^{*}e(V)
.
Here

\psi^{*}V:=\big\{(c,x)\in C\times V\ \big|\ \psi(c)=\pi(x)\big\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\psi^{*}\pi}}{{\ttoup}}C\,,\ \ (c,x)\mapsto(\psi^{*}\pi)(c,x):=c\,,

so that each fiber
(\psi^{*}V)_{c}
is isomorphic to
V_{\psi(c)}
.

(ii)

The direct sum of two oriented vector bundles
V_{1}\to B
and
V_{2}\to B
has Euler class

e(V_{1}\oplus V_{2})=e(V_{1})\smallsmile e(V_{2})
. Here,
V_{1}\oplus V_{2}\to B
is the vector bundle with fibers
(V_{1}\oplus V_{2})_{x}=(V_{1})_{x}\oplus(V_{2})_{x}
.

(iii)

If an oriented vector bundle
\pi:V\to B
has a nowhere vanishing section (namely a continuous map
s:B\to V\setminus 0_{V}
such that
\pi\circ s=\mathrm{id}
) then
e(V)=0
.

(iv)

If
\overline{V}
is the vector bundle
V
with opposite orientation, then
e(\overline{V})=-e(V)
.

(v)

The tensor product of two complex line bundles
L_{1}\to B
and
L_{2}\to B
has Euler class

e(L_{1}\otimes L_{2})=e(L_{1})+e(L_{2})
.
Here,
L_{1}\otimes L_{2}\to B
is the complex line bundle with fibers
(L_{1}\otimes L_{2})_{x}=(L_{1})_{x}\otimes(L_{2})_{x}
, where the tensor product is over
{\mathbb{C}}
.

If
V
is a finite dimensional
vector space with a linear action of
G
then the Borel quotient
V_{G}=V\times_{G}EG
, with canonical projection

\pi_{V}:V_{G}\to BG
, is a vector bundle (see e.g.
[
54
]
Proposition 4.7) which is orientable since
G
is connected.

Example 2.13
.

Consider the circle
{\mathbb{T}}={\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
acting on
V(k):={\mathbb{C}}
as

\theta\cdot z:=e^{{\rm i}2\pi k\theta}z
for any
\theta\in{\mathbb{T}}
,
z\in V(k)
,
where
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
is an integer.
The Borel quotient

V(k)_{\mathbb{T}}=V(k)\times_{{\mathbb{T}}}E{\mathbb{T}}
with the canonical projection
V(k)_{\mathbb{T}}\to B{\mathbb{T}}
,
[z,f]\mapsto[f]
, is a complex line bundle.

Lemma 2.14
.

There exists a generator
u
of
H^{2}(B{\mathbb{T}};{\mathbb{Z}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}
such that, for each
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, the Euler class of the complex line bundle
V(k)_{\mathbb{T}}\to B{\mathbb{T}}
of Example
2.13
is

e_{k}=k\,u
.

Proof.

By Definition
2.12
,
the Euler class
e(L)

of
L:=V(1)_{\mathbb{T}}
is

e_{1}:=l^{*}i^{*}m

where

m\in H^{2}(L,L\setminus 0_{L};{\mathbb{Z}})
is the Thom class of
L
.
The homomorhism

l^{*}:H^{*}(L;{\mathbb{Z}})\to H^{*}(B{\mathbb{T}};{\mathbb{Z}})
is an isomorphism because
l:B{\mathbb{T}}\hookrightarrow L
is an homotopic equivalence
with homotopy inverse the base projection
\pi_{V(1)}:L\to B{\mathbb{T}}
.
We now prove that also

i^{*}
is an isomorphism
and therefore
e_{1}
is a generator of
H^{2}(B{\mathbb{T}};{\mathbb{Z}})
.
Consider the following portion of the long exact sequence associated with the inclusion
L\setminus 0_{L}\subset L

(2.21)

H^{1}(L\setminus 0_{L};{\mathbb{Z}})\toup H^{2}(L,L\setminus 0_{L};{\mathbb{Z}})\toup^{i^{*}}H^{2}(L;{\mathbb{Z}})\to H^{2}(L\setminus 0_{L};{\mathbb{Z}})\,.

Note that
L\setminus 0_{L}
is homotopy equivalent to
E{\mathbb{T}}
.
Indeed,

L\setminus 0_{L}
is homotopy equivalent to the
sphere bundle

{\mathbb{T}}\times_{\mathbb{T}}E{\mathbb{T}}
of
L
(here
{\mathbb{T}}=\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}\ |\ |z|=1\}
)
and

{\mathbb{T}}\times_{\mathbb{T}}E{\mathbb{T}}\cong E{\mathbb{T}}
.
Explicitly the inclusion
j:{\mathbb{T}}\times_{\mathbb{T}}E{\mathbb{T}}\hookrightarrow L\setminus 0_{L}
has homotopy inverse
\ell:L\setminus 0_{L}\to{\mathbb{T}}\times_{\mathbb{T}}E{\mathbb{T}}:[z,f]\mapsto[\tfrac{z}{|z|},f]
.

For any integer
k\geq 1
, we have an isomorphism of complex line bundles

(2.22)

\underbrace{L\otimes...\otimes L}_{\times k}\ttoup^{\cong}V(k)_{{\mathbb{T}}}\,,\qquad[z_{1},f]\otimes...\otimes[z_{k},f]\mapsto[z_{1}...z_{k},f]\,.

Therefore, by properties (i) and (v), the Euler class of
V(k)_{\mathbb{T}}
is

e_{k}=k\,e(L)=k\,e_{1}
.
Moreover, we have an isomorphism of complex line bundles

\displaystyle\overline{L}\ttoup^{\cong}V(-1)_{{\mathbb{T}}}\,,\qquad[\overline{z},f]\mapsto[z,f]

where
\overline{L}
denotes the complex conjugate vector bundle to
L
. As a real vector bundle,
L
is equal to
\overline{L}
, but is equipped with the opposite complex structure, and thus has the opposite canonical orientation than
L
. Property (iv) implies that the Euler class of
V(-1)_{\mathbb{T}}
is

e_{-1}=e(\overline{L})=-e(L)=-\,e_{1}
, and,
as in (
2.22
) we deduce that
e_{-k}:=e(V(-k)_{\mathbb{T}})=k\,e_{-1}=-ke_{1}
for any
k\geq 1
.
Finally, for
k=0
,
the
{\mathbb{T}}
-action in Example

2.13
is trivial, and
the vector bundle
V(0)_{\mathbb{T}}\cong{\mathbb{C}}\times BG
is trivial. In particular, it admits a nowhere vanishing section, and property (iii) implies that its Euler class is
e_{0}=0
.
∎

Example 2.15
.

More generally, consider a vector
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
, and the
d
-torus
{\mathbb{T}}^{d}={\mathbb{T}}\times...\times{\mathbb{T}}
acting on
V(k):={\mathbb{C}}
as

\theta\cdot z:=e^{{\rm i}2\pi\langle k,\theta\rangle}z
,
for any
\theta\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
,
z\in V(k)
.
The Borel quotient
V(k)_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}
with the canonical projection
V(k)_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\to B{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
,
[z,f]\mapsto[f]
is a complex line bundle.

Lemma 2.16
.

For
k=(k_{1},...,k_{d})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
, the Euler class of the complex line bundle
V(k)\to B{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
of Example
2.15
is

e_{k}=k_{1}\,u_{1}+...+k_{d}\,u_{d}
, where
u_{1},...,u_{d}
is the canonical basis of the cohomology group
H^{2}(B{\mathbb{T}}^{d};{\mathbb{Z}})
, namely

H^{*}(B{\mathbb{T}}^{d};{\mathbb{Z}})={\mathbb{Z}}[u_{1},...,u_{d}]
.

Proof.

Recalling that
B{\mathbb{T}}^{d}=B{\mathbb{T}}\times...\times B{\mathbb{T}}
by Example
2.2
,
we consider the projections

p_{j}:B{\mathbb{T}}^{d}\to B{\mathbb{T}}
,
p_{j}([f_{1},...,f_{d}]):=[f_{j}]
for any
j=1,\ldots,d
,
and
the complex line bundles

L_{k_{j}}:=V(k_{j})_{\mathbb{T}}\to B{\mathbb{T}}
.
We have an isomorphism of complex line bundles

\displaystyle p_{1}^{*}L_{k_{1}}\otimes...\otimes p_{d}^{*}L_{k_{d}}\ttoup^{\cong}V(k)_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\,,\quad([z_{1},f_{1}]\otimes...\otimes[z_{d},f_{d}],[f_{1},\dots,f_{d}])\mapsto[z_{1}...z_{d},f_{1},...,f_{d}]\,.

Therefore, by Lemma
2.14
and properties (i) and (v), the Euler class
e_{k}
of
V(k)_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}
is

\displaystyle e_{k}=p_{1}^{*}e_{k_{1}}+...+p_{d}^{*}e_{k_{d}}=k_{1}\,p_{1}^{*}u+...+k_{d}\,p_{d}^{*}u=k_{1}\,u_{1}+...+k_{d}\,u_{d}

where
u_{j}:=p_{j}^{*}u

are the canonical generators of

H^{2}(B{\mathbb{T}}^{d};{\mathbb{Z}})\cong H^{2}(B{\mathbb{T}};{\mathbb{Z}})\oplus...\oplus H^{2}(B{\mathbb{T}};{\mathbb{Z}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
.
∎

Example 2.17
.

Even more generally, consider integer vectors
k_{1},...,k_{n}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
, which we group in a
d\times n
matrix
k=(k_{1},...,k_{n})
,
and the
d
-torus
{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
acting on
V(k):={\mathbb{C}}^{n}
as

(2.23)

\displaystyle\theta\cdot z:=(e^{{\rm i}2\pi\langle k_{1},\theta\rangle}z_{1},...,e^{{\rm i}2\pi\langle k_{n},\theta\rangle}z_{n})\,,\quad\forall\theta\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}\,,\ z\in V(k)\,.

The Borel quotient
V(k)_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}
with the canonical projection
\pi_{V(k)}:V(k)_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\to B{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
,
[z,f]\mapsto[f]
, is an oriented vector bundle of real rank
2n
.

Lemma 2.18
.

For a matrix
k=(k_{1},...,k_{n})
with entries
k_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
, the Euler class of the oriented vector bundle
V(k)_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\to B{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
is

e_{k}=e_{k_{1}}\smallsmile...\smallsmile e_{k_{n}}
where
each
e_{k_{i}}\in H^{2}(B{\mathbb{T}}^{d};{\mathbb{Z}})
is given by Lemma
2.16
.

Proof.

In terms of bundle operations

V(k)_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}=V(k_{1})_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}\oplus...\oplus V(k_{n})_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}
.
Therefore, by property (ii) and Lemma
2.16
, its Euler class is

e_{k}=e_{k_{1}}\smallsmile...\smallsmile e_{k_{n}}
.
∎

We now introduce the Gysin exact sequence.
Let
\pi:V\to B
be an oriented vector bundle of rank
r
, that is numerable, i.e. it is trivial over an open covering which admits a locally finite partition of unity.
This assumption ensures that the vector bundle is a fibration (see e.g.
[
51
]
[ Th. 12 page 95]) and that admits a bundle Riemannian metric which gives an inner product and a norm
\|\cdot\|_{x}
on each fiber
V_{x}
. The space

E:=\mathrm{sph}(V):=\big\{v\in V\ \big|\ \|v\|_{\pi(v)}=1\big\}

with the restricted base projection
\pi:E\to B
is an oriented
(r-1)
-sphere bundle, namely each fiber
\pi^{-1}(x)
is the unit
sphere in
the
r
-dimensional vector space
V_{x}
. With a common abuse of notation, we denote by
e(E):=e(V)
the Euler class of
V
, and we call it the Euler class of
E
as well.

The following is another fundamental result of the theory of vector bundles (see, e.g.
[
51
, Th. 3, page 483]
.

Theorem 2.19

(Gysin sequence)
.

Let
\pi:V\to B
be an oriented numerable vector bundle of rank
r
with associated sphere bundle
E=\mathrm{sph}(V)
.
For each subset
B^{\prime}\subseteq B
consider the sphere bundle pair
\pi:(E,E^{\prime})\to(B,B^{\prime})

where
E^{\prime}:=\pi^{-1}(B^{\prime})
.
Then there exists a homomorphism

\pi_{*}:H^{*+r-1}(E,E^{\prime};{\mathbb{Z}})\to H^{*}(B,B^{\prime};{\mathbb{Z}})

that lowers the degree of
r-1
and fits in a long exact sequence

\displaystyle...\tttoup^{\pi_{*}}H^{*}(B,B^{\prime};{\mathbb{Z}})\tttoup^{e(E)\smallsmile}H^{*+r}(B,B^{\prime};{\mathbb{Z}})\tttoup^{\pi^{*}}H^{*+r}(E,E^{\prime};{\mathbb{Z}})\tttoup^{\pi_{*}}H^{*+1}(B,B^{\prime};{\mathbb{Z}})\tttoup^{e(E)\smallsmile}...

In particular, if
B^{\prime}=\varnothing
, we have a long exact sequence

(2.24)

...\tttoup^{\pi_{*}}H^{*}(B;{\mathbb{Z}})\tttoup^{e(E)\smallsmile}H^{*+r}(B;{\mathbb{Z}})\tttoup^{\pi^{*}}H^{*+r}(E;{\mathbb{Z}})\tttoup^{\pi_{*}}H^{*+1}(B;{\mathbb{Z}})\tttoup^{e(E)\smallsmile}...

An analogous Gysin sequence for singular cohomology with coefficients in
{\mathbb{Q}}
holds.

We apply the Gysin sequence to the Borel quotient
V_{G}

of a
vector space
V
with a linear action of
G
with canonical projection
\pi_{V}:V_{G}\to BG
. Note that
V_{G}
is numerable, see e.g.
[
18
]
Theorem 8.

Example 2.20
.

Consider the torus
T:={\mathbb{T}}^{d}
acting on
V(k)={\mathbb{C}}^{n}
as in Example
2.17
.
The unit sphere

S(k):=S^{2n-1}\subset V(k)

is invariant under this action. The Borel quotient
S(k)_{T}=S(k)\times_{T}ET
is the total space of the sphere sub-bundle of the vector bundle
V(k)_{T}\to BT
, i.e.

S(k)_{T}=\mathrm{sph}(V(k)_{T})
.
By Lemma

2.18

the sphere bundle
S(k)_{T}\to BT
,
[z,f]\mapsto[f]
, has Euler class

(2.25)

e_{k}=e_{k_{1}}\smallsmile...\smallsmile e_{k_{n}}\qquad\text{where}\qquad e_{k_{i}}=k_{i,1}u_{1}+...+k_{i,d}u_{d}

and

k_{i}=(k_{i,1},\ldots,k_{i,d})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
for any
i=1,\ldots,n
.

Lemma 2.21
.

Assume that the matrix
k=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})
has non-zero components
k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
. Then the
T
-equivariant cohomology of the unit sphere
S(k)\subset V(k)
equipped with the action (
2.23
) is

(2.26)

\frac{{\mathbb{Z}}[u_{1},...,u_{d}]}{(e_{k})}\cong H^{*}_{T}(S(k);{\mathbb{Z}})\,,

where
(e_{k}):=e_{k}\smallsmile{\mathbb{Z}}[u_{1},...,u_{d}]
is the ideal generated by the Euler class

e_{k}
in (
2.25
). The isomorphism in (
2.26
) is given by
v+(e_{k})\to\pi^{*}_{S(k)}v
.

Proof.

Since
k_{i}\neq 0
for any
i=1,\ldots,n
, the Euler class
e_{k}
in
(
2.25
) is non-zero and, since
the ring
H^{*}(BT;{\mathbb{Z}})
is without
non-trivial zero divisors, the homomorphism

H^{*}(BT;{\mathbb{Z}})\tttoup^{e_{k}\smallsmile}H^{*+2n}(BT;{\mathbb{Z}})

is injective. Then we can extract from the exact Gysin sequence
(
2.24
) of the sphere bundle
S(k)_{T}\to BT
the short exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\ttoup H^{*-2n}(BT;{\mathbb{Z}})\ttoup^{e_{k}\smallsmile}H^{*}(BT;{\mathbb{Z}})\ttoup^{\pi_{S(k)}^{*}}H^{*}_{T}(S(k);{\mathbb{Z}})\ttoup 0

where
H^{*}(BT;{\mathbb{Z}})={\mathbb{Z}}[u_{1},...,u_{d}]
by Lemma
2.16
.
Thus

\pi_{S(k)}^{*}

is surjective,

\ker\pi_{S(k)}^{*}=(e_{k})

and the first isomorphism theorem
implies (
2.26
).
∎

Example 2.22
.

Let
G
be a compact connected Lie group acting on the unit sphere
S^{n}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
.
We can extend the
G
action to
{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
by setting

g\cdot\rho z:=\rho(g\cdot z)
,
for any

g\in G
,
\rho\geq 0
,
z\in S^{n}
. By Remark
2.4

the classifying space
BG
is simply connected
and
the vector bundle
{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}_{G}\to BG
is orientable. Let
e\in H^{n+1}(BG;{\mathbb{Q}})
be its Euler class with rational coefficients.
The Borel quotient
S^{n}_{G}
is precisely the total space of the sphere bundle
\mathrm{sph}({\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}_{G})\to BG
, and its Gysin sequence (
2.24
),

\displaystyle...\ttoup H^{*}(BG;{\mathbb{Q}})\ttoup^{e\smallsmile}H^{*+n+1}(BG;{\mathbb{Q}})\ttoup^{\pi_{S^{n}}^{*}}H^{*+n+1}_{G}(S^{n};{\mathbb{Q}})\ttoup...\,,

implies that

(2.27)

H^{*}_{G}(S^{n};{\mathbb{Q}})_{\mathrm{ann}}=\ker(\pi_{S^{n}}^{*})=(e)\qquad\text{where}\qquad(e)=e\smallsmile H^{*}(BG;{\mathbb{Q}})\,.

Note that
e
may be zero.
This is the case if the action on
S^{n}
has fixed points, by Lemma
2.5
.

2.4
Some computations of equivariant cohomology

From now on we use the singular cohomology with coefficients in

{\mathbb{Q}}
.
All computations made with coefficients in
{\mathbb{Z}}
still hold with coefficients in
{\mathbb{Q}}

after taking the tensor product with
{\mathbb{Q}}
.

Product of two spheres.

With the notation of Example
2.20
,
we consider unit spheres
S(k_{1})\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n_{1}}
and
S(k_{2})\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n_{2}}
, equipped with
T={\mathbb{T}}^{d}
actions of the form (
2.23
)
defined by matrices

k_{j}=(k_{j,1},...,k_{j,n_{j}})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d\times n_{j}}
for
j=1,2
, and their product

(2.28)

\displaystyle S(k_{1},k_{2}):=S(k_{1})\times S(k_{2})\,,

equipped with the diagonal
T
action. We make the following
non-collinearity
assumption:
for any
h_{1}\in\{1,...,n_{1}\}
and
h_{2}\in\{1,...,n_{2}\}
,

(2.29)

\text{the vectors}\ k_{1,h_{1}},\,k_{2,h_{2}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}\ \text{are linearly independent over}\ {\mathbb{Q}}\,.

Proposition 2.23
.

(
T
-cohomology of products of spheres)

Under the non-collinearity assumption (
2.29
), the rational
T
-equivariant cohomology of the product of spheres
S(k_{1},k_{2})
in (
2.28
) is

(2.30)

H^{*}_{T}(S(k_{1},k_{2});{\mathbb{Q}})\cong\frac{{\mathbb{Q}}[u_{1},...,u_{d}]}{(e_{k_{1}},e_{k_{2}})}\,,

where
(e_{k_{1}},e_{k_{2}}):=e_{k_{1}}\smallsmile{\mathbb{Q}}[u_{1},...,u_{d}]+e_{k_{2}}\smallsmile{\mathbb{Q}}[u_{1},...,u_{d}]
is the ideal generated by the Euler classes
e_{k_{j}}
of the sphere bundles
S(k_{j})_{T}=\mathrm{sph}(V(k_{j})_{T})
, i.e. for
j=1,2
,

(2.31)

e_{k_{j}}=e_{k_{j},1}\smallsmile...\smallsmile e_{k_{j},n_{j}}\,,\qquad e_{k_{j},h}=k_{j,h,1}u_{1}+...+k_{j,h,d}u_{d}\,.

Proof.

We pull-back the vector bundle
\pi_{2}:=\pi_{V(k_{2})}:V(k_{2})_{T}\to BT
by the projection
\pi_{1}:=\pi_{V(k_{1})}:S(k_{1})_{T}\to BT
, obtaining the vector bundle
\pi_{1}^{*}V(k_{2})_{T}\to S(k_{1})_{T}
, whose total space is

(2.32)

\pi_{1}^{*}V(k_{2})_{T}=\big\{(q_{1},q_{2})\in S(k_{1})_{T}\times V(k_{2})_{T}\ \big|\ \pi_{1}(q_{1})=\pi_{2}(q_{2})\big\}.

By property (i) its Euler class is

e(\pi_{1}^{*}V(k_{2})_{T})=\pi_{1}^{*}e_{k_{2}}
.
We can rewrite the total space of (
2.32
) as

(2.33)

\pi_{1}^{*}V(k_{2})_{T}=\big\{([z_{1},y],[z_{2},y])\ \big|\ [z_{1},y]\in S(k_{1})_{T},\ [z_{2},y]\in V(k_{2})_{T},\ y\in ET\big\}.

We infer that the sphere sub-bundle
\mathrm{sph}(\pi_{1}^{*}V(k_{2})_{T})
is homeomorphic to
S(k_{1},k_{2})_{T}
via the homeomorphism

\mathrm{sph}(\pi_{1}^{*}V(k_{2})_{T})\toup^{\cong}S(k_{1},k_{2})_{T}\,,\qquad([z_{1},y],[z_{2},y])\longmapsto[(z_{1},z_{2}),y]\,.

We claim that the homomorphism
(which enters in the Gysin sequence of the sphere bundle)

(2.34)

H^{*-2n_{2}}_{T}(S(k_{1});{\mathbb{Q}})\to H^{*}_{T}(S(k_{1});{\mathbb{Q}})\,,\ p\mapsto\pi_{1}^{*}e_{k_{2}}\smallsmile p\,,\quad\text{is injective}\,.

Indeed, by Lemma
2.21
, we have the commutative diagram

(2.35)

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{43.52963pt}{-3.16447pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\qquad\text{where}\qquad 2n_{2}-1=\dim(S(k_{2}))\,,

and it is sufficient to show that the map
e_{k_{2}}\smallsmile
in the last row is injective.
Note that the non-collinearity assumption (
2.29
) implies that
the polynomials

(2.36)
