e_{k_{1}},e_{k_{2}}\in{\mathbb{Q}}[u_{1},\ldots,u_{d}]\ \text{in \eqref{ekj} are
relatively prime}\,,

namely, for each
h_{1}\in\{1,...,n_{1}\}
and
h_{2}\in\{1,...,n_{2}\}
, their irreducible factors
e_{k_{1},h_{1}},e_{k_{2},h_{2}}
are not
rational multiple of each other.
If
e_{k_{2}}\smallsmile v+(e_{k_{1}})=(e_{k_{1}})

for some
v\in{\mathbb{Q}}[u_{1},\dots,u_{d}]
then

e_{k_{2}}\smallsmile v=e_{k_{1}}\smallsmile r

for some
r\in{\mathbb{Q}}[u_{1},\dots,u_{d}]

and, in view of (
2.36
),

e_{k_{1}}
divides
v
,
so
v\in(e_{k_{1}})
.
This proves (
2.34
).
The Gysin sequence
(
2.24
) of the sphere bundle
S(k_{1},k_{2})_{T}\to S(k_{1})_{T}:[(z_{1},z_{2}),y]\mapsto[z_{1},y]
, (
2.34
)
and the diagram (
2.35
),
implies the short exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\tttoup\frac{{\mathbb{Q}}[u_{1},\dots,u_{d}]}{(e_{k_{1}})}\tttoup^{e_{k_{2}}\smallsmile}\frac{{\mathbb{Q}}[u_{1},\dots,u_{d}]}{(e_{k_{1}})}\tttoup^{P^{*}}H^{*}_{T}(S(k_{1},k_{2});{\mathbb{Q}})\tttoup 0\,.

Then
H^{*}_{T}(S(k_{1},k_{2});{\mathbb{Q}})\cong\frac{{\mathbb{Q}}[u_{1},\dots,u_{d}]}{(e_{k_{1}})}\Big/\ker(P^{*})=\frac{{\mathbb{Q}}[u_{1},\dots,u_{d}]}{(e_{k_{1}})}\Big/\frac{(e_{k_{1}},e_{k_{2}})}{(e_{k_{1}})}\cong\frac{{\mathbb{Q}}[u_{1},\dots,u_{d}]}{(e_{k_{1}},e_{k_{2}})}
proving (
2.30
).
∎

Corollary 2.24
.

We define

(2.37)

\displaystyle f:=e_{k_{1},1}\smallsmile...\smallsmile e_{k_{1},n_{1}-1}\smallsmile e_{k_{2},1}\smallsmile...\smallsmile e_{k_{2},n_{2}-1}\in H^{2(n_{1}+n_{2}-2)}(B{\mathbb{T}}^{d};{\mathbb{Q}})\,.

Under the non-collinearity assumption (
2.29
),

\pi_{S(k_{1},k_{2})}^{*}f\neq 0\,,\quad i.e.\ f\notin(H^{*}_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}(S(k_{1},k_{2})))_{\mathrm{ann}}\,,

where

\pi_{S(k_{1},k_{2})}:S(k_{1},k_{2})_{T}\to BT
is the canonical projection.

Proof.

In view of (
2.30
),
we have to show that
f\not\in(e_{k_{1}},e_{k_{2}})
.
Assume by contradiction that

(2.38)

f=p_{1}\smallsmile e_{k_{1}}+p_{2}\smallsmile e_{k_{2}}\qquad\text{for some}\ \ p_{1},p_{2}\in{\mathbb{Q}}[u_{1},...,u_{d}]\,.

We factorize each polynomial
in (
2.31
)
as

e_{k_{j}}=f_{j}\smallsmile e_{k_{j},n_{j}}

where

f_{j}:=e_{k_{j},1}\smallsmile...\smallsmile e_{k_{j},n_{j}-1}
.
Note that
f=f_{1}\smallsmile f_{2}
.
By (
2.38
)
and (
2.36
) we get that
f_{1}
divides
p_{2}
, and
f_{2}
divides
p_{1}
, i.e.

p_{2}=f_{1}\smallsmile q_{2}
,
p_{1}=f_{2}\smallsmile q_{1}

for some
q_{1},q_{2}\in{\mathbb{Q}}[u_{1},...,u_{d}]
. By dividing both sides of (
2.38
) by
f
, we obtain

\displaystyle 1=q_{1}\smallsmile e_{k_{1},n_{1}}+q_{2}\smallsmile e_{k_{2},n_{2}}\,.

This is a contradiction, since the right-hand side does not have terms of degree 0.
∎

Join product with a sphere.

The
join
of two topological spaces
X
and
Y
is the quotient space

(2.39)

X\star Y:=\frac{X\times Y\times[0,1]}{\sim}\,,

where the equivalence relation
\sim
collapses
X\times Y\times\{0\}
to
X
, and
X\times Y\times\{1\}
to
Y
, i.e.

(2.40)

(x,y,0)\sim(x,y^{\prime},0)\,,\quad(x,y,1)\sim(x^{\prime},y,1)\,,\qquad\forall x,x^{\prime}\in X\,,\ y,y^{\prime}\in Y\,.

If
X
and
Y
are
G
-spaces, their join is also a
G
-space with the action

g\cdot[x,y,t]=[g\cdot x,g\cdot y,t]
, for any
[x,y,t]\in X\star Y
,

g\in G
.
From here on, we remove the coefficient field from the notation, and just write
H^{*}(\,\cdot\,)
for
H^{*}(\,\cdot\,;{\mathbb{Q}})
. The next result will be used in the proof of Theorem
3.14
.

Lemma 2.25
.

Let
G
be a compact connected Lie group,
X
a
G
-space, and
S^{n}
a sphere with a
G
action. If
H^{*}_{G}(X)_{\mathrm{free}}=H^{*}_{G}(X\star S^{n})_{\mathrm{free}}=0
then

H^{*}_{G}(X\star S^{n})_{\mathrm{ann}}=H^{*}_{G}(X)_{\mathrm{ann}}\smallsmile H^{*}_{G}(S^{n})_{\mathrm{ann}}
.

Proof.

We consider the cone of
X
, which is the join product

CX:=X\star\{q\}

where
\{q\}
is a singleton regarded as a trivial
G
-space. The space
X
can be seen as a
G
-invariant subspace of
CX
via the continuous inclusion
X\hookrightarrow CX
,
x\mapsto[(x,q,0)]
.

Step 1.

There is an exact sequence

(2.41)

\displaystyle...\ttoup H^{*}_{G}(CX\star S^{n},X\star S^{n})\ttoup^{i^{*}}H^{*}_{G}(CX,X)\ttoup^{p^{*}}H^{*}_{G}(CX\times S^{n},X\times S^{n})\ttoup...

where
i:(CX,X)\hookrightarrow(CX\star S^{n},X\star S^{n})
denotes the inclusion and

(2.42)

p:((CX\times S^{n})_{G},(CX\times S^{n})_{G})\to((CX)_{G},(X)_{G})\,,\quad p([z,(x,w)]):=[z,x]\,.

We decompose the join
CX\star S^{n}
as
U\cup V
, where
U
and
V
are the open subsets

U=(CX\star S^{n})\setminus(CX\times S^{n}\times\{0\})\,,\qquad V=(CX\star S^{n})\setminus(CX\times S^{n}\times\{1\})\,.

We also set
U^{\prime}:=U\cap(X\star S^{n})
and
V^{\prime}:=V\cap(X\star S^{n})
. By collapsing the interval factors, the pairs
(U,U^{\prime})
,
(V,V^{\prime})
, and
(U\cap V,U^{\prime}\cap V^{\prime})
are
G
-equivariantly homotopy equivalent to
(CX,X)
,
(S^{n},S^{n})
, and
(CX\times S^{n},X\times S^{n})
respectively.
Note that
H^{*}_{G}(V,V^{\prime})\cong H^{*}_{G}(S^{n},S^{n})
is trivial. The Mayer-Vietoris exact sequence associated with the decomposition

(CX\star S^{n},X\star S^{n})=(U\cup V,U^{\prime}\cup V^{\prime})

reduces to (
2.41
).

Step 2.

Considering the inclusions
j:\{q\}\hookrightarrow(CX,X)
and
k:\{q\}\hookrightarrow(CX\star S^{n},X\star S^{n})
we have
the commutative diagram

(2.43)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{G}(CX\star S^{n},X\star S^{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{G}(CX,X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{G}(X\star S^{n})_{\mathrm{ann}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{G}(X)_{\mathrm{ann}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}}

where

j^{*}:H^{*}_{G}(CX,X)\epi H^{*}(X)_{\mathrm{ann}}
,
k^{*}:H^{*}_{G}(CX\star S^{n},X\star S^{n})\epi H^{*}(X\star S^{n})_{\mathrm{ann}}

are surjectives
and the bottom horizontal arrow is an inclusion
.

As usual, we identify
H^{*}_{G}(\{q\})
with
H^{*}(BG)
via the homeomorphism
\pi_{q}:\{q\}_{G}\to BG
.
Since the cone
CX
is
G
-equivariantly contractible, the cohomology
H^{*}_{G}(CX,\{q\})
is trivial, and the inclusion
X\hookrightarrow CX
is
G
-equivariantly homotopic to the constant map to
q
. Analogously, the join
CX\star S^{n}
is
G
-equivariantly contractible, and therefore
H^{*}_{G}(CX\star S^{n},\{q\})
is trivial, and the inclusion
X\star S^{n}\hookrightarrow CX\star S^{n}
is
G
-equivariantly homotopic to the constant map to
q
. Furthermore
H^{*}_{G}(X)_{\mathrm{free}}=H^{*}_{G}(X\star S^{n})_{\mathrm{free}}=0
and by Lemma
2.10
, the inclusions
j
and
k
induce surjective homomorphisms.
The bottom horizontal arrow is an inclusion, which follows by the inclusion
X\hookrightarrow X\star S^{n}
and the monotonicity of the annihilator (Proposition
2.9
(
i
)).

Step 3.

The diagram (
2.43
) and
the exact sequence (
2.41
) imply that

(2.44)

H^{*}_{G}(X\star S^{n})_{\mathrm{ann}}=\mathrm{im}(k^{*})=\mathrm{im}(j^{*}i^{*})=j^{*}(\ker(p^{*}))\,.

Step 4.

\ker(p^{*})=(\pi^{*}_{CX}e)\smallsmile H_{G}^{*}(CX,X)

where

e\in H^{n+1}(BG)
is
the Euler class of the oriented sphere bundle

\pi_{S^{n}}:S^{n}_{G}\to BG
.

Note that the oriented sphere bundle
\breve{p}:(CX\times S^{n})_{G}\to(CX)_{G}:[z,(x,w)]\mapsto[z,x]
is the pull-back of the sphere bundle
\pi_{S^{n}}:S^{n}_{G}\to BG
via the canonical projection
\pi_{CX}:CX_{G}\to BG:[z,x]\mapsto[z]
. By property (i) its Euler class is therefore
\pi_{CX}^{*}e
.
As an oriented sphere bundle pair

p:((CX\times S^{n})_{G},(X\times S^{n})_{G})\to((CX)_{G},(X)_{G})
,
its associated Gysin exact sequence (Theorem
2.19
) reads

\displaystyle...\ttoup H^{*}_{G}(CX,X)\ttoup^{\pi^{*}_{CX}e\smallsmile}H^{*+n+1}_{G}(CX,X)\ttoup^{p^{*}}H^{*+n+1}_{G}(CX\times S^{n},X\times S^{n})\ttoup...

which implies
\ker(p^{*})=(\pi^{*}_{CX}e)\smallsmile H_{G}^{*}(CX,X)
.

Conclusion.
By
(
2.44
)
and Step 4
we have

(2.45)

H^{*}_{G}(X\star S^{n})_{\mathrm{ann}}=j^{*}(\ker(p))=j^{*}((\pi_{CX}^{*}e)\smallsmile H^{*}_{G}(CX,X))=\breve{\jmath}^{*}(\pi_{CX}^{*}e)\smallsmile j^{*}(H^{*}_{G}(CX,X))

where
\breve{\jmath}:\{q\}\hookrightarrow CX
.
Note that
\breve{\jmath}^{*}\pi_{CX}^{*}e=e
, so

\displaystyle\breve{\jmath}^{*}(\pi_{CX}^{*}e)\smallsmile j^{*}(H^{*}_{G}(CX,X))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{diagk}}}{{=}}e\smallsmile H^{*}_{G}(X)_{\mathrm{ann}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{annsfera}}}{{=}}H_{G}^{*}(S^{n})_{\mathrm{ann}}\smallsmile H^{*}_{G}(X)_{\mathrm{ann}}

and by (
2.45
) the lemma is proved.
∎

3
Equivariant Morse-Conley theory

Let
G
be a compact Lie group and
X
a
compact
metric
G
-space, namely a metric space equipped with a continuous
G
action of isometries.
A
G
-equivariant flow on
X
is an
{\mathbb{R}}
action that commutes with the
G
action. We see this action as a family
\phi=(\phi^{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
of homeomorphisms
\phi^{t}:X\to X
, depending continuously on time
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, such that
\phi^{0}=\mathrm{id}
and
\phi^{s+t}=\phi^{s}\circ\phi^{t}
for any
s,t\in{\mathbb{R}}
. We denote the set of stationary points of
\phi
by

\mathrm{fix}(\phi):=\big\{x\in X\ |\ \phi^{t}(x)=x\,,\ \forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}\big\}.

The set
\mathrm{fix}(\phi)
is
G
-invariant and compact. The alpha-limit and the omega-limit of the
\phi
-orbit through a point
x\in X
are the compact, not-empty, connected
\phi
-invariant sets

\alpha(x):=\bigcap_{t>0}\overline{\phi^{(-\infty,-t]}(x)}\,,\qquad\omega(x):=\bigcap_{t>0}\overline{\phi^{[t,\infty)}(x)}\,.

Equivalently,
\alpha(x)
and
\omega(x)
are the limit sets of
\phi^{t}(x)
as
t\to\mp\infty
respectively, i.e.

\displaystyle\alpha(x)

\displaystyle=\big\{y\in X\ |\ \exists\ t_{n}\to+\infty\ \mbox{such that }\phi^{-t_{n}}(x)\to y\big\}\,,

\displaystyle\omega(x)

\displaystyle=\big\{y\in X\ |\ \exists\ t_{n}\to+\infty\ \mbox{such that }\phi^{t_{n}}(x)\to y\big\}\,.

The set
\alpha(x)
, resp.
\omega(x)
, is the smallest compact set
K\subseteq X
such that, for any neighborhood

U
of
K
, there exists
t_{U}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\phi^{t}(x)\in U
for all
t<t_{U}
, resp. for all
t>t_{U}
.

Definition 3.1
.

(Lyapunov function and gradient-like flow)

A
G
-invariant continuous function

F:X\to{\mathbb{R}}

is a
strict Lyapunov function
when

(3.1)

F(\phi^{t}(x))\leq F(x)\,,\,\forall x\in X,t\geq 0,\,\text{with equality}\,F(\phi^{t}(x))=F(x)\ \text{if and only if}\ \phi^{t}(x)=x\,.

We say that
\phi
is a
gradient-like flow
for
F
.

We say that
c\in{\mathbb{R}}
is a
critical value
of
F
if
F^{-1}(c)\cap\mathrm{fix}(\phi)\neq\varnothing
. A real number that is not a critical value is called a
regular value
of
F
. Note that these notions depend on both
F
and
\phi
.

Remark 3.2
.

If the
G
-space
X
is a manifold equipped with a
G
-invariant Riemannian metric, then any
G
-invariant smooth function
F
is a strict Lyapunov function with respect to the negative gradient flow
\phi^{t}
of
F
. In this case, the notions of critical and regular values are the usual ones from analysis.

For any
x\in X
, since the function
t\mapsto F(\phi^{t}(x))
is monotone decreasing,
by (
3.1
), and
recalling that

\omega(x)
and
\alpha(x)
are
\phi
-invariant,
there exist critical values
a\leq b
such that

(3.2)

\omega(x)\subseteq F^{-1}(a)\,,\ \alpha(x)\subseteq F^{-1}(b)\quad\text{and}\quad\alpha(x)\cup\omega(x)\subseteq\mathrm{fix}(\phi)\,.

Notation.
For any subset
Y\subseteq X
and
c\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we denote

Y^{<c}:=Y\cap F^{-1}(-\infty,c)
,

Y^{\leq c}:=Y\cap F^{-1}(-\infty,c]
,

Y^{>c}:=Y\cap F^{-1}(c,\infty)

and

Y^{\geq c}:=Y\cap F^{-1}[c,\infty)
.
Since
F
is
G
-invariant, if
Y
is
G
-invariant, so are
these four subsets.

Isolating blocks.

For any
W\subseteq X
, we denote by
\mathrm{inv}(W)
its maximal
\phi
-invariant subset, i.e.

\displaystyle\mathrm{inv}(W):=\bigcap_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\phi^{t}(W)=\big\{x\in W\ |\ \phi^{t}(x)\in W\,,\ \forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}\big\}\,.

We assume in the sequel that
W
is compact and so

\mathrm{inv}(W)
is compact as well.

In view of
(
3.2
), if
\mathrm{inv}(W)
is non-empty, it contains the non-empty
\phi
-invariant set
\mathrm{fix}(\phi)\cap W

and, if
F(W)
contains at most one critical value, then
\mathrm{inv}(W)=\mathrm{fix}(\phi)\cap W
. If instead
F(W)
contains at least two critical values,
W
may also contain non-stationary
\phi
-orbits with alpha-limit and omega-limit contained in
W\cap\mathrm{fix}(\phi)
.

The exit set of
W
with respect to the gradient-like flow

\phi
is

W_{-}:=\big\{x\in W\ |\ \phi^{[0,t)}(x)\not\subseteq W\ \forall t>0\big\}=\big\{x\in\partial W\ |\ \phi^{[0,t)}(x)\not\subseteq W\ \forall t>0\big\}.

A subset
S
of
X
is called

(G,\phi)
-invariant if it is both
G
-invariant and
\phi
-invariant.

Definition 3.3
.

(Isolated set, isolating neighborhood, isolating block)

A compact
(G,\phi)
-invariant subset
S\subseteq X
is
isolated
when it admits an
isolating neighborhood

W\subseteq X
, namely a subset
W\subseteq X
such that

\mathrm{inv}(W)=S\subseteq\interior(W)
.
An
isolating block
of
S
is a
G
-invariant compact isolating neighborhood
W
whose exit set satisfies

(3.3)

\overline{W_{-}}\subseteq W^{<c}\qquad\text{where}\qquad c:=\min F|_{K}=\min F_{|S}\,,\quad K:=\mathrm{fix}(\phi)\cap W\,.

The equality

\min F|_{K}=\min F_{|S}
follows by (
3.2
).
Note that the
G
-space
X
is an isolating block of itself with empty exit set.

Remark 3.4
.

Let
K:=\mathrm{fix}(\phi)\cap W
be the stationary set in an isolating block
W
. For each interval
[a,b]\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
with endpoints
a,b\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus F(K)
, the intersection
Z:=W\cap F^{-1}[a,b]
is an isolating block as well with maximal
\phi
-invariant subset

\mathrm{inv}(Z)\subseteq\mathrm{inv}(W)\cap F^{-1}(a,b)\subseteq\interior(Z)

and exit set

Z_{-}=(F^{-1}(a)\cap W)\cup(F^{-1}[a,b]\cap W_{-})
.

An isolated
(G,\phi)
-invariant subset
S
does not admit an isolating block if there is a point
x\in X\setminus S
whose
\alpha(x)
and
\omega(x)
limit sets are both contained in
S
. Such an obstruction never occurs if
S
is contained in a level set of the Lyapunov function
F
, and the following holds.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
K\subseteq\mathrm{fix}(\phi)\cap F^{-1}(c)
be a non-empty isolated compact
G
-invariant set.
Then any isolating neighborhood
V
of
K
contains an isolating block of
K
.

Proof.

Since
K
is compact and
G
-invariant,
we can assume
with no loss of generality
that the
isolating neighborhood
V

of
K
is compact and
G
-invariant.
For any
\tau>0
, we define
the
compact

G
-invariant sets

\displaystyle V^{\tau}

\displaystyle:=\bigcap_{t\in[0,\tau]}\phi^{t}(V)=\big\{x\in V\ \big|\ \phi^{[-\tau,0]}(x)\subseteq V\big\}\,,

(3.4)

\displaystyle V^{\infty}

\displaystyle:=\bigcap_{t\in[0,\infty)}\phi^{t}(V)=\big\{x\in V\ \big|\ \phi^{(-\infty,0]}(x)\subseteq V\big\}\,.

Clearly
K\subseteq V^{\infty}\subseteq V^{\tau}
and,
by the continuity of the flow, each
V^{\tau}
is a
neighborhood
of
K
in
V
.
Moreover

K\subseteq\mathrm{inv}(V^{\tau})\subseteq\mathrm{inv}(V)=K

and

V^{\tau}
is an
isolating neighborhood of

K

for any
\tau>0
.

We now prove that for any
\tau
sufficiently large
V^{\tau}
is an isolating block of
K
.
If
V^{\tau}_{-}=\varnothing
then
V^{\tau}
is an isolating block, as
(
3.3
) trivially holds.
Assume now that
V^{\tau}_{-}\neq\varnothing
.

We first claim that the compact subset
Z:=\partial V\cap V^{\geq c}
has no points in
V^{\infty}
, namely

(3.5)

Z\cap V^{\infty}=\varnothing\,.

Indeed, since no point of
Z
is on a stationary
\phi
-orbit (as
V
is an isolating block), for any
x\in Z
we have
F(\phi^{-t}(x))>c
for all
t>0
.
If there were a point
x\in Z\cap V^{\infty}
, its alpha-limit
\alpha(x)
would be contained in
V^{>c}
. This is impossible because, for any

x\in V^{\infty}
,

\alpha(x)
is non-empty and, by
(
3.2
),

\alpha(x)\subseteq V\cap\mathrm{fix}(\phi)\subseteq\mathrm{inv}(V)=K\subseteq F^{-1}(c)
, having used that
V
is an isolating neighborhood of
K
.

By (
3.5
) and (
3.4
), for any
x\in Z
there is a time
\tau_{x}>0
such that
\phi^{-\tau_{x}}(x)\not\in V
. Therefore, by the continuity of the gradient-like flow, there is an open neighborhood
U_{x}\subseteq X
of
x
such that
\phi^{-\tau_{x}}(U_{x})\cap V=\varnothing
. Since
Z
is compact, it is contained in a finite union
U_{x_{1}}\cup...\cup U_{x_{n}}
.
For any
\tau\geq\max\{\tau_{x_{1}},...,\tau_{x_{n}}\}
, if

x\in Z
then
\phi^{-\tau}(x)\notin V
and
we conclude that

(3.6)

Z\cap V^{\tau}=\varnothing\,,\quad\forall\tau\geq\max\{\tau_{x_{1}},...,\tau_{x_{n}}\}\quad\text{in particular}\quad\overline{V^{\tau}_{-}}\cap Z=\varnothing\,.

Since

\overline{V^{\tau}_{-}}\subseteq\partial V

we deduce by (
3.6
) that

\overline{V^{\tau}_{-}}\subseteq\partial V\setminus Z\subseteq V^{<c}
,
proving that
V^{\tau}
is an isolating block of
K
.
∎

3.1
Local cohomology

The following key notion originated in the
work of Morse
[
41
]
and it is also referred to as the equivariant cohomological Conley index, or as the equivariant critical module of
S
.

Definition 3.6
.

(Local cohomology)

Let
S
be an isolated
(G,\phi)
-invariant set with an isolating block
W
. Let
K:=\mathrm{fix}(\phi)\cap S
be the stationary set therein and
c:=\min F|_{K}=\min F|_{S}
the corresponding minimal critical value. The
G
-equivariant
local cohomology
of
S
is defined as

\displaystyle I^{*}(S):=H^{*}_{G}(W,W^{<c})\,.

As the notation suggests,
I^{*}(S)
is
independent
of the choice of the isolating block
W
. This is a consequence of the following lemma.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
S
be an isolated
(G,\phi)
-invariant set admitting two isolating blocks
V
and
W
. Let
c:=\min F|_{S}
. Then the intersection
Z:=V\cap W
is also an isolating block of
S
, and the inclusions induce isomorphisms

(3.7)

H^{*}_{G}(V,V^{<c})\ttoup^{\cong}H^{*}_{G}(Z,Z^{<c})\leftttoup^{\cong}H^{*}_{G}(W,W^{<c})\,.

Proof.

The intersection
Z=V\cap W
is a compact
G
-invariant neighborhood of
S
, and

S=\mathrm{inv}(Z)\subseteq\interior(Z)
. Thus

Z

is an isolating neighborhood of
S
. Furthermore
the exit set of
Z
satisfies

\overline{Z_{-}}\,\subseteq\,Z\cap\big(\overline{V_{-}}\cup\overline{W_{-}}\big)\subseteq X^{<c}

since both
\overline{V_{-}}
and

\overline{W_{-}}
satisfy (
3.3
).
Therefore
Z
is an isolating block of
S
as well.

Since
\overline{Z_{-}\cup W_{-}}\subseteq X^{<c}
, there is
\epsilon>0
such that

\overline{Z_{-}}\cup\overline{W_{-}}\subseteq X^{<c-\epsilon}
.
We define the compact set

Y:=W^{\geq c-\epsilon}\setminus\interior(Z)\,,

whose exit set satisfies
Y_{-}\subseteq F^{-1}(c-\epsilon)\cup\partial Z
. Since

Y
does not contain the invariant set
S=\mathrm{inv}(W)\subseteq\interior(Z)
, we have

\mathrm{inv}(Y)=\varnothing
and, for any
x\in Y
there is a time
\tau_{x}>0
such that

\phi^{\tau_{x}}(x)\notin Y
, thus

\phi^{\tau_{x}}(x)\in X^{<c-\epsilon}\cup\interior(Z)
. By the continuity of the flow
\phi
, there is an open neighborhood
U_{x}
of
x
such that
\phi^{\tau_{x}}(U_{x})\subseteq X^{<c-\epsilon}\cup\interior(Z)
.
Since the exit
set
Z_{-}\subseteq X^{<c-\epsilon}
, the set

X^{<c-\epsilon}\cup Z
is preserved by the gradient-like flow
\phi^{t}
for any
t\geq 0
, and therefore
\phi^{t}(U_{x})\subseteq X^{<c-\epsilon}\cup Z
for all
t\geq\tau_{x}
. Since
Y
is compact, it is contained in a finite union
U_{x_{1}}\cup...\cup U_{x_{n}}
and we conclude that

(3.8)

\phi^{\tau}(Y)\subseteq Z\cup X^{<c-\epsilon}\qquad\text{where}\qquad\tau:=\max\{\tau_{x_{1}},...,\tau_{x_{n}}\}\,.

The inclusion of pairs

(3.9)

i:(Z\cup X^{<c-\epsilon},Z^{<c}\cup X^{<c-\epsilon})\hookrightarrow(W\cup X^{<c-\epsilon},W^{<c}\cup X^{<c-\epsilon})

is a
G
-equivariant homotopy equivalence, with homotopic inverse

\psi:=\phi^{\tau}:\big(W\cup X^{<c-\epsilon},W^{<c}\cup X^{<c-\epsilon}\big)\to\big(Z\cup X^{<c-\epsilon},Z^{<c}\cup X^{<c-\epsilon}\big)\,,

which is a map between pairs thanks to
(
3.8
) and since
X^{<c-\epsilon}\cup Z

and
Z^{<c}\cup X^{<c-\epsilon}

are preserved by the gradient-like flow
\phi^{t}
for any
t\geq 0
.
Indeed, both compositions
\psi\circ i
and
i\circ\psi
are homotopic to the identity through the corresponding restrictions of the homotopy
h_{s}:X\to X
,
h_{s}(x):=\phi^{s\tau}(x)
, for
s\in[0,1]
. The inclusion
i
in (
3.9
) induces the cohomology isomorphism

(3.10)

i^{*}:H_{G}^{*}(W\cup X^{<c-\epsilon},W^{<c}\cup X^{<c-\epsilon})\toup^{\cong}H_{G}^{*}(Z\cup X^{<c-\epsilon},Z^{<c}\cup X^{<c-\epsilon})\,.

By excision of
the sets
X^{<c-\epsilon}\setminus W
and
X^{<c-\epsilon}\setminus Z
respectively, the inclusion induces isomorphisms

(3.11)

\displaystyle H_{G}^{*}(W\cup X^{<c-\epsilon},W^{<c}\cup X^{<c-\epsilon})

\displaystyle\toup^{\cong}H^{*}_{G}(W,W^{<c})\,,

\displaystyle H_{G}^{*}(Z\cup X^{<c-\epsilon},Z^{<c}\cup X^{<c-\epsilon})

\displaystyle\toup^{\cong}H^{*}_{G}(Z,Z^{<c})\,.

The isomorphisms
(
3.10
) and (
3.11
) fit into the following commutative diagram, where

j:(Z,Z^{<c})\to(W,W^{<c})
is the inclusion,
and all homomorphisms are induced by inclusions,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{G}(W,W^{<c})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{G}(Z,Z^{<c})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{G}^{*}(W\cup X^{<c-\epsilon},W^{<c}\cup X^{<c-\epsilon})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{G}^{*}(Z\cup X^{<c-\epsilon},Z^{<c}\cup X^{<c-\epsilon})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

proving that
j^{*}
is an isomorphism. The same holds for the isolating block
V
and
(
3.7
) is proved.
∎

In order to study the local cohomology, we need the following version of the classical deformation argument from Morse theory.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
W
be an isolating block,
K=\mathrm{fix}(\phi)\cap W
the corresponding stationary set,
[b,c)\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}\setminus F(K)
an interval, and
a\in(-\infty,b]
a real number such that
\overline{W_{-}}\subseteq W^{<a}
. Then the inclusion induces the cohomology isomorphism

(3.12)

H^{*}_{G}(W^{<c},W^{<a})\ttoup^{\cong}H^{*}_{G}(W^{\leq b},W^{<a})\,.

Proof.

The argument is slightly more involved that the one in the proof of Lemma
3.7
, since
c
may be a critical level. We consider a monotone increasing sequence
b:=c_{0}<c_{1}<c_{2}<...
such that
c_{n}\to c
. The family
Z_{n}:=W\cap F^{-1}[b,c_{n+1}]
, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, is an exhaustion by compact sets of
Z:=W\cap F^{-1}[b,c)
. Since
[b,c_{n+1}]
are all regular values
\mathrm{inv}(Z_{n})=\varnothing
, and there is a monotone increasing sequence times
0<\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}\leq\tau_{3}<...
such that

(3.13)

\phi^{\tau_{n}}(Z_{n})\subseteq X^{\leq b},\qquad\forall n\in{\mathbb{N}}\,.

Let
q:(-\infty,c)\to(0,\infty)
be a monotone increasing continuous positive function such that
q(c_{n})\geq\tau_{n}
for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. We define the continuous function
\tau:X^{<c}\to[0,\infty)
,
\tau(x):=q(F(x))
, and the
G
-equivariant continuous map
\psi:X^{<c}\to X^{<c}
,
\psi(x):=\phi^{\tau(x)}(x)
. In view of (
3.13
),

(3.14)

\psi(Z)\subseteq X^{\leq b}.

Since
\overline{W_{-}}\subseteq W^{<a}
, there is
\epsilon>0
so that
\overline{W_{-}}\subseteq W^{<a-\epsilon}
.
We claim that the inclusion

(3.15)

i:(W^{\leq b}\cup X^{<a-\epsilon},W^{<a}\cup X^{<a-\epsilon})\hookrightarrow(W^{<c}\cup X^{<a-\epsilon},W^{<a}\cup X^{<a-\epsilon})

is a
G
-equivariant
homotopy equivalence
, with homotopic inverse

(3.16)

\psi:(W^{<c}\cup X^{<a-\epsilon},W^{<a}\cup X^{<a-\epsilon})\to(W^{\leq b}\cup X^{<a-\epsilon},W^{<a}\cup X^{<a-\epsilon})\,.

Let us first check that

\psi
is a map between the topological pairs (
3.16
).
If
x\in W^{<c}
then

c_{n}\leq F(x)<c_{n+1}
for some
n\in{\mathbb{N}}

and so

\tau_{n}\leq q(c_{n})\leq q(F(x))=\tau(x)
implying that

\phi^{\tau(x)}(x)\in X^{\leq b}
by (
3.13
).
Furthermore, since
the exit set
W_{-}\subseteq W^{<a-\epsilon}
we conclude that
\psi(x)=\phi^{\tau(x)}(x)\in W^{\leq b}\cup X^{<a-\epsilon}
.
Similarly, if
x\in W^{<a}

then

\phi^{\tau(x)}(x)\in W^{<a}\cup X^{<a-\epsilon}
.
The compositions
\psi\circ i
and
i\circ\psi
are homotopic to the identity by means of suitable restrictions of the homotopy
h_{s}:X^{<c}\to X^{<c}
,
h_{s}(x):=\phi^{s\tau(x)}(x)
, for
s\in[0,1]
.

The inclusion
i

in (
3.15
)
induces the cohomology isomorphism

(3.17)

\displaystyle i^{*}:H_{G}^{*}(W^{<c}\cup X^{<a-\epsilon},W^{<a}\cup X^{<a-\epsilon})\toup^{\cong}H_{G}^{*}(W^{\leq b}\cup X^{<a-\epsilon},W^{<a}\cup X^{<a-\epsilon})\,,

and, by excision of the set
X^{<a-\epsilon}\setminus W
, the inclusions induce isomorphisms

(3.18)

\displaystyle H_{G}^{*}(W^{<c}\cup X^{<a-\epsilon},W^{<a}\cup X^{<a-\epsilon})

\displaystyle\toup^{\cong}H^{*}_{G}(W^{<c},W^{<a})\,,

\displaystyle H_{G}^{*}(W^{\leq b}\cup X^{<a-\epsilon},W^{<a}\cup X^{<a-\epsilon})

\displaystyle\toup^{\cong}H^{*}_{G}(W^{\leq b},W^{<a})\,.

The isomorphisms (
3.17
)
and (
3.18
) fit into the commutative diagram induced by inclusions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{G}(W^{<c},W^{<a})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{G}(W^{\leq b},W^{<a})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{G}^{*}(W^{<c}\cup X^{<a-\epsilon},W^{<a}\cup X^{<a-\epsilon})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{G}^{*}(W^{\leq b}\cup X^{<a-\epsilon},W^{<a}\cup X^{<a-\epsilon})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

proving (
3.12
).
∎

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
S
be an isolated
(G,\phi)
-invariant set with isolating block
W
. Let
K:=\mathrm{fix}(\phi)\cap S
be the corresponding stationary set, and
b\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus F(K)
. We decompose
W
as a union of isolating blocks
W^{\leq b}
and
W^{\geq b}
, and consider the corresponding
(G,\phi)
-invariant sets

S_{1}:=\mathrm{inv}(W^{\leq b})
and

S_{2}:=\mathrm{inv}(W^{\geq b})
.
Then there is a long exact sequence of
H^{*}(BG)
-modules

(3.19)

\displaystyle...\ttoup^{\delta^{*-1}}I^{*}(S_{2})\ttoup I^{*}(S)\ttoup I^{*}(S_{1})\ttoup^{\delta^{*}}I^{*+1}(S_{2})\ttoup...

In particular

(3.20)

I^{*}(S_{1})_{\mathrm{ann}}\smallsmile I^{*}(S_{2})_{\mathrm{ann}}\subseteq I^{*}(S)_{\mathrm{ann}}\,.

Proof.

The compact sets
W^{\leq b}
and
W^{\geq b}
are isolating blocks respectively of
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
, by Remark
3.4
.
We can assume that both
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
are non-empty, for otherwise the lemma is trivial.
We set
c_{1}:=\min F|_{S_{1}}=\min F|_{S}
and
c_{2}:=\min F|_{S_{2}}
, so that
c_{1}<b<c_{2}
. The long exact sequence of the triple
W^{<c_{1}}\subseteq W^{<c_{2}}\subseteq W
reads (cf. Proposition
2.6
)

(3.21)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces...}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{G}(W,W^{<c_{2}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underbrace{H^{*}_{G}(W,W^{<c_{1}})}_{=I^{*}(S)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{*}_{G}(W^{<c_{2}},W^{<c_{1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces...}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{*-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{*}}

The set

V:=W^{\geq b}
is an isolating block of
S_{2}
with exit set
V_{-}=W\cap F^{-1}(b)
. By excision of the set
W^{<b}
(note that

\overline{W^{<b}}\subset W^{<c_{2}}
), the inclusion induces an isomorphism

(3.22)

H^{*}_{G}(W,W^{<c_{2}})\ttoup^{\cong}H^{*}_{G}(V,V^{<c_{2}})=I^{*}(S_{2})\,.

The set
W^{\leq b}
is an isolating block for
S_{1}
, and Lemma
3.8
implies that the inclusion induces an isomorphism

(3.23)

H^{*}_{G}(W^{<c_{2}},W^{<c_{1}})\ttoup^{\cong}H^{*}_{G}(W^{\leq b},W^{<c_{1}})=I^{*}(S_{1})\,.

Inserting (
3.22
)-(
3.23
) into (
3.21
), we deduce (
3.19
) and Proposition
1.4
-(
ii
) implies (
3.20
).
∎

Within a level set of the Lyapunov function, a disjoint union decomposition of a
G
-invariant compact stationary set has the following effect on the local cohomology.

Lemma 3.10
.

(Splitting)

Let
K_{1},K_{2}\subseteq\mathrm{fix}(\phi)\cap F^{-1}(c)
be disjoint isolated
G
-invariant compact stationary sets contained in a level set of the Lyapunov function.
Then the inclusion induces an
H^{*}(BG)
-module isomorphism

I^{*}(K_{1}\sqcup K_{2})\ttoup^{\cong}I^{*}(K_{1})\oplus I^{*}(K_{2})
.
In particular

I^{*}(K_{1})_{\mathrm{ann}}\cap I^{*}(K_{2})_{\mathrm{ann}}=I^{*}(K_{1}\sqcup K_{2})_{\mathrm{ann}}
.

Proof.

Since the compact stationary sets
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
are disjoint, they admit disjoint isolating neighborhoods, and thus disjoint isolating blocks
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
by Lemma
3.5
.
The disjoint union
W_{1}\sqcup W_{2}
is an isolating block of
K_{1}\sqcup K_{2}
.
The cohomology of a disjoint union of pairs is isomorphic to the direct sum of the cohomology of the pairs with an isomorphism induced by the pair of inclusions.
Therefore

I^{*}(K_{1}\!\sqcup\!K_{2})\!=\!H^{*}_{G}(W_{1}\!\sqcup\!W_{2},W_{1}^{<c}\!\sqcup\!W_{2}^{<c})\!\cong\!H^{*}_{G}(W_{1},W_{1}^{<c})\!\oplus\!H^{*}_{G}(W_{2},W_{2}^{<c})\!=\!I^{*}(K_{1})\!\oplus\!I^{*}(K_{2})

proving the lemma.
∎

The following lemma is used in
the proofs of Lemma
3.12

and Theorem
3.14
.

Lemma 3.11
.

Any
G
-orbit
G\cdot x
has a
G
-invariant open neighborhood
U
such that

H^{*}_{G}(G\cdot x)_{\mathrm{ann}}=H^{*}_{G}(U)_{\mathrm{ann}}.

Proof.

The existence theorem of a slice, see e.g.
[
12
]
[ chapter II Theorem 5.4, Def. 4.1, Theorem 4.2],
implies the existence of a
G
-invariant neighborhood
U
of
G\cdot x
and a
G
-equivariant retraction
r:U\to G\cdot x
.
Applying the monotonicity property of the annihilator in
Proposition
2.9
(
i
)
to the map
r
and the inclusion
i:G\cdot x\hookrightarrow U

we conclude that

H^{*}_{G}(G\cdot x)_{\mathrm{ann}}\subseteq H^{*}_{G}(U)_{\mathrm{ann}}

and

H^{*}_{G}(U)_{\mathrm{ann}}\subseteq H^{*}_{G}(G\cdot x)_{\mathrm{ann}}
.
∎

The computation of the local cohomology of an isolated

\phi
-invariant set
may be complicated.
For our applications, it will be sufficient to single out
an
H^{*}(BG)
-submodule of the local cohomology.

Lemma 3.12
.

If
G\cdot x\subseteq\mathrm{fix}(\phi)
is an isolated
(G,\phi)
-invariant set,
then
H^{*}_{G}(G\cdot x)_{\mathrm{ann}}\subseteq I^{*}(G\cdot x)_{\mathrm{ann}}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.11

there is a
G
-invariant open neighborhood
U
of the orbit
G\cdot x
such that
H^{*}_{G}(G\cdot x)_{\mathrm{ann}}=H^{*}_{G}(U)_{\mathrm{ann}}
.
Any sufficiently small neighborhood
V\subset U
of
G\cdot x
is an isolating neighborhood, and contains an isolating block
W\subset V\subset U
of
G\cdot x
according to Lemma
3.5
.
The monotonicity property of the annihilator (cf. Proposition
2.9
-(
i
)) implies that

H^{*}_{G}(G\cdot x)_{\mathrm{ann}}\subseteq H^{*}_{G}(W)_{\mathrm{ann}}\subseteq H^{*}_{G}(U)_{\mathrm{ann}}=H^{*}_{G}(G\cdot x)_{\mathrm{ann}}\,.

The local cohomology of
G\cdot x
is
I^{*}(G\cdot x)=H^{*}_{G}(W,W^{<c})
where
c:=F(G\cdot x)
.
For any
r\in H^{*}_{G}(G\cdot x)_{\mathrm{ann}}=H^{*}_{G}(W)_{\mathrm{ann}}=\ker(\pi_{W}^{*})
and
h\in I^{*}(G\cdot x)
, we have

r\cdot h=0

by (
2.8
)
and therefore
r\in I^{*}(G\cdot x)_{\mathrm{ann}}
.
∎

3.2
A cup-length lower bound for the number of critical values

A cohomological version of the classical Lüsternik-Schnirelmann theorem asserts that the number of critical points of a smooth function on a closed manifold is strictly larger than the cup-length of the manifold, namely the maximal number of cohomology classes of positive degree whose total cup product is non-zero. For functions invariant by the action of a compact Lie group
G
,
it is convenient to consider the cup-length with respect to the equivariant cohomology classes coming from
H^{*}(BG)
. For a given
G
-space
X
, we define

(3.24)

\mathrm{CL}_{G}(X):=\sup\Big\{k\geq 1\ \big|\ \exists\ w_{1},...,w_{k}\in\tilde{H}^{*}(BG)\mbox{ such that }w_{1}\smallsmile...\smallsmile w_{k}\not\in H^{*}_{G}(X)_{\mathrm{ann}}\Big\}

with the convention
\sup\varnothing=0
. Here,

\tilde{H}^{*}(BG)
is the reduced cohomology

\displaystyle\tilde{H}^{d}(BG):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}ll}0\,,&\mbox{if }d=0\,,\\
H^{d}(BG)\,,&\mbox{if }d>0\,.\end{array}\right.

Example 3.13
.

Let
S(k_{1})\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n_{1}}
and
S(k_{2})\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n_{2}}
denote the unit spheres
with
{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
-actions defined by two matrices
k_{1}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d\times n_{1}}
and
k_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d\times n_{2}}
as in Example
2.17
, satisfying
the non-collinearity assumption (
2.29
). Then the product
S(k_{1})\times S(k_{2})

equipped with the diagonal action satisfies

(3.27)

\mathrm{CL}_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}(S(k_{1})\times S(k_{2}))\geq n_{1}+n_{2}-2\,.

Indeed,

f\in H^{*}(B{\mathbb{T}}^{d})
in (
2.37
) is the cup product of
n_{1}+n_{2}-2
cohomology classes of
\tilde{H}^{*}(B{\mathbb{T}}^{d})
and does not annihilate
H^{*}_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}(S(k_{1},k_{2}))
.
From the definition of cup-length (
3.24
) we deduce (
3.27
).

In the proof of Theorem
1.1
we shall use the following
result.
Let
X\star Y
be the join product of two
G
-spaces
X
and
Y
, introduced in
(
2.39
).
We recall that
X
and
Y
can be identified with the subspaces
(X\times Y\times\{0\})/\sim
and
(X\times Y\times\{1\})/\sim
of
X\star Y
respectively. A
G
action on
X
is
transitive
when the whole space
X
is a single
G
-orbit.

Theorem 3.14
.

Let
T={\mathbb{T}}^{d}
be a torus of dimension
d\geq 1
,
M
a compact metric
T
-space
such that every point of
M
has finite stabilizer, and
S^{n}
an
n
-sphere equipped with a transitive
T
action. Let
F:M\star S^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be a continuous
T
-invariant function admitting a gradient-like flow
\phi
such that the subspace
S^{n}\subseteq M\star S^{n}
is stationary, i.e.

S^{n}\subseteq\mathrm{fix}(\phi)
.
If there are only finitely many
T
-orbits in
\mathrm{fix}(\phi)\setminus S^{n}
, then
F
has at least
\mathrm{CL}_{T}(M)+1

critical values
different from
F(S^{n})
.

Proof.

By assumption, the subspace
S^{n}\subseteq M\star S^{n}
is a
T
-orbit in
\mathrm{fix}(\phi)
, and we denote by
\ell_{*}:=F(S^{n})
its critical value. We assume that
\mathrm{fix}(\phi)
consists of finitely many
T
-orbits, so that in particular
F(\mathrm{fix}(\phi))
consists of finitely many pairwise distinct
critical values
\ell_{*},\ell_{1},...,\ell_{k}
. We shall prove that

(3.28)

\underbrace{\tilde{H}^{*}(BT)\smallsmile\ldots\smallsmile\tilde{H}^{*}(BT)}_{\times k}\subseteq H^{*}_{T}(M)_{\mathrm{ann}}\,,

where
\tilde{H}^{*}(BT)
is the reduced cohomology of the classifying space
BT
introduced in (
3.2
).
In view of (
3.24
), this implies that
k\geq\mathrm{CL}_{T}(M)+1
, and thus proves the theorem.

Step 1 (Morse decomposition).

The whole space
M\star S^{n}
is an isolating block of itself with local cohomology

I^{*}(M\star S^{n})=H^{*}_{T}(M\star S^{n})
.
For each critical value
\ell
of
F
, we denote the corresponding stationary set by
K_{\ell}:=\mathrm{fix}(\phi)\cap F^{-1}(\ell)
.
A repeated application of Lemma
3.9
and Remark
3.4
implies that

(3.29)

I^{*}(K_{\ell_{*}})_{\mathrm{ann}}\smallsmile I^{*}(K_{\ell_{1}})_{\mathrm{ann}}\smallsmile...\smallsmile I^{*}(K_{\ell_{k}})_{\mathrm{ann}}\subseteq I^{*}(M\star S^{n})_{\mathrm{ann}}\,.

Step 2.

The annihilator of
I^{*}(M\star S^{n})
is given by

(3.30)

I^{*}(M\star S^{n})_{\mathrm{ann}}=e\smallsmile H^{*}_{T}(M)_{\mathrm{ann}}\,,

where

e\in H^{n+1}(BT)\setminus\{0\}
is a
non-zero cohomology class such that

(3.31)

H^{*}_{T}(S^{n})_{\mathrm{ann}}=e\smallsmile H^{*}(BT)\,.

The
T
action on
M\star S^{n}
has no fixed points; indeed, each point of
M
has finite stabilizer, and the
T
action on
S^{n}
is transitive.
By Lemma
2.7
, any orbit
T\cdot x\subset M\star S^{n}
has non-trivial annihilator
H^{*}_{T}(T\cdot x)_{\mathrm{ann}}\neq 0
. Moreover, by Lemma
3.11
,
T\cdot x
has a
T
-invariant open neighborhood
U_{x}
such that

(3.32)

H^{*}_{T}(U_{x})_{\mathrm{ann}}=H^{*}_{T}(T\cdot x)_{\mathrm{ann}}\neq 0.

By compactness, we can cover

M\star S^{n}\subset U_{x_{1}}\cup\ldots\cup U_{x_{N}}

by finitely many neighborhoods as in (
3.32
),
and by the union subadditivity property of the annihilators (Proposition
2.9
-(
ii
))
we infer
that
H^{*}_{T}(M\star S^{n})_{\mathrm{ann}}\neq 0
,
and thus
that
H^{*}_{T}(M\star S^{n})_{\mathrm{free}}=0
. Similarly,
H^{*}_{T}(M)_{\mathrm{free}}=0
.
Thus Lemma
2.25
implies that

(3.33)

\displaystyle I^{*}(M\star S^{n})_{\mathrm{ann}}=H^{*}_{T}(M)_{\mathrm{ann}}\smallsmile H^{*}_{T}(S^{n})_{\mathrm{ann}}\,.

By (
2.27
), there is a cohomology class

e\in H^{n+1}(BT)
such that
(
3.31
) holds.
Once again, since the
T
action on
S^{n}
has no fixed points, Lemma
2.7
implies that
H^{*}_{T}(S^{n})_{\mathrm{ann}}
is non-trivial, and therefore
e\neq 0
. The identity (
3.30
) follows by (
3.33
)
and (
3.31
).

Step 3.

The local cohomology of the critical sets
K_{\ell_{*}},K_{\ell_{1}},...,K_{\ell_{k}}
satisfies

(3.34)

\displaystyle e\smallsmile H^{*}(BT)\subseteq I^{*}(K_{\ell_{*}})_{\mathrm{ann}}\,,\qquad\tilde{H}^{*}(BT)\subseteq I^{*}(K_{\ell_{i}})_{\mathrm{ann}}\,,\ \forall i=1,\ldots,k\,,

where
\tilde{H}^{*}(BT)

is the reduced cohomology
(
3.2
).

By Lemma
3.10
,
for any critical level
\ell
, we have

(3.35)

I^{*}(K_{\ell})_{\mathrm{ann}}=\bigcap_{T\cdot x\subseteq K_{\ell}}I^{*}(T\cdot x)_{\mathrm{ann}}\,.

Lemma
3.12
implies

(3.36)

H^{*}_{T}(T\cdot x)_{\mathrm{ann}}\subseteq I^{*}(T\cdot x)_{\mathrm{ann}}\,,\quad\forall x\in M\star S^{n}\,.

Since any point of
M
has finite stabilizer, any
x\in\mathrm{fix}(\phi)\setminus S^{n}
has finite stabilizer as well. Therefore, by Remark
2.8
, the equivariant cohomology
H^{d}_{T}(T\cdot x)
is trivial in every degree
d>0
. This implies

(3.37)

H^{*}_{T}(T\cdot x)_{\mathrm{ann}}=\tilde{H}^{*}(BT)\,.

If
\ell\neq\ell_{*}

we conclude,
by (
3.35
) and
(
3.36
)-(
3.37
), that

\tilde{H}^{*}(BT)\subseteq I^{*}(K_{\ell})_{\mathrm{ann}}
,
proving the last inclusions in
(
3.34
).
If
\ell=\ell_{*}

we deduce similarly the first
inclusion in
(
3.34
) using
also
(
3.31
)
where
\deg(e)>0
.

Step 4.

Substituting (
3.30
) and (
3.34
)
in (
3.29
), we obtain

e\smallsmile\tilde{H}^{*}(BT)^{\smallsmile k}\subseteq e\smallsmile H^{*}_{T}(M)_{\mathrm{ann}}
,
and, since
e\neq 0
and
H^{*}(BT)
has no non-trivial zero divisors, we deduce (
3.28
).
∎

The following, more ordinary, Lüsternik-Schnirelmann’s type result can be proved in a way similar to Theorem
3.14
.

Theorem 3.15
.

Let
G
be a compact Lie group,
M
be a
compact metric
G
-space such that every point of
M
has finite stabilizer, and
F:M\to{\mathbb{R}}
a continuous
G
-invariant function admitting a gradient-like flow
\phi
such that
\mathrm{fix}(\phi)
consists of finitely many
G
-orbits. Then
F
possesses at least
\mathrm{CL}_{G}(M)+1
critical levels.

Theorems
3.14

and
3.15

provide a lower bound for the number of distinct
critical values
, allowing in Section
8

to prove
multiplicity of

({\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
-geometrically distinct Stokes waves.

Part II
Bifurcation of
3d
Stokes waves

In this part we apply the previous results to prove multiplicity
of gravity-capillary Stokes waves.

4
The variational Lyapunov-Schmidt reduction

By (
1.15
)
the linearized operator
{\mathcal{L}}_{c}:={\rm d}_{u}{\mathcal{F}}(c,0)
is equal to

{\mathcal{L}}_{c}=c\cdot\partial_{x}+J{\rm d}_{u}\nabla^{L^{2}}{\mathcal{H}}(0)=c\cdot\partial_{x}+J\left(\begin{matrix}g-\kappa\Delta&0\\
0&G(0)\end{matrix}\right)\,

where
G(0)=|D|\tanh({\mathtt{h}}|D|)
is the Fourier multiplier
with symbol
|\xi|\tanh({\mathtt{h}}|\xi|)

if
{\mathtt{h}}<\infty
, and

G(0)=|D|
in infinite depth
(we denote

D:={\rm i}^{-1}\partial_{x}
)

The operator
{\mathcal{L}}_{c}
can be symplectically
diagonalized:
the real phase space

L^{2}:=L^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma},{\mathbb{R}}^{2})
decomposes as direct sum of infinitely many
2
dimensional real
symplectic
subspaces
invariant

under
{\mathcal{L}}_{c}
that we now describe.
Define the real vectors

(4.1)

v_{j}^{(1)}(x):=\begin{pmatrix}M_{j}\cos(j\cdot x)\\
M_{j}^{-1}\sin(j\cdot x)\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad v_{j}^{(2)}(x):=\begin{pmatrix}-M_{j}\sin(j\cdot x)\\
M_{j}^{-1}\cos(j\cdot x)\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad j\in\Gamma^{\prime}\setminus\{0\}\,,

where

(4.2)

M_{j}:=(g+\kappa|j|^{2})^{-\frac{1}{4}}(|j|\tanh({\mathtt{h}}|j|))^{\frac{1}{4}}

and

v_{0}^{(1)}:=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}

and
v_{0}^{(2)}:=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}
.
The following lemma is directly verified.

Lemma 4.1
.

The following holds:

(i)

Symplecticity.

For any
j\in\Gamma^{\prime}

the
2
-dimensional real subspace

(4.3)

V_{j}:=\big\{\alpha_{j}v_{j}^{(1)}+\beta_{j}v_{j}^{(2)}\ |\ \alpha_{j},\beta_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}\big\}

is symplectic.
Each
V_{j}

is symplectic orthogonal to any other
V_{k}
for any
j\neq k
.

(ii)

Completeness.

The phase space admits the decomposition

L^{2}:=L^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma},{\mathbb{R}}^{2})=\overline{\bigoplus_{j\in\Gamma^{\prime}}V_{j}}^{L^{2}}

where

\{v_{j}^{(1)},v_{j}^{(2)}\}_{j\in\Gamma^{\prime}}
is a symplectic basis of
L^{2}
and

(4.4)

u=\sum_{j\in\Gamma^{\prime}}\alpha_{j}\,v_{j}^{(1)}+\beta_{j}\,v_{j}^{(2)}\,,\quad\forall u\in L^{2}\,,

with coordinates

(4.5)

\alpha_{j}:=\alpha_{j}(u):=\boldsymbol{\Omega}(u,v_{j}^{(2)})\,,\quad\beta_{j}:=\beta_{j}(u):=\boldsymbol{\Omega}(v_{j}^{(1)},u)\,,\quad\forall j\in\Gamma^{\prime}\,.

(iii)

Invariance.
For any
j\in\Gamma^{\prime}\setminus\{0\}
, it results

\partial_{x}v_{j}^{(1)}=jv_{j}^{(2)}
,
\partial_{x}v_{j}^{(2)}=-jv_{j}^{(1)}
and

(4.6)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{c}v_{j}^{(1)}=(c\cdot j-\omega(j))v_{j}^{(2)}\,,\quad{\mathcal{L}}_{c}v_{j}^{(2)}=-(c\cdot j-\omega(j))v_{j}^{(1)}\,,

where
\omega(j)
is the frequency defined
in (
1.23
),
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{c}v_{0}^{(1)}=-g\,v_{0}^{(2)}
.

(iv)

{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}
and
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
symmetries.
For any

u\in L^{2}
and any
j\in\Gamma^{\prime}
we have

(4.7)

\displaystyle\alpha_{j}(\tau_{\theta}u)+{\rm i}\,\beta_{j}(\tau_{\theta}u)=e^{-{\rm i}\,j\cdot\theta}(\alpha_{j}(u)+{\rm i}\,\beta_{j}(u))\,,\quad\forall\theta\in{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}\,,

\displaystyle\alpha_{j}(\rho u)+{\rm i}\,\beta_{j}(\rho u)=\alpha_{j}(u)-{\rm i}\,\beta_{j}(u)\,.

(v)

Momentum.
The momentum

{\mathcal{I}}
in (
1.12
) is equal to

(4.8)

{\mathcal{I}}(u)=\frac{1}{2}{\rm d}_{u}{\mathcal{I}}(u)[u]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{Def Hamiltonian vector field}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{2}{\bf\Omega}(X_{{\mathcal{I}}}(u),u)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{XI}}}{{=}}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j\in\Gamma^{\prime}\setminus\{0\}}j\,(\alpha_{j}^{2}(u)+\beta_{j}^{2}(u))\,.

In view of (
4.6
), for any
c\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, the Kernel of
{\mathcal{L}}_{c}

is Fourier supported on the wave vectors

j\in\Gamma^{\prime}
such that

c\cdot j=\omega(j)
, so

\ker({\mathcal{L}}_{c})\not=0
if and only if

c
belongs to the set
\mathsf{C}
defined in (
1.22
).

We reduce (
1.14
) to a finite dimensional problem by a variational Lyapunov-Schmidt procedure.

The Kernel
V=\ker({\mathcal{L}}_{c_{*}})
.

Let
c_{*}
be a speed in the set
\mathsf{C}
defined in (
1.22
) so that

\ker({\mathcal{L}}_{c_{*}})\not=0
. Specifically, in view of
(
4.6
),

(4.9)

V:=\ker({\mathcal{L}}_{c_{*}})=\Big\{c_{*}\cdot\partial_{x}v+{\rm d}_{u}X_{\mathcal{H}}(0)v=0\Big\}=\Big\{{\rm d}_{u}\nabla^{L^{2}}({\mathcal{H}}+c_{*}{\mathcal{I}})(0)v=0\Big\}=\bigoplus_{j\in{\mathcal{V}}}V_{j}

where

V_{j}

are the
2
dimensional real symplectic subspaces in (
4.3
) and
{\mathcal{V}}
is the set of resonant wave vectors
in (
1.24
). The subspace

V
is symplectic.
Furthermore
V
is finite dimensional, because
the dispersion relation in
(
1.23
) satisfies

\omega(j)\approx\sqrt{\kappa}|j|^{\frac{3}{2}}
, and then

(4.10)

\#{\mathcal{V}}\leq\#\Big\{j\in\Gamma^{\prime}\setminus\{0\}\ \big|\ |c|\geq\frac{\omega(j)}{|j|}\Big\}\lesssim_{\Gamma,\kappa,g,{\mathtt{h}}}|c|^{4}\,.

Lemma 4.2
.

(Collinear vectors in
{\mathcal{V}}
)
For any value of surface tension

\kappa>0
, gravity
g>0
, depth

{\mathtt{h}}>0
, and any lattice
\Gamma
, the number of collinear vectors of
{\mathcal{V}}
is at most
2
.

Proof.

The dispersion relation
\omega(\xi)

in (
1.23
)
is radial, i.e.
\omega(\xi)=\breve{\omega}(|\xi|)
. The function

t\mapsto\frac{\breve{\omega}(t)}{t}
has at most one local minimum and

\#\big\{t>0\ \big|\ \frac{\breve{\omega}(t)}{t}=s\big\}\leq 2

for any
s>0
.
∎

Lyapunov Schmidt decomposition.

We decompose
the phase space
L^{2}=L^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma},{\mathbb{R}}^{2})

equipped with the symplectic form (
1.8
)
as

(4.11)

L^{2}=V\oplus W

where
V=\ker({\mathcal{L}}_{c_{*}})
and
W
is its symplectic orthogonal

(4.12)

W:=V^{\bot_{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}}=\overline{\bigoplus_{j\in\Gamma^{\prime}\setminus{\mathcal{V}}}V_{j}}^{L^{2}}\,.

We denote by
\Pi_{V}
and
\Pi_{W}=\text{Id}-\Pi_{V}

the symplectic projectors onto
V
and
W
respectively.
According to (
4.11
)
the space
X
in (
1.18
)
admits the decomposition

X=V\oplus(W\cap X)
and (
1.14
) is equivalent to
find a solution
(c,v,w)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times V\times(W\cap X)
of

\begin{cases}\Pi_{V}{\cal F}(c,v+w)=0\qquad\quad\,\text{bifurcation equation}\\
\Pi_{W\cap Y}{\cal F}(c,v+w)=0\qquad\text{range equation}\end{cases}

where
\Pi_{W\cap Y}
and
\Pi_{V}
are the projectors associated to the decomposition

Y=V\oplus(W\cap Y)
.

The range equation is solved by the implicit function theorem. We denote
B_{r}^{V}
the ball of radius
r
and center 0 in
V
.

Lemma 4.3
.

(Solution of the range equation)

There exists an analytic function
w:B_{r}(c_{*})\times B_{r}^{V}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times V\to W\cap X

defined in a neighborhood of
(c_{*},0)
solving

(4.13)

\Pi_{W\cap Y}{\cal F}(c,v+w(c,v))=0\,,

satisfying

(4.14)

w(c,0)=0\,,\quad{\rm d}_{v}w(c,0)=0\,,\quad\forall c\in B_{r}(c_{*})\,,

and

(4.15)

w(c,\rho v)=\rho w(c,v)\,,\quad w(c,\tau_{\theta}v)=\tau_{\theta}w(c,v)\,,\quad\forall\theta\in\mathbb{T}^{2}_{\Gamma}\,.

Furthermore
the range equation (
4.13
) is equivalent to

(4.16)

{\rm d}_{u}\Psi(c,v+w(c,v))[\hat{w}]=\boldsymbol{\Omega}({\mathcal{F}}(c,v+w(c,v)),\hat{w})=0\,,\quad\forall\hat{w}\in W\,.

Proof.

The variational characterization
(
4.16
) follows because, recalling (
1.9
), (
1.15
), (
1.16
),

(4.17)

{\rm d}_{u}\Psi(c,v+w(c,v))[\hat{u}]=\boldsymbol{\Omega}({\mathcal{F}}(c,v+w(c,v)),\hat{u})=0\,,\quad\forall\hat{u}\in X\,,

and the subspaces

V
and
W
are symplectic orthogonal, cf. (
4.12
). The solution of
(
4.13
)
is constructed by the analytic implicit function theorem in the Appendix.
∎

In view of Lemma
4.3

we are reduced to
solve the bifurcation equation

\Pi_{V}{\mathcal{F}}(c,v+w(c,v))=0

where
w(c,v)
is the solution of (
4.13
).
This equation has still a
variational structure.

Lemma 4.4
.

(Variational
structure of the bifurcation equation)

The reduced functional
\Phi:B_{r}(c_{*})\times B_{r}^{V}\to{\mathbb{R}}
,

(4.18)

\Phi(c,v):=\Psi(c,v+w(c,v))=({\mathcal{H}}+c\cdot{\mathcal{I}})(v+w(c,v))\,,

is analytic and satisfies, for any
(c,v)\in B_{r}(c_{*})\times B_{r}^{V}
,

(4.19)

\Phi(c,\rho v)=\Phi(c,v)\,,\quad\Phi(c,\tau_{\theta}v)=\Phi(c,v)\,,\quad\forall\theta\in\mathbb{T}^{2}_{\Gamma}\,.

Furthermore, for any
(c,v)\in B_{r}(c_{*})\times B_{r}^{V}
, for any
\hat{v}\in V
,

(4.20)

\displaystyle{\rm d}_{v}\Phi(c,v)[\hat{v}]={\rm d}_{u}\Psi(c,v+w(c,v))[\hat{v}]

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\Omega}(\Pi_{V}{\mathcal{F}}(c,v+w(c,v)),\hat{v})\,.

Therefore, for any
c\in B_{r}(c_{*})
, any critical point
v\in B_{r}^{V}
of
\Phi(c,\cdot)
gives rise to a solution
u:=v+w(c,v)\in X
of (
1.14
).

Proof.

Differentiating
(
4.18
) with respect to
v
we have

\displaystyle{\rm d}_{v}\Phi(c,v)[\hat{v}]

\displaystyle={\rm d}_{u}\Psi(c,v+w(c,v))[\hat{v}+\underbrace{{\rm d}_{v}w(c,v)[\hat{v}]}_{\in W}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{Variational Range0},\eqref{Variational Range}}}{{=}}\boldsymbol{\Omega}({\cal F}(c,v+w(c,v)),\hat{v})

and
(
4.20
) follows because

V
and
W
are symplectic orthogonal, cf. (
4.12
). ∎

From now on, the analysis
becomes drastically different from
[
15
]
, and crucially exploits the symmetries of
2d
and
3d
waves.

Phase space decomposition along
2d
and
3d
waves.

For any
j\in\Gamma^{\prime}\setminus\{0\}
we denote by

(4.21)

[j]:=\big\{j^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\prime}\ |\ j^{\prime}\parallel j\big\}

the
1
-dimensional
sub-lattice of
\Gamma^{\prime}
formed by its vectors parallel to
j
. The lattice

[j]
is generated by a vector
\underline{j}
in
[j]

of minimal norm (which may not be
j
)
namely

(4.22)

[j]={\mathbb{Z}}\,\underline{j}\,.

Remark that the dual of
[j]
is the lattice

2\pi\underline{j}|\underline{j}|^{-2}{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Definition 4.5
.

(
2d
and
3d
waves)

For any
j\in\Gamma^{\prime}\setminus\{0\}

we denote

{\mathcal{X}}_{[j]}^{{2d}}:=\overline{\bigoplus_{j^{\prime}\in[j]}V_{j^{\prime}}}^{L^{2}}=\overline{\bigoplus_{j^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\prime},j^{\prime}\parallel j}V_{j^{\prime}}}^{L^{2}}

the subspace of
2d
waves along the direction
[j]
.
A function
u\in L^{2}

which does not belong to any

{\mathcal{X}}_{[j]}^{{2d}}

is referred to as a truly
3d
-wave.

Clearly, if two wave vectors
j_{1},j_{2}\in\Gamma^{\prime}

are parallel, then
[j_{1}]=[j_{2}]
and thus

{\mathcal{X}}_{[j_{1}]}^{{2d}}={\mathcal{X}}_{[j_{2}]}^{{2d}}
.

The functions in
{\mathcal{X}}_{[j]}^{{2d}}
are really
2d
-waves as described by the following lemma.

Lemma 4.6
.

(Shape of
2d
-waves)

A
2d
-wave
u(x)
in
{\mathcal{X}}_{[j]}^{{2d}}
is

2\pi\underline{j}|\underline{j}|^{-2}
-periodic in space where
\underline{j}

is the generator of the
1
-d lattice
[j]
defined in
(
4.22
), i.e.

(4.23)

u(x+2\pi\underline{j}|\underline{j}|^{-2})=u(x)\,,\quad\forall x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\,,

and it is constant along the directions orthogonal to the wave vector
j
, namely

(4.24)

u(x)=u\big((x\cdot\hat{\jmath})\hat{\jmath}\big)\quad\text{where}\quad\hat{\jmath}:=j|j|^{-1}\,,\quad\forall x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\,.

Proof.

Let
u\in{\mathcal{X}}_{[j]}^{{2d}}
.
The periodicity property (
4.23
) follows by the Fourier series expansion

(4.25)

u(x)=\sum_{j^{\prime}\in[j]}\alpha_{j^{\prime}}\,v_{j^{\prime}}^{(1)}(x)+\beta_{j^{\prime}}\,v_{j^{\prime}}^{(2)}(x)\quad\text{with}\ v_{j^{\prime}}^{(1)}\,,v_{j^{\prime}}^{(2)}\ \text{in}\ \eqref{symplectic base}\,,

since

j^{\prime}\cdot\underline{j}|\underline{j}|^{-2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
for any
j^{\prime}\in[j]={\mathbb{Z}}\underline{j}
. We deduce
(
4.24
), by (
4.25
), (
4.1
), decomposing
x=(x\cdot\hat{\jmath})\hat{\jmath}+(x\cdot\hat{\jmath}^{\bot})\hat{\jmath}^{\bot}
and since
j^{\prime}\cdot\hat{\jmath}^{\bot}=0
for
any
j^{\prime}\in[j]
.
∎

A
2d
-wave
u

may be characterized
in terms of the isotropy group

(4.26)

({\mathbb{T}}_{\Gamma}^{2})_{u}:=\Big\{\theta\in{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}\ |\ \tau_{\theta}u=u\Big\}=\bigcap_{j\in A}\big\{\theta\in{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}\ |\ e^{{\rm i}j\cdot\theta}=1\big\}

where
A:=\big\{j\in\Gamma^{\prime}\setminus\{0\}\ |\ \alpha_{j}(u)+{\rm i}\beta_{j}(u)\neq 0\big\}
is the set of “active frequencies” of
u
,
cf. (
4.5
),
and the subgroup

(4.27)

{\mathtt{T}}_{[j]}:=\bigcap_{j^{\prime}\in[j]}\big\{\theta\in{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}\ |\ e^{{\rm i}j^{\prime}\cdot\theta}=1\big\}\,.

Lemma 4.7
.

(Characterization of
2d
-waves)

Any function

u\in L^{2}
is a

2d
-wave along the direction
[j]
, i.e.
u\in{\mathcal{X}}_{[j]}^{{2d}}
, if and only if

(4.28)

\tau_{\theta}u=u\,,\quad\forall\theta\in{\mathtt{T}}_{[j]}\,,\quad\text{i.e.}\quad{\mathtt{T}}_{[j]}\subseteq({\mathbb{T}}_{\Gamma}^{2})_{u}\,.

Proof.

We Fourier decompose

u=\sum_{j^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\prime}}\alpha_{j^{\prime}}(u)\,v_{j^{\prime}}^{(1)}+\beta_{j^{\prime}}(u)\,v_{j^{\prime}}^{(2)}

as in
(
4.4
).
By (
4.7
)
we have

\alpha_{j^{\prime}}(u-\tau_{\theta}u)+{\rm i}\beta_{j^{\prime}}(u-\tau_{\theta}u)=\big(\alpha_{j^{\prime}}(u)+{\rm i}\,\beta_{j^{\prime}}(u)\big)\big(1-e^{-{\rm i}\,{j^{\prime}}\cdot\theta}\big)\,.

Thus
\tau_{\theta}u=u
for any
\theta\in{\mathtt{T}}_{[j]}
is equivalent to

\alpha_{j^{\prime}}(u)+{\rm i}\,\beta_{j^{\prime}}(u)=0

for any
j^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\prime}
,
j^{\prime}\not\in[j]
, namely
j^{\prime}\not\parallel j
.
Recalling
Definition
4.5
, this means that

u\in{\mathcal{X}}_{[j]}^{{2d}}
.
∎

Decomposition of
V
along resonant wave directions.

We introduce on the kernel
V
in (
4.9
) the scalar product

(4.29)

\langle v,v_{1}\rangle=\mathrm{Re}\Big\{\sum_{j\in{\mathcal{V}}}|j|\,z_{j}(v)\overline{z_{j}(v_{1})}\Big\}\,,\qquad\|v\|^{2}:=\sum_{j\in{\mathcal{V}}}|j|\,|z_{j}(v)|^{2}\,,

where
z_{j}(v)=\alpha_{j}(v)+{\rm i}\beta_{j}(v)
. By (
4.7
)
it is
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-invariant, i.e.

\langle\tau_{\theta}v,\tau_{\theta}v_{1}\rangle=\langle v,v_{1}\rangle\,,\quad\forall\theta\in{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}\,,\quad\langle\rho v,\rho v_{1}\rangle=\langle v,v_{1}\rangle\,.

In the sequel all the gradients in the variable
v
are taken with respect to this scalar product.
The spaces
\{V_{j}\}_{j\in{\mathcal{V}}}
are pairwise orthogonal with
respect to the scalar product (
4.29
) and we denote
\pi_{j}:V\to V_{j}
the orthogonal projectors associated to the decomposition (
4.9
).

We also consider the orthogonal decomposition of
V
,

V=\bigoplus_{\hat{\jmath}\in{\mathcal{D}}}V_{\hat{\jmath}}\,,

where
{\mathcal{D}}
is the set of
resonant wave directions

(4.30)

{\mathcal{D}}:=\Big\{\hat{\jmath}\in S^{1}\ |\ j\in{\mathcal{V}}\Big\}\,,\quad\hat{\jmath}=j|j|^{-1}\,,

and

(4.31)

V_{\hat{\jmath}}:=V\cap{\mathcal{X}}_{[j]}^{{2d}}=\bigoplus_{j^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{V}}\cap[j]}V_{j^{\prime}}

is
the subspace of
V
formed by the
2d
-waves along
[j]
.
For any resonant wave direction
{\hat{\jmath}}\in{\mathcal{D}}
, we denote the corresponding orthogonal projectors

(4.32)

\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}:V\to V_{\hat{\jmath}}\,,\ \Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}=\sum_{j^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{V}}\cap[j]}\pi_{j}\,,\quad\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}^{\bot}:=\text{Id}-\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}=\sum_{\hat{\jmath}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{D}}\setminus\hat{\jmath}}\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}^{\prime}}\,.

For any
v\in V
it results

\|v\|^{2}=\sum_{\hat{\jmath}\in{\mathcal{D}}}\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}v\|^{2}
. We denote

(4.33)

{\bf V}^{{2d}}:=\bigcup_{\hat{\jmath}\in{\mathcal{D}}}V_{\hat{\jmath}}\,,\qquad V_{\hat{\jmath}}=\big\{v\in V\ |\ \Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}^{\bot}v=0\big\}\,,

the union of subspaces in
V
formed by
2d
-waves.

Remark 4.8
.

(Resonant wave directions)

Since the set (
1.24
) of resonant wave vectors
{\mathcal{V}}
is contained in the half-plane (
1.27
), there is a
1-1
correspondence between the resonant wave directions
{\mathcal{D}}=\{\hat{\jmath}\ |\ j\in{\mathcal{V}}\}
and the lattices
\{[j]\ |\ j\in{\mathcal{V}}\}
in (
4.21
).
Furthermore, in view of Lemma
4.2
, for each resonant wave direction
\hat{\jmath}
, the set
[j]\cap{\mathcal{V}}
consists of

1
vector or
2
collinear vectors, and

V_{\hat{\jmath}}

is respectively
V_{j}

or has
dimension
4
.

Remark 4.9
.

By Lemma
4.7

an equivariant function
f:V\to V

maps each subspace
V_{\hat{\jmath}}\to V_{\hat{\jmath}}
, for any

\hat{\jmath}\in{\mathcal{D}}
, and
V\setminus{\bf V}^{{2d}}\to V\setminus{\bf V}^{{2d}}
.

In view of (
4.8
) and (
4.29
) the momentum is

(4.34)

{\mathcal{I}}(v)=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j\in{\mathcal{V}}}\hat{\jmath}\|\pi_{j}v\|^{2}=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\hat{\jmath}\in{\mathcal{D}}}\hat{\jmath}\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}v\|^{2}

where
\hat{\jmath}=j|j|^{-1}=(\hat{\jmath}_{1},\hat{\jmath}_{2})^{\top}
and therefore

(4.35)

\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}(v):=\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}_{1}(v)\\
\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}_{2}(v)\end{pmatrix}=-\sum_{\hat{\jmath}\in{\mathcal{D}}}\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\jmath}_{1}\\
\hat{\jmath}_{2}\end{pmatrix}\,\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}v\,,\quad\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}(v)=-\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\jmath}_{1}\\
\hat{\jmath}_{2}\end{pmatrix}\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}v\,,\quad\forall\hat{\jmath}\in{\mathcal{D}}\,.

Note that
\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}(v)
commutes with the projectors
\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}
, i.e.
\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}(v)=\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}(\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}v)
.

The following notation will be used throughout the paper.

Definition 4.10
.

(Parallel and orthogonal components to
\hat{\jmath}
)

For any
resonant wave direction
\hat{\jmath}\in{\mathcal{D}}
, cf. (
4.30
),
we decompose any vector
y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}

as

(4.36)

y=y^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}\hat{\jmath}+y^{\bot_{\hat{\jmath}}}\hat{\jmath}^{\bot}\,,\quad y^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}:=y\cdot\hat{\jmath}\,,\quad y^{\bot_{\hat{\jmath}}}:=y\cdot\hat{\jmath}^{\bot}\,,\ \hat{\jmath}=\begin{pmatrix}j_{1}\\
j_{2}\end{pmatrix}|j|^{-1}\,,\ \hat{\jmath}^{\bot}=\begin{pmatrix}-j_{2}\\
j_{1}\end{pmatrix}|j|^{-1}.

We set

(4.37)

y^{\hat{\jmath}}:=\begin{pmatrix}y^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}\\
y^{\bot_{\hat{\jmath}}}\end{pmatrix}=Q_{\hat{\jmath}}y\qquad\text{where}\qquad Q_{\hat{\jmath}}:=[\hat{\jmath}\ |\ \hat{\jmath}^{\bot}]\,.

Note that
Q_{\hat{\jmath}}
is an orthogonal involution,

Q_{\hat{\jmath}}^{\top}=Q_{\hat{\jmath}}=Q_{\hat{\jmath}}^{-1}
, in particular
\det Q_{\hat{\jmath}}=1
.

By Lemma
4.6
, any function
u(x)
in
{\mathcal{X}}^{{2d}}_{[j]}
is constant along the direction
\hat{\jmath}^{\bot}
and hence the Hamiltonian vector field
generated by
{\mathcal{I}}^{\bot_{\hat{\jmath}}}(u)
,

(4.38)

X_{{\mathcal{I}}^{\bot_{\hat{\jmath}}}}(u)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{XI}}}{{=}}(\hat{\jmath}^{\bot}\cdot\partial_{x})u=0\,,\quad\forall u\in{\mathcal{X}}^{{2d}}_{[j]}\,,

vanishes on the
2d
-waves along the direction
[j]
.
By (
4.8
) and
(
4.38
)
we also deduce

(4.39)

{\mathcal{I}}^{\bot_{\hat{\jmath}}}(u)=0\,,\quad\forall u\in{\mathcal{X}}^{{2d}}_{[j]}\,.

Note that, for any
\hat{\jmath}\in{\mathcal{D}}
,

(4.40)

\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}^{\bot_{\hat{\jmath}}}(v)=0\,,\ \forall v\in V_{\hat{\jmath}}\,,\qquad\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}^{\bot_{\hat{\jmath}}}(v)=0\,,\ \forall v\in V\,,

because
{\rm d}_{v}{\mathcal{I}}^{\bot_{\hat{\jmath}}}(v)[\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}v_{1}]={\rm d}_{v}{\mathcal{I}}^{\bot_{\hat{\jmath}}}(\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}v_{1})[v]={\bf\Omega}(X_{{\mathcal{I}}^{\bot_{\hat{\jmath}}}}(\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}v_{1}),v)=0
by (
4.38
)
(or use (
4.35
)).

Lemma 4.11
.

(
2d
-wave symmetry)

For any
{\hat{\jmath}}\in{\mathcal{D}}
,
any
c\in B_{r}(c_{*})
and
v\in B_{r}^{V}\cap V_{\hat{\jmath}}
,
the solution
w(c,v)
of the range equation constructed in Lemma

4.3

satisfies

(i)

w(c,v)\in{\mathcal{X}}_{[j]}^{{2d}}\cap X

is a
2d
-wave along the direction
{\hat{\jmath}}
;

(ii)

w(c,v)
is independent on the component
c^{\bot_{\hat{\jmath}}}
of
c
orthogonal to
j
, cf. (
4.36
).

Proof.

Item
(i)
follows
by the equivariance property
(
4.15
) of
w(c,\cdot)
and Lemma
4.7
. By (
1.15
) and
(
4.38
) we have

(4.41)

\mathcal{F}(c,u)=X_{{\mathcal{H}}+c\cdot{\mathcal{I}}}(u)=X_{{\mathcal{H}}+c^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}{\mathcal{I}}^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}}=\mathcal{F}((c\cdot\hat{\jmath})\hat{\jmath},u)\,,\quad\forall u\in{\mathcal{X}}_{[j]}^{{2d}}\,,

where
{\mathcal{I}}^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}(v)
is the parallel components of
{\mathcal{I}}(v)
according to the decomposition (
4.36
).
If
v\in V_{\hat{\jmath}}\cap B_{r}^{V}

then
v+w(c,v)\in{\mathcal{X}}_{[j]}^{{2d}}
by item (
i
)
and therefore by (
4.41
) and (
4.13
) we obtain

\Pi_{W\cap Y}\mathcal{F}((c\cdot\hat{\jmath})\hat{\jmath},v+w(c,v))=\Pi_{W\cap Y}\mathcal{F}(c,v+w(c,v))=0\,.

The uniqueness of the solution of the range equation implies that

w(c,v)=w((c\cdot\hat{\jmath})\hat{\jmath},v)
.
∎

Proposition 4.12
.

(Properties of
\Phi(c,v)
)

For any
(c,v)\in B_{r}(c_{*})\times B_{r}^{V}
,

\bullet
the reduced Hamiltonian
\Phi(c,v)
in (
4.18
) has the form

(4.42)

\Phi(c,v)=(c-c_{*})\cdot{\mathcal{I}}(v)+G_{\geq 3}(c,v)

where

G_{\geq 3}(c,v)
is an analytic
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}-
invariant function
satisfying

(4.43)

{\rm d}_{v}^{\ell}G_{\geq 3}(c,0)=0\,,\quad\forall\ell=0,1,2\,,\quad\forall c\in B_{r}(c_{*})\,.

\bullet
For any
{\hat{\jmath}}\in{\mathcal{D}}
,
for any
c\in B_{r}(c_{*})
,
any
v\in B_{r}^{V}\cap V_{\hat{\jmath}}

the reduced Hamiltonian

(4.44)

\Phi(c,v)=\Phi(c^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}\hat{\jmath}+c^{\bot_{\hat{\jmath}}}_{*}\hat{\jmath}^{\bot},v)=(c-c_{*})^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}{\mathcal{I}}^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}(v)+G_{\geq 3}(c^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}\hat{\jmath}+c^{\bot_{\hat{\jmath}}}_{*}\hat{\jmath}^{\bot},v)\,,

is independent of
c^{\bot_{\hat{\jmath}}}
,
and any critical point

v
of

\Phi(c,\cdot):V_{\hat{\jmath}}\cap B_{r}^{V}\to{\mathbb{R}}
gives rise to a
2d
Stokes wave
v+w(c,v)
in
{\mathcal{X}}^{{2d}}_{[j]}\cap X
.

\bullet
The
reduced momentum

(4.45)

{\mathtt{I}}:B_{r}(c_{*})\times B_{r}^{V}\to{\mathbb{R}}\,,\quad{\mathtt{I}}(c,v):={\mathcal{I}}(v+w(c,v))\,,

is analytic,
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-invariant and

(4.46)

{\mathtt{I}}(c,v)=\partial_{c}\Phi(c,v)\,,\quad\forall c\in B_{r}(c_{*})\,,\ v\in B_{r}^{V}\,.

Proof.

In view of
(
4.18
),
the expansion (
4.42
) holds with

G_{\geq 3}(c,v)=c\cdot{\mathcal{I}}(v+w(c,v))+{\mathcal{H}}(v+w(c,v))-(c-c_{*})\cdot{\mathcal{I}}(v)\,.

Let us prove (
4.43
). By
(
4.14
)
we have
G_{\geq 3}(c,0)=0
and
{\rm d}_{v}G_{\geq 3}(c,0)=0
for any
c\in B_{r}(c_{*})
and

(4.47)

{\rm d}_{v}^{2}G_{\geq 3}(c,0)[\hat{v}]^{2}={\rm d}_{v}^{2}\big(c_{*}\cdot{\mathcal{I}}+{\mathcal{H}}\big)_{|v=0}[\hat{v}]^{2}=\boldsymbol{\Omega}({\rm d}_{v}X_{c_{*}\cdot{\mathcal{I}}+{\mathcal{H}}}(0)[\hat{v}],\hat{v})\quad\forall\hat{v}\in V\,.

Since
V=\ker({\mathcal{L}}_{c_{*}})=\ker\big({\rm d}_{v}X_{c_{*}\cdot{\mathcal{I}}+{\mathcal{H}}}(0)\big)
we also deduce
{\rm d}_{v}^{2}G_{\geq 3}(c,0)=0
.
Using (
4.39
) and Lemma
4.11
-
(ii)
, by (
4.42
) we deduce (
4.44
).
In view of Lemma
A.1
we have that any critical point of
\Phi(c,\cdot)_{|V_{\hat{\jmath}}}
is a critical point of
\Phi(c,\cdot)
and thus gives rise to a
2d
-Stokes waves solution.
Finally, differentiating (
4.18
)
we get

\partial_{c}\Phi(c,v)=(\partial_{c}\Psi)(c,v+w(c,v))+\underbrace{({\rm d}_{u}\Psi)(c,v+w(c,v))[\partial_{c}w(c,v)]}_{=0\ \text{by}\,\eqref{Variational Range},\ \partial_{c}w(c,v)\in W}={\cal I}(v+w(c,v))={\mathtt{I}}(c,v)

proving (
4.46
).
∎

2d
-Stokes waves.

In view of Proposition
4.12
any critical point of
\Phi(c,\cdot)_{|V_{\hat{\jmath}}}
is a critical point also for
\Phi(c,\cdot)
.
The
classical
2d
-theory is then recovered by restricting to
V_{\hat{\jmath}}
:

\bullet

(
2d
Stokes waves: non-resonant case)

If
V_{\hat{\jmath}}=V_{j}
is
2
dimensional, any
2d
-Stokes wave solution in
{\mathcal{X}}_{j}^{2d}
is, up to translation of
\vartheta\in S^{1}
, an analytic curve of functions

(4.48)

u(\varepsilon)=\varepsilon v_{j}^{(1)}+w(c^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}_{*}(\varepsilon)\hat{\jmath}+c_{*}^{\bot_{\hat{\jmath}}}\hat{\jmath}^{\bot},\varepsilon v_{j}^{(1)})

with observed speed

c_{*}^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}(\varepsilon)\hat{\jmath}=c_{*}\cdot{\hat{\jmath}}+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{2})

(see e.g.
[
4
]
[page 16] or (
4.52
))
and momentum

{\mathcal{I}}(u(\varepsilon))=-\tfrac{1}{2}j\varepsilon^{2}+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{3})
(cf. (
4.8
)).

\bullet

(
2d
Stokes waves: resonant)

In this case

V_{\hat{\jmath}}
is
4
-dimensional.
This is the resonant bifurcation
problem of
2d
-Stokes waves fully described
in
[
4
]
[Thm. 1.3, 1.4].

3d
Stokes waves. Non-resonant case.

Suppose

(4.49)

\ker({\mathcal{L}}_{c_{*}})=V=V_{j_{*}}\oplus V_{j}=\Big\{v=\alpha_{j_{*}}v_{j_{*}}^{(1)}+\beta_{j_{*}}v_{j_{*}}^{(2)}+\alpha_{j}v_{j}^{(1)}+\beta_{j}v_{j}^{(2)}\colon\alpha_{j_{*}},\alpha_{j},\beta_{j*},\beta_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}\Big\}

where

j,j_{*}\in\Gamma^{\prime}
satisfy

\omega(j_{*})=c_{*}\cdot j_{*}

and

\omega(j)=c_{*}\cdot j
and

j\not\parallel j_{*}
.
The symmetries (
4.19
) show,
recalling
(
4.7
), that
(for simplicity we denote

\alpha_{*}=\alpha_{j_{*}}

and
\beta_{*}=\beta_{j_{*}}
)

(4.50)

\displaystyle\Phi(c,\alpha_{*},\beta_{*},\alpha_{j},\beta_{j})=\Phi\Big(c,R(-j_{*}\cdot\theta)(\alpha_{*},\beta_{*}),R(-j\cdot\theta)(\alpha_{j},\beta_{j})\Big)\,,\ \forall\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\,,

\displaystyle\Phi(c,\alpha_{*},\beta_{*},\alpha_{j},\beta_{j})=\Phi(c,\alpha_{*},-\beta_{*},\alpha_{j},-\beta_{j})\,.

where
R(\cdot)
is the rotation matrix

R(\varphi):=\begin{pmatrix}\cos(\varphi)&-\sin(\varphi)\\
\sin(\varphi)&\cos(\varphi)\end{pmatrix}
.
Here we denoted

\Phi(c,v)
equivalently as

\Phi(c,(\alpha_{j},\beta_{j})_{j\in{\cal V}})
.
Since
j,j_{*}
are independent, the linear map

{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
,
\theta\mapsto(j_{*}\cdot\theta,j\cdot\theta)

is invertible, and therefore

\Phi(c,\alpha_{*},\beta_{*},\alpha_{j},\beta_{j})

is a function of

\alpha_{*}^{2}+\beta_{*}^{2}
and

\alpha_{j}^{2}+\beta_{j}^{2}
only,
for any
c
. Thus all the
critical points of
\Phi(c,\alpha_{*},\beta_{*},\alpha_{j},\beta_{j})

are
obtained by rotations of
critical points of the function

(\alpha_{*},\alpha_{j})\mapsto\Phi(c,\alpha_{*},0,\alpha_{j},0)

of two variables only.
By Proposition
4.12
, (
4.8
)
and the symmetry (
4.50
),
this function has the form

(4.51)

\Phi(c,\alpha_{*},0,\alpha_{j},0)=-\frac{1}{2}(c-c_{*})\cdot j_{*}\,\alpha_{*}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}(c-c_{*})\cdot j\,\alpha_{j}^{2}+\underbrace{G_{\geq 3}(c,\alpha_{*},0,\alpha_{j},0)}_{=:G(c,\alpha_{*}^{2},\alpha_{j}^{2})}\,,

where

(c,y_{1},y_{2})\mapsto G(c,y_{1},y_{2})

is an analytic function
satisfying
G(c,0,0)=0
,

\partial_{y_{1}}G(c,0,0)=\partial_{y_{2}}G(c,0,0)=0
for any
c\in B_{r}(c_{*})
.
We have to find zeros of

\displaystyle-(\partial_{\alpha_{*}}\Phi)(c,\alpha_{*},0,\alpha_{j},0)=(c-c_{*})\cdot j_{*}\,\alpha_{*}-2(\partial_{y_{1}}G)(c,\alpha_{*}^{2},\alpha_{j}^{2})\alpha_{*}=0

\displaystyle-(\partial_{\alpha_{j}}\Phi)(c,\alpha_{*},0,\alpha_{j},0)=(c-c_{*})\cdot j\,\alpha_{j}-2(\partial_{y_{2}}G)(c,\alpha_{*}^{2},\alpha_{j}^{2})\alpha_{j}=0

or equivalently, dividing the first equation by

\alpha_{*}\neq 0
and the second one by
\alpha_{j}\neq 0
,

(4.52)

(c-c_{*})\cdot j_{*}-2(\partial_{y_{1}}G)(c,\alpha_{*}^{2},\alpha_{j}^{2})=0\,,\quad(c-c_{*})\cdot j-2(\partial_{y_{2}}G)(c,\alpha_{*}^{2},\alpha_{j}^{2})=0\,.

Since
j_{*},j
are independent,
by the implicit function theorem
there exists
a unique analytic solution

c(\alpha_{*}^{2},\alpha_{j}^{2})
of (
4.52
), defined for any

(\alpha_{*},\alpha_{j})
in a small neighborhood of

(0,0)
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, satisfying

c(0,0)=c_{*}
. If
\alpha_{*}\alpha_{j}\neq 0

these waves are truly
3d
.
If
\alpha_{j}\alpha_{*}=0
they are
2d
.

This result improves
[
15
]
[Theorem 4.3] since it holds in a full neighborhood of
0
,
namely also near
\alpha_{*}\alpha_{j}=0
.

In the
resonant
cases
when

\dim\ker{{\mathcal{L}}_{c_{*}}}\geq 6
the choice of the speed vector
c
(which a
2
dimensional vector) does not determine uniquely a solution anymore.
In the next section
5
we choose properly
c:=c(v)

as a function of
v
. Then in Section
8
variational/topological arguments will determine the existence of suitable
v
’s from which non-zero Stokes wave bifurcate.

5
Construction of
c(v)

The main result of this section is to construct a speed

c(v)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, close to

c_{*}
, solving
the system

(5.1)

{\rm d}_{v}\Phi(c,v)[\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}(v)]=\begin{pmatrix}{\rm d}_{v}\Phi(c,v)[\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}_{1}(v)]\\
{\rm d}_{v}\Phi(c,v)[\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}_{2}(v)]\end{pmatrix}=0\,,

for
any

v
in a
small neighborhood
B_{r^{\prime}}^{V}

of the origin,
where
\Phi(c,v)
is the reduced Hamiltonian in (
4.18
) and
\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}(v)
is defined in (
4.35
).

The construction of
c(v)
if very different if

v
is “far” from
{\bf V}^{2d}
(this is the case addressed in
[
15
]
) or
“near”
{\bf V}^{2d}
.
The difficulty is that as
v

approaches
{\bf V}^{{2d}}
the construction
becomes singular as the bifurcation problem tends to the
2d
one, and
it is necessary to exploit the
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}
-symmetry of the problem.
In order to state precisely the result we define the region

(5.2)

{\cal F}_{r^{\prime}}:=\Big\{v\in B_{r^{\prime}}^{V}\setminus\{0\}\ |\ d(v,{\bf V}^{{2d}})\geq\sqrt{\delta/4}\,\|v\|\Big\}\,,\quad d(v,{\bf V}^{{2d}}):=\min_{{\hat{\jmath}}\in{\mathcal{D}}}\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}^{\bot}v\|\,,

and

(5.3)

{\mathcal{N}}_{r^{\prime}}:=\bigcup_{\hat{\jmath}\in{\mathcal{D}}}{\mathcal{N}}_{\hat{\jmath}}(r^{\prime})\,,\quad{\cal N}_{\hat{\jmath}}(r^{\prime}):=\Big\{v\in B_{r^{\prime}}^{V}\ |\ \|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}^{\bot}v\|\leq\sqrt{\delta/4}\,\|v\|\Big\}\,.

In (
5.2
)
and (
5.3
) we choose

(5.4)

\delta:=\min\Big\{(\hat{\jmath}^{\prime}\cdot\hat{\jmath}^{\bot})^{2}\ |\ j\not\parallel j^{\prime}\Big\}\,.

Note that
\delta\in(0,1]
.
The role of the constant
\delta
enters in Lemma
5.6
.

Proposition 5.1
.

(Construction of
c(v)
)

There exist
r^{\prime}\in(0,r)
and a
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-invariant function

c:B_{r^{\prime}}^{V}\to B_{r}(c_{*})

solving system (
5.1
), satisfying

(5.5)

|c(v)-c_{*}|\lesssim\|v\|\,,\quad\forall v\in B_{r^{\prime}}^{V}\,,

and the following properties:

•

(Solution outside
{\bf V}^{2d}
)
The function

c:B_{r^{\prime}}^{V}\setminus{\bf V}^{{2d}}\to B_{r}(c_{*})
is analytic and it is the unique solution of (
5.1
).
Moreover

(5.6)

\|{\rm d}_{v}c(v)[\hat{v}]\|\lesssim\|\hat{v}\|\,,\quad\forall v\in{\mathcal{F}}_{r^{\prime}}\,,\quad\forall\hat{v}\in V\,,

and for any
v\in{\mathcal{N}}_{\hat{\jmath}}(r^{\prime})\setminus V_{\hat{\jmath}}
,
\hat{\jmath}\in{\mathcal{D}}
, the parallel and orthogonal components
c^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}(v)=c(v)\cdot\hat{\jmath}
and
c^{\bot_{\hat{\jmath}}}(v)=c(v)\cdot\hat{\jmath}^{\bot}

satisfy

(5.7)

|{\rm d}_{v}c^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}(v)[\hat{v}]|\lesssim\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}\hat{v}\|+\frac{\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}^{\bot}v\|}{\|v\|}\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}^{\bot}\hat{v}\|\,,\quad|{\rm d}_{v}c^{\bot_{\hat{\jmath}}}(v)[\hat{v}]|\lesssim\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}\hat{v}\|+\frac{\|v\|}{\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}^{\bot}v\|}\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}^{\bot}\hat{v}\|\,.

•

(Solution on
{\bf V}^{2d}
)

for any

\hat{\jmath}\in{\mathcal{D}}
the function

c:(V_{\hat{\jmath}}\cap B_{r^{\prime}}^{V})\setminus\{0\}\to{\mathbb{R}}

is analytic and
any other solution of (
5.1
) is of the kind
c(v)+k\hat{\jmath}^{\bot}
for
k\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

•

(Regularity of
c^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}(v)
)

for any
\hat{\jmath}\in{\mathcal{D}}

the parallel component
c^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}(v)
is of class

(5.8)

C(B_{r^{\prime}}^{\hat{\jmath}})\cap C^{1}(B_{r^{\prime}}^{\hat{\jmath}}\setminus\{0\})\quad\text{where}\quad B_{r^{\prime}}^{\hat{\jmath}}:=(B_{r^{\prime}}^{V}\setminus{\bf V}^{{2d}})\cup(B_{r^{\prime}}^{V}\cap V_{\hat{\jmath}}).

Proposition
5.1
results from the construction of
c_{{3d}}(v)
far from
{\bf V}^{{2d}}

(Proposition
5.2
), near
{\bf V}^{{2d}}
(Proposition
5.5
) and on
{\bf V}^{2d}
(Lemma
5.39
).

5.1
Construction of
c(v)
far away
from
{\bf V}^{{2d}}

In this section we prove the
following result.

Proposition 5.2
.

(Speed
c_{{3d}}(v)
in
{\mathcal{F}}_{r^{\prime}}
)

There exists
r^{\prime}\in(0,r)
and a
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-invariant analytic function

c_{{3d}}:{\mathcal{F}}_{r^{\prime}}\to B_{r}(c_{*})

which is the unique solution of (
5.1
),
satisfying (
5.5
) in
{\mathcal{F}}_{r^{\prime}}
and (
5.6
).

We now prove Proposition
5.2
.
In view of
(
4.42
),
system (
5.1
) is equivalent to

(5.9)

{\mathcal{A}}(v)(c-c_{*})+{\mathtt{G}}_{\geq 3}(c,v)=0

where
{\mathcal{A}}(v)
is the
symmetric matrix

(5.10)

{\mathcal{A}}(v):=\begin{pmatrix}{\mathcal{A}}_{11}(v)&{\mathcal{A}}_{12}(v)\\
{\mathcal{A}}_{12}(v)&{\mathcal{A}}_{22}(v)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\langle\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}_{1}(v),\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}_{1}(v)\rangle&\langle\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}_{2}(v),\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}_{1}(v)\rangle\\
\langle\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}_{1}(v),\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}_{2}(v)\rangle&\langle\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}_{2}(v),\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}_{2}(v)\rangle\end{pmatrix}

and

{\mathtt{G}}_{\geq 3}(c,v):=\begin{pmatrix}{\rm d}_{v}G_{\geq 3}(c,v)[\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}_{1}(v)]\\
{\rm d}_{v}G_{\geq 3}(c,v)[\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}_{2}(v)]\end{pmatrix}\,.

The function

{\mathtt{G}}_{\geq 3}:B_{r}(c_{*})\times B_{r}^{V}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
is analytic and satisfies

(5.11)

{\rm d}_{v}^{\ell}{\mathtt{G}}_{\geq 3}(c,0)=0\,,\quad\forall\ell=0,1,2\,,\quad\forall c\in B_{r}(c_{*})\,.

Lemma 5.3
.

The matrix
{\mathcal{A}}(v)
in (
5.10
) is equal to

(5.12)

{\mathcal{A}}(v)=\sum_{j\in{\mathcal{V}}}\|\pi_{j}v\|^{2}\,\hat{\jmath}\otimes\hat{\jmath}=\sum_{{\hat{\jmath}}\in{\mathcal{D}}}\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}v\|^{2}\,\hat{\jmath}\otimes\hat{\jmath}\,,\quad\forall v\in V\,,

where
\hat{\jmath}\otimes\hat{\jmath}
is the symmetric, rank
1
, positive semi-definite matrix
\hat{\jmath}\otimes\hat{\jmath}:=\hat{\jmath}\hat{\jmath}^{\top}
.
For any
v\in V

(5.13)

\det({\mathcal{A}}(v))=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\hat{\jmath},\hat{\jmath}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{D}}}\det([\hat{\jmath}|\hat{\jmath}^{\prime}])^{2}\,\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}v\|^{2}\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}^{\prime}}v\|^{2}

where
[\hat{\jmath}|\hat{\jmath}^{\prime}]
is the matrix with columns
\hat{\jmath}
and
\hat{\jmath}^{\prime}
.
The matrix
{\mathcal{A}}(v)
is singular on the set

(5.14)

\Big\{v\in V\ |\ \det({\mathcal{A}}(v))=0\Big\}={\bf V}^{{2d}}=\bigcup_{{\hat{\jmath}}\in{\mathcal{D}}}V_{\hat{\jmath}}\,,

more precisely

\det({\mathcal{A}}(v))>0
on

d(v,{\bf V}^{{2d}})>0
.

Proof.

Since the projectors
\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}
are orthogonal, using (
4.35
), we deduce (
5.12
).
The determinant is multilinear and skew-symmetric in the columns and the rows, so writing

\hat{\jmath}=(\hat{\jmath}_{1},\hat{\jmath}_{2})^{\top}
, we get

\displaystyle\det({\mathcal{A}}(v))

\displaystyle=\det\Big[\sum_{\hat{\jmath}\in{\mathcal{D}}}\hat{\jmath}_{1}\hat{\jmath}\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}v\|^{2}\Big|\sum_{\hat{\jmath}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{D}}}\hat{\jmath}_{2}^{\prime}\hat{\jmath}^{\prime}\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}v\|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\hat{\jmath},\hat{\jmath}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{D}}}

\displaystyle\hat{\jmath}_{1}\hat{\jmath}_{2}^{\prime}\det[\hat{\jmath}|\hat{\jmath}^{\prime}]\,\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}v\|^{2}\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}^{\prime}}v\|^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\hat{\jmath},\hat{\jmath}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{D}}}\big(\hat{\jmath}_{1}\hat{\jmath}_{2}^{\prime}\det[\hat{\jmath}|\hat{\jmath}^{\prime}]+\hat{\jmath}_{1}^{\prime}\hat{\jmath}_{2}\det[\hat{\jmath}^{\prime}|\hat{\jmath}]\big)\,\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}v\|^{2}\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}^{\prime}}v\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\hat{\jmath},\hat{\jmath}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{D}}}\det([\hat{\jmath}|\hat{\jmath}^{\prime}])^{2}\,\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}v\|^{2}\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}^{\prime}}v\|^{2}

proving (
5.13
).
Therefore
\det({\mathcal{A}}(v))>0
if and only if there exists two distinct resonant wave directions
\hat{\jmath},\hat{\jmath}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{D}}
such that
\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}v\|\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}^{\prime}}v\|>0
. This proves
(
5.14
), recalling (
4.31
), (
4.33
).
∎

If
v\in V
satisfies
d(v,{\bf V}^{{2d}})\geq\sqrt{\delta/4}\|v\|
, cf. (
5.2
),
there exist two distinct
\hat{\jmath},\hat{\jmath}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{D}}
such that

\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}v\|^{2}\geq\tfrac{\delta/4}{\#{\mathcal{D}}-1}\|v\|^{2}
and
\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}^{\prime}}v\|^{2}\geq\tfrac{\delta/4}{\#{\mathcal{D}}-1}\|v\|^{2}
. Therefore the determinant
(
5.13
)
satisfies

(5.15)

\det({\mathcal{A}}(v))\gtrsim\|v\|^{4}

and, using also (
5.12
),

(5.16)

{\mathcal{A}}(v)^{-1}=\frac{1}{\det({\mathcal{A}}(v))}\left(\begin{matrix}{\mathcal{A}}_{22}(v)&-{\mathcal{A}}_{12}(v)\\
-{\mathcal{A}}_{12}(v)&{\mathcal{A}}_{11}(v)\end{matrix}\right)\lesssim\frac{1}{\|v\|^{2}}\,,\quad\forall v\in{\mathcal{F}}_{r^{\prime}}\,,

meaning that each component is bounded by
\lesssim\tfrac{1}{\|v\|^{2}}
(actually the matrix
{\mathcal{A}}(v)
in
(
5.12
) has
an explicit inverse,
that however we shall not use).

System (
5.9
) is equivalent,
for any
v\in B_{r}^{V}\setminus{\bf V}^{{2d}}
, to find a fixed point of

(5.17)

c=c_{*}-{\mathcal{A}}(v)^{-1}{\mathtt{G}}_{\geq 3}(c,v)=:{\mathcal{G}}(c,v)\,.

Lemma 5.4
.

There exists

r^{\prime}\in(0,r)
such that for any

v\in{\mathcal{F}}_{r^{\prime}}
,
the map
{\mathcal{G}}(\cdot,v)
is a contraction in the ball

B_{r}(c_{*})
.
The unique fixed point solution
c_{{3d}}(v)
of (
5.17
)
in
B_{r}(c_{*})
is analytic in
v
and
satisfies (
5.5
) in
{\mathcal{F}}_{r^{\prime}}
and (
5.6
).

Proof.

By (
5.11
), it results
{\mathtt{G}}_{\geq 3}(c,v)={\mathcal{O}}(\|v\|^{3})
, uniformly in
c
, and, using also (
5.16
),

(5.18)

{\mathcal{G}}(c,v)=c_{*}+{\mathcal{O}}(\|v\|)\,,\quad\partial_{c}{\mathcal{G}}(c,v)={\mathcal{O}}(\|v\|)\,.

By (
5.18
) we deduce that if
r^{\prime}
is sufficiently small, for any
v\in B_{r^{\prime}}^{V}
, the map
{\mathcal{G}}(\cdot,v)
is a contraction
in
B_{r}(c_{*})
.
The unique solution
c_{{3d}}(v)
of
c_{{3d}}(v)={\mathcal{G}}(c_{{3d}}(v),v)
satisfies (
5.5
) and is analytic
in
v
since
{\mathcal{G}}
is analytic.
Differentiating the equation
{\mathcal{A}}(v)(c_{{3d}}(v)-c_{*})+{\mathtt{G}}_{\geq 3}(c_{{3d}}(v),v)=0
we get

{\rm d}_{v}{\mathcal{A}}(v)[\hat{v}](c_{{3d}}(v)-c_{*})+{\mathcal{A}}(v){\rm d}_{v}c_{{3d}}(v)[\hat{v}]=-{\rm d}_{v}{\mathtt{G}}_{\geq 3}(c_{{3d}}(v),v)[\hat{v}]-\partial_{c}{\mathtt{G}}_{\geq 3}(c_{{3d}}(v),v){\rm d}_{v}c_{{3d}}(v)[\hat{v}]\,.

Since
{\rm d}_{v}{\mathcal{A}}(v)[\hat{v}]={\mathcal{O}}(\|v\|\|\hat{v}\|)
,
{\mathtt{G}}_{\geq 3}(c,v)={\mathcal{O}}(\|v\|^{3})
and
c_{{3d}}(v)-c_{*}={\mathcal{O}}(\|v\|)
we
deduce that

{\rm d}_{v}c_{{3d}}(v)[\hat{v}]=({\mathcal{A}}(v)+\partial_{c}{\mathtt{G}}_{\geq 3}(c_{{3d}}(v),v)\big)^{-1}{\mathcal{O}}(\|v\|^{2}\|\hat{v}\|)={\mathcal{O}}(\|\hat{v}\|)

which is (
5.6
).
∎

5.2
Construction of
c(v)
near
{\bf V}^{{2d}}

We now construct the
solution
c_{{3d}}(v)
of system (
5.1
)
in the region
{\mathcal{N}}_{r^{\prime}}\setminus{\bf V}^{2d}
(cf. (
5.3
))
for some
r^{\prime}\in(0,r)
eventually smaller than in Proposition
5.2
.
The main difficulty is that the matrix

{\mathcal{A}}(v)

becomes singular when
v
approaches
some subspace
V_{\hat{\jmath}}

of
2d
-waves.

Proposition 5.5
.

(Speed
c_{{3d}}(v)

in
{\mathcal{N}}_{r^{\prime}}\setminus{\bf V}^{2d}
)

There is
r^{\prime}\in(0,r)
and a
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-invariant analytic function

c_{{3d}}:{\mathcal{N}}_{r^{\prime}}\setminus{\bf V}^{2d}\to B_{r}(c_{*})

which is the unique solution of (
5.1
),
satisfying (
5.5
)
in
{\mathcal{N}}_{r^{\prime}}\setminus{\bf V}^{2d}
and (
5.7
).

In order to highlight the singularity near each
V_{\hat{\jmath}}
,
{\hat{\jmath}}\in{\mathcal{D}}
, we write system (
5.1
) as

(5.19)

\begin{pmatrix}{\rm d}_{v}\Phi(c,v)[\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}(v)]\\
{\rm d}_{v}\Phi(c,v)[\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}^{\bot_{\hat{\jmath}}}(v)]\end{pmatrix}=0

where
{\mathcal{I}}^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}
and
{\mathcal{I}}^{\bot_{\hat{\jmath}}}
the parallel and orthogonal components of
{\mathcal{I}}
as defined by (
4.36
). Actually
system (
5.19
) is obtained applying to (
5.1
) the linear orthogonal matrix
Q_{\hat{\jmath}}
in
(
4.37
).
In view of (
4.42
) and

(c-c_{*})\cdot{\mathcal{I}}(v)=(c-c_{*})^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}{\mathcal{I}}^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}(v)+(c-c_{*})^{\bot_{\hat{\jmath}}}{\mathcal{I}}^{\bot_{\hat{\jmath}}}(v)
,
system (
5.19
) is equivalent to

(5.20)

{\mathcal{A}}^{\hat{\jmath}}(v)(c^{\hat{\jmath}}-c_{*}^{\hat{\jmath}})+{\mathtt{G}}^{\hat{\jmath}}_{\geq 3}(c^{\hat{\jmath}},v)=0

where
{\mathcal{A}}^{\hat{\jmath}}(v)
is the symmetric matrix

(5.21)

{\mathcal{A}}^{\hat{\jmath}}(v):=\begin{pmatrix}{\mathcal{A}}_{11}^{\hat{\jmath}}(v)&{\mathcal{A}}_{12}^{\hat{\jmath}}(v)\\
{\mathcal{A}}_{12}^{\hat{\jmath}}(v)&{\mathcal{A}}_{22}^{\hat{\jmath}}(v)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\langle\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}(v),\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}(v)\rangle&\langle\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}^{\bot_{\hat{\jmath}}}(v),\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}(v)\rangle\\
\langle\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}(v),\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}^{\bot_{\hat{\jmath}}}(v)\rangle&\langle\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}^{\bot_{\hat{\jmath}}}(v),\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}^{\bot_{\hat{\jmath}}}(v)\rangle\end{pmatrix}\,,

the vector
c^{\hat{\jmath}}-c_{*}^{\hat{\jmath}}=(c-c_{*})^{j}=Q_{\hat{\jmath}}(c-c_{*})
(according to (
4.37
)) and

(5.22)

{\mathtt{G}}_{\geq 3}^{\hat{\jmath}}(c^{\hat{\jmath}},v):=\begin{pmatrix}{\mathtt{G}}_{\geq 3}^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}(c^{\hat{\jmath}},v)\\
{\mathtt{G}}_{\geq 3}^{\bot_{\hat{\jmath}}}(c^{\hat{\jmath}},v)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}{\rm d}_{v}G_{\geq 3}(c,v)[\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{I}}^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}(v)]\\
