v\in V\setminus{\bf V}^{{2d}}
we deduce that
c\in B_{{r^{\prime}}}(c_{*})
solves (
5.1
) and thus Lemma
5.1
implies that
c=c_{{3d}}(v)
.
Hence
u=v+w(c(v),v)
.
Otherwise, if
v\in V_{\hat{\jmath}}
for some

\hat{\jmath}\in{\mathcal{D}}
, we have
{\rm d}_{v}\Phi(c,v)={\rm d}_{v}\Phi(c^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}\hat{\jmath},v)
and by (
8.18
) we deduce that
c^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}\in(c_{*}^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}-{r^{\prime}},c_{*}^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}+{r^{\prime}})
solves (
5.37
). Thus by Lemma
5.10
, we conclude that
c^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}\hat{\jmath}=c^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}_{{2d}}(v)\hat{\jmath}=c(v)
and

u=v+w(c^{\parallel_{\hat{\jmath}}}\hat{\jmath},v)=v+w(c(v),v)
by
Lemma
4.11
-
(ii)
.
∎

The momentum of the Stokes wave
u
is equal to

a:={\mathcal{I}}(u)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Lemma}\,\ref{lem:uvw}}}{{=}}{\mathcal{I}}(v+w(c(v),v))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{Def I dritto}}}{{=}}I(v)\,,\quad v\in D_{\varepsilon}\subset U_{2}\,,

and therefore

v\in\mathbb{M}_{a}

(cf. definition (
7.1
)).
Theorem
7.3
implies that
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
, proving (
1.30
).
Since
v\in D_{\varepsilon}
then
|a|\leq\varepsilon
.
The proof of Theorem
1.3
is concluded by the following two cases:

(\mathfrak{B})
] If
a=0
then, Theorem
7.3
implies that
\mathbb{M}_{a}=\{0\}
, thus
v=0
and
u=0
.
If
a\in\partial{\mathcal{C}}\setminus\{0\}
then, Theorem
7.3
-
(\beth)
implies that
v\in\mathbb{M}_{a}\subset{\bf V}^{{2d}}

and we deduce by
Lemma
4.11

that

u=v+w(c(v),v)
is a
2d
wave.

(\mathfrak{I})
] If
a\in\interior({\mathcal{C}})
, and
a
is not collinear with any
\hat{\jmath}\in{\mathcal{D}}
then, Theorem
7.3
-
(\daleth 1)
implies that
v\in\mathbb{M}_{a}\subset V\setminus{\bf V}^{{2d}}
is a
3d
wave, and thus
u=v+w(c(v),v)
is a
3d
wave.

Appendix A
Invariant functions and equivariant flows

We give some properties of
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}
-invariant functions defined on the Euclidean
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}
-space
V

in (
4.9
).

Lemma A.1
.

Let
f:B_{r}^{V}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be a
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}
-invariant function
of class
C^{1}
. Then, for any

\hat{\jmath}\in{\mathcal{D}}
,

(A.1)

{\rm d}_{v}f(\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}v)\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}^{\bot}=0\,,\quad\forall v\in B_{r}^{V}\,.

Proof.

Since
f
is
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}
-invariant,
{\rm d}_{v}f(\tau_{\theta}v)\,\tau_{\theta}={\rm d}_{v}f(\tau_{\theta}v)
for any
v\in B_{r}^{V}
,
\theta\in{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}
,
and therefore, for any
\hat{\jmath}\in{\mathcal{D}}
, for any
\theta
in the subgroup
{\mathtt{T}}_{[j]}\subset{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}
defined in (
4.27
),

(A.2)

{\rm d}_{v}f(\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}v)\pi_{j^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{One dimensional iff simmetric}}}{{=}}{\rm d}_{v}f(\tau_{\theta}\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}v)\pi_{j^{\prime}}={\rm d}_{v}f(\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}v)\pi_{j^{\prime}}\tau_{\theta}

where
\pi_{j^{\prime}}:V\to V_{j^{\prime}}

is the projector.
By
(
A.2
)
the linear operator
{\rm d}_{v}f(\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}v)\pi_{j^{\prime}}:V_{j^{\prime}}\to V
is
\mathtt{T}_{[j]}
-invariant.
If
j^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{V}}\setminus[j]
then
V_{j^{\prime}}
is an irreducible representation of
\mathtt{T}_{[j]}
and, since
{\rm d}_{v}f(\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}v)\pi_{j^{\prime}}
is not injective, Schur lemma implies that

(A.3)

{\rm d}_{v}f(\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}v)\pi_{j^{\prime}}=0\,,\quad\forall j\in{\mathcal{V}}\setminus[j]\,.

By (
A.3
), recalling (
4.32
), we deduce (
A.1
).
∎

By the previous lemma a
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}
-invariant function
f
satisfies
{\rm d}_{v}f(0)=0
and
a critical point
v\in V_{\hat{\jmath}}
of
f_{|V_{\hat{\jmath}}}
is actually a critical point of

{\rm d}_{v}f(v)=0
.

Lemma A.2
.

(
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
-invariant functions)

Let
f:B_{r}(c_{*})\times B_{r}^{V}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be a smooth function with bounded derivatives at any order,
satisfying
f(c,v)={\mathcal{O}}(\|v\|^{m})
uniformly in
c
, for some
m\geq 2
,
and
f(c,\cdot)
is
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}
-invariant for any
c\in B_{r}(c_{*})
. Then, for any
\hat{\jmath}\in{\mathcal{D}}
, the following holds:

(i)

for any
v\in B_{r}^{V}
,
\hat{v}\in V
,

(A.4)

{\rm d}_{v}f(c,v)[\hat{v}]={\mathcal{O}}(\|v\|^{m-1}\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}\hat{v}\|+\|v\|^{m-2}\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}^{\bot}v\|\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}^{\bot}\hat{v}\|)\quad\text{ uniformly in}\ c\,.

(ii)
If

f(c,v)=0
for any
v\in B_{r}^{V}\cap V_{\hat{\jmath}}

then

(A.5)

\displaystyle f(c,v)={\mathcal{O}}(\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}^{\bot}v\|^{2}\|v\|^{m-2})

uniformly in
c
,
and if
m\geq 3
then

(A.6)

\displaystyle{\rm d}_{v}f(c,v)[\hat{v}]={\mathcal{O}}(\|v\|^{m-3}\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}^{\bot}v\|^{2}\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}\hat{v}\|+\|v\|^{m-2}\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}^{\bot}v\|\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}^{\bot}\hat{v}\|)\quad\text{ uniformly in}\ c\,.

Proof.

Since
f(c,\cdot)
is
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}
-invariant for any
c\in B_{r}(c_{*})
, by (
A.1
)
the Taylor expansion of
f(c,\cdot)

at any
\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}v
in the direction

\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}^{\bot}v
is

(A.7)

f(c,v)=f(c,\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}v)+R(c,v)[\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}^{\bot}v]^{2}\,,\qquad\forall v\in B_{r}^{V}\,,

with
quadratic integral Taylor remainder

R(c,v)[h]^{2}:=\int_{0}^{1}(1-s){\rm d}_{v}^{2}f(c,\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}v+s\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}^{\bot}v)[h]^{2}\,{\rm d}s
for any
h\in V
.
Taking the differential in (
A.7
) we get

(A.8)

{\rm d}_{v}f(c,v)[\hat{v}]={\rm d}_{v}f(c,\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}v)[\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}\hat{v}]+{\rm d}_{v}R(c,v)[\hat{v}][\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}^{\bot}v]^{2}+2R(c,v)[\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}^{\bot}v,\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}^{\bot}\hat{v}]\,.

Now
(i)
follows by (
A.8
)
since
|{\rm d}_{v}^{\ell}f(c,v)|\lesssim_{m}\|v\|^{m-\ell}
for any
0\leq\ell\leq m
,
which follows because
f(c,v)={\mathcal{O}}(\|v\|^{m})
uniformly in
c
, is smooth, and has bounded derivatives
(for
m=2
just use that
{\rm d}_{v}^{2}f(c,v)
is bounded).
Item
(ii)
follows similarly by (
A.7
) and (
A.8
) because

f(c,\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}v)=0
and

{\rm d}_{v}f(c,\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}v)\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}=0

for any
v\in B_{r}^{V}\cap V_{\hat{\jmath}}
.
∎

The following lemma is used twice in the paper (for proving Theorem
6.2
and
Proposition

8.4
).

Lemma A.3
.

(Continuous equivariant flow)

Let

J\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
be an open interval. Assume that the vector field
Z:J\times B_{r^{\prime\prime}}^{V}\to V
is

{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}
-equivariant for any
t\in J
, and

(
Z
)

(1)

Z(t,v)
is continuous on
J\times B_{r^{\prime\prime}}^{V}
;

(2)

Z(t,v)
is analytic on
J\times[B_{r^{\prime\prime}}^{V}\setminus{\bf V}^{{2d}}]

and restricted to
J\times[(B_{r^{\prime\prime}}^{V}\cap V_{\hat{\jmath}})\setminus\{0\}]
for any
{\hat{\jmath}}\in{\mathcal{D}}
;

(3)

\|Z(t,v)\|\lesssim\|v\|
for any
(t,v)\in J\times B_{r^{\prime\prime}}^{V}
;

(4)

For any
{\hat{\jmath}}\in{\mathcal{D}}
we have

\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}^{\bot}Z(t,v)\|\lesssim{\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}^{\bot}v\|}
for any
(t,v)\in J\times B_{r^{\prime\prime}}^{V}
.

Then
Z(t,v)
admits a flow
\zeta(t,v)

defined for any
v\in B_{r^{\prime\prime}}^{V}
, locally in time,
which is
continuous and

{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}
-equivariant.
Moreover, for any closed interval
J^{\prime}\subset J
there is
{r^{\prime\prime\prime}}\in(0,r^{\prime\prime})
such that
\zeta
is defined on
J^{\prime}\times B_{r^{\prime\prime\prime}}^{V}
.

Proof.

For any
v_{0}\in B_{r^{\prime\prime}}^{V}
let
v(t)
be a solution of

(A.9)

\dot{v}(t)=Z(t,v(t))\,,\ v(0)=v_{0}\,,\quad t\in J(v)\,,

defined on the open interval
J(v)\subseteq J
containing
0
. By the regularity Assumptions (

Z

),
problem (
A.9
) has always a solution that is unique for any
v_{0}\in B_{r^{\prime\prime}}^{V}\setminus{\bf V}^{2d}
.
However
Z(t,v)
does not satisfy a Lipschitz property near the subspaces
V_{\hat{\jmath}}
,
\hat{\jmath}\in{\mathcal{D}}
.
We deduce
uniqueness of the solution by the following estimates.

Step 1:

there is

C>0
such that any solution

v:J(v)\to B_{r^{\prime\prime}}^{V}
of (
A.9
) satisfies

(A.10)

\|v(t)\|\geq e^{-C|t|}\|v_{0}\|\,,\quad d(v(t),{\bf V}^{{2d}})\geq e^{-C|t|}d(v_{0},{\bf V}^{{2d}})\,,\qquad\forall t\in J(v)\,.

The function
\varphi(t):=\|v(t)\|^{2}

satisfies
\varphi(0)={\|v_{0}\|^{2}}
and, by (
A.9
) and Assumption
{(3)}
,

(A.11)

{|\varphi^{\prime}(t)|=2|\langle Z(t,v(t)),v(t)\rangle|\leq 2C\varphi(t)}\,.

Thus by comparison

{\varphi(t)\geq\varphi(0)e^{-2C|t|}}
for any
t\in J(v)
and we deduce the first inequality in (
A.10
). Let us prove the other one.
For any
{\hat{\jmath}}\in{\mathcal{D}}
, the function
\varphi_{\hat{\jmath}}(t):=\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}^{\bot}v(t)\|^{2}

satisfies
\varphi_{\hat{\jmath}}(0){=\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}^{\bot}v_{0}\|^{2}}
and
by (
A.9
) and Assumption
{(4)}
,

|\varphi_{\hat{\jmath}}^{\prime}(t)|={2|\langle\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}^{\bot}Z(t,v(t)),\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}^{\bot}v(t)\rangle|\leq 2C\varphi_{\hat{\jmath}}(t)}
.
By comparison we get
\varphi_{\hat{\jmath}}(t)\geq\varphi_{\hat{\jmath}}(0)e^{-2C|t|}
and thus

\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}^{\bot}v(t)\|\geq e^{-C|t|}\|\Pi_{\hat{\jmath}}^{\bot}v_{0}\|\geq e^{-C|t|}d({v_{0}},{\bf V}^{{2d}})

(recall (
5.2
)).
Taking the minimum in
{\hat{\jmath}}\in{\mathcal{D}}

we deduce also the second inequality in (
A.10
).

Existence of the flow.

By the regularity Assumption (
2
) the solutions of
(
A.9
) are unique in

B_{r^{\prime\prime}}\setminus{\bf V}^{2d}
.
Assumptions (
3
)-(
4
)
imply that
Z(t,0)=0
and
Z(t,\cdot):V_{\hat{\jmath}}\to V_{\hat{\jmath}}

and thus, if
v_{0}=0
, a solution of the Cauchy problem (
A.9
) is
v(t)=0
and for any
v_{0}\in V_{\hat{\jmath}}\setminus\{0\}
,
a solution of (
A.9
) lives in

V_{\hat{\jmath}}\setminus\{0\}
. These are the unique solutions because,
by (
A.10
), if
v_{0}\in B_{r^{\prime\prime}}^{V}\setminus{\bf V}^{2d}
then the solution
v(t)

of (
A.9
)
will never reach in finite time
{\bf V}^{2d}
.
This proves that, for any
v\in B_{r}^{V}
the flow
v_{0}\mapsto\zeta(t,v):=v(t)
of
Z(t,v)
is well defined locally in time near
t=0
.
The equivariance of
Z(t,v)
and the uniqueness of the solutions of (
A.9
) also imply that

\zeta(t,\cdot)

is
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma}
-equivariant. Furthermore for any closed
J^{\prime}\subset J
the flow
\zeta(t,v)
is defined for any
v\in B_{r^{\prime\prime\prime}}^{V}
sufficiently small. Indeed (
A.11
) implies the upper bound
\varphi(t)\leq\varphi(0)e^{2C|t|}
and therefore

\|v(t)\|\leq\|v_{0}\|e^{2CT}

for any
t\in J^{\prime}
with
T:=\max_{t\in J^{\prime}}{|t|}
. Then it is sufficient to take
r^{\prime\prime\prime}<r^{\prime\prime}e^{-2CT}
.

Continuity of the flow.

Let
D\subseteq J\times B_{r^{\prime\prime}}^{V}
be the domain of definition of the flow
\zeta
i.e. the set of pairs
(t,v_{0})\in J\times B_{r^{\prime\prime}}^{V}
such that the maximal solution of (
A.9
) is defined at time
t
. We claim that the uniqueness of the solutions implies also the continuity of the flow
\zeta:D\to B_{r^{\prime\prime}}^{V}
at any
(\bar{t},\bar{v})\in D
. Let
\{(t_{i},v_{i})\}\subset D
such that
(t_{i},v_{i})\to(\bar{t},\bar{v})
.
Note that, since
Z
is bounded (cf. (

Z

)-
(3)
) we have

(A.12)

|\zeta(v,t)-\zeta(v,t_{1})|\leq\sup_{(s,u)\in J\times B_{r^{\prime\prime}}^{V}}\|Z(s,v)\||t-t_{1}|\lesssim|t-t_{1}|\,\quad\forall(v,t),(v,t_{1})\in D\,.

If
\bar{t}=0
, since
Z(t,v)
is uniformly bounded, we have
|\zeta(\bar{t},\bar{v})-\zeta(t_{i},v_{i})|\leq|\zeta(0,\bar{v})-\zeta(0,v_{i})|+|\zeta(0,v_{i})-\zeta(t_{i},v_{i})|\lesssim|\bar{v}-v_{i}|+|t_{i}|\to 0
by (
A.12
).
Consider the case
\bar{t}>0
(for
\bar{t}<0
the argument is the same).
For any
\tau\in[0,\bar{t})
we have
t_{i}>\tau
for
i
large enough,
each

\zeta(\cdot,v_{i})
and
\zeta(\cdot,\bar{v})
are well defined for
[0,\tau]
, and
we claim that

(A.13)

\sup_{t\in[0,\tau]}|\zeta(t,v_{i})-\zeta(t,\bar{v})|\to 0\quad\text{as}\quad i\to+\infty\,.

For any subsequence
i_{m}
, since the functions
\{\zeta(\cdot,v_{i_{m}}):[0,\tau]\to B_{r^{\prime\prime}}^{V}\}
are uniformly equicontinuous (cf. (
A.12
) ), by Ascoli-Arzelá theorem there exists another subsequence
i_{m_{\ell}}
such that
\zeta(\cdot,{i_{m_{\ell}}})
converges uniformly to a function
\bar{\mathsf{z}}\in C([0,\tau],\overline{B_{r^{\prime\prime}}^{V}})
.
The function
\bar{\mathsf{z}}
is a solution of (
A.9
) with
v_{0}=\bar{v}
on

\mathfrak{p}:=\{t\in[0,\tau]\ |\ [0,t]\subset{\mathsf{\bar{z}}}^{-1}(B_{r^{\prime\prime}}^{V})\}
. The set
\mathfrak{p}
is open in
[0,\tau]
.
By uniqueness,
\mathsf{\bar{z}}(t)=\zeta(t,\bar{v})
for any
t\in\mathfrak{p}
and hence also for
t\in\overline{\mathfrak{p}}
.
By the definition of
\mathfrak{p}
we deduce that
\overline{\mathfrak{p}}\subset\mathfrak{p}
and thus
\mathfrak{p}=[0,\tau]
i.e.
\mathsf{\bar{z}}([0,\tau])\subset B_{r^{\prime\prime}}^{V}
.
We deduce (
A.13
).
By triangular inequality
|\zeta(\bar{t},\bar{v})-\zeta(t_{i},v_{i})|\leq|\zeta(\bar{t},\bar{v})-\zeta(\tau,\bar{v})|+|\zeta(\tau,\bar{v})-\zeta(\tau,v_{i})|+|\zeta(\tau,v_{i})-\zeta(t_{i},v_{i})|

and (
A.12
), (
A.13
) we deduce
\zeta(t_{i},v_{i})\to\zeta(\bar{t},\bar{v})
.
Thus
\zeta
is continuous on
D
.
∎

Solution of the range equation.

We prove Lemma
4.3
.
The range equation (
4.13
) amounts to solve

{\mathcal{G}}(c,v,w)=0
where

{\mathcal{G}}:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times V\times(W\cap X)\to W\cap Y
is

{\mathcal{G}}(c,v,w):=\Pi_{W\cap Y}{\mathcal{F}}(c,v+w)
.
The map
{\mathcal{G}}
is analytic in a small neighborhood of

(c_{*},0,0)
by
[
11
]
[Theorem 1.2]
about the analyticity of the Dirichlet-Neumann operator
and the algebra properties of
H^{\sigma,s}({\mathbb{T}}^{2}_{\Gamma})
.
It results

(A.14)

{\mathcal{G}}(c,0,0)=0\,,\quad{\rm d}_{w}{\mathcal{G}}(c,0,0)=\Pi_{W\cap Y}{\mathcal{L}}_{c|W\cap X}\,.

We now construct the inverse
L:W\cap Y\to W\cap X
of
{\rm d}_{w}{\mathcal{G}}(c_{*},0,0)
.
Let
\mathscr{W}
be the linear span generated by
\{v_{j}^{(1)},v_{j}^{(2)}\ |\ j\in\Gamma^{\prime}\setminus({\mathcal{V}}\cup\{0\})\}
and let
\mathscr{W}_{X}:=\mathscr{W}\cup\{v_{0}^{(1)}\}
and
\mathscr{W}_{Y}:=\mathscr{W}\cup\{v_{0}^{(2)}\}
.
We consider the linear operator
L:\mathscr{W}_{Y}\to\mathscr{W}_{X}
,

(A.15)

\begin{cases}Lv_{j}^{(1)}:=\frac{-1}{c_{*}\cdot j-\omega(j)}v_{j}^{(2)}\\
Lv_{j}^{(2)}:=\frac{1}{c_{*}\cdot j-\omega(j)}v_{j}^{(1)}\end{cases}\quad\forall j\not\in{\mathcal{V}}\cup\{0\}\,,\qquad Lv_{0}^{(2)}:=-\frac{1}{g}v_{0}^{(1)}\,,

which is well defined since
c_{*}\cdot j-\omega(j)\neq 0
for any
j\in\Gamma^{\prime}\setminus({\mathcal{V}}\cup\{0\})
.
By Lemma
4.1

(iii)
, (
A.14
) and (
A.15
) we have

(A.16)

L{\rm d}_{w}{\mathcal{G}}(c_{*},0,0)_{|\mathscr{W}_{X}}=\text{Id}_{\mathscr{W}_{Y}}\,,\quad{\rm d}_{w}{\mathcal{G}}(c_{*},0,0)L=\text{Id}_{\mathscr{W}_{X}}\,.

Lemma A.4
.

\|Lu\|_{H^{\sigma,s}\times H^{\sigma,s}}\lesssim\|u\|_{H^{\sigma,s-1}\times H^{\sigma,s-2}}

for any
u\in\mathscr{W}_{Y}
. Thus
L
extends to a continuous operator
W\cap Y\to W\cap X
(still denoted
L
) that is
an inverse of
{\rm d}_{w}{\mathcal{G}}(c_{*},0,0)
.

Proof.

The analytic norms
\|\ \|_{H^{\sigma,s}}
defined in (
1.19
) of the components of

u=\begin{pmatrix}\eta\\
\psi\end{pmatrix}\in\mathscr{W}
expanded as in
(
4.4
) are, recalling (
4.1
),

(A.17)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\|\eta\|^{2}_{H^{\sigma,s}}=\tfrac{1}{4}\sum\big(|\alpha_{j}(u)+\alpha_{-j}(u)|^{2}+|\beta_{j}(u)-\beta_{-j}(u)|^{2}\big)\,M_{j}^{2}\,|j|^{2s}e^{2\sigma|j|_{1}}

\displaystyle\|\psi\|^{2}_{H^{\sigma,s}}=\tfrac{1}{4}\sum\big(|\alpha_{j}(u)-\alpha_{-j}(u)|^{2}+|\beta_{j}(u)+\beta_{-j}(u)|^{2}\big)\,M_{j}^{-2}\,|j|^{2s}e^{2\sigma|j|_{1}}

where the sums are over
j\in\Gamma^{\prime}\setminus({\mathcal{V}}\cup\{0\})
.
By (
A.15
), the operator
L

acts, in the coordinates
\{(\alpha_{j},\beta_{j})\}_{j}
in (
4.5
), as

\alpha_{j}(Lu)=\tfrac{\beta_{j}(u)}{c_{*}\cdot j-\omega(j)}\,,\quad\beta_{j}(Lu)=\tfrac{-\alpha_{j}(u)}{c_{*}\cdot j-\omega(j)}\,,\quad\forall j\in\Gamma^{\prime}\,,\ \forall u\in\mathscr{W}\,.

In view of (
A.17
), in order to estimate
\|Lu\|_{H^{\sigma,s}\times H^{\sigma,s}}
we consider

(A.18)

\displaystyle\alpha_{j}(Lu)\pm\alpha_{-j}(Lu)=\tfrac{c_{*}\cdot j}{(c_{*}\cdot j)^{2}-\omega(j)^{2}}(\beta_{j}(u)\mp\beta_{-j}(u))+\tfrac{\omega(j)}{(c_{*}\cdot j)^{2}-\omega(j)^{2}}(\beta_{j}(u)\pm\beta_{-j}(u))

\displaystyle\beta_{j}(Lu)\pm\beta_{-j}(Lu)=-\tfrac{c_{*}\cdot j}{(c_{*}\cdot j)^{2}-\omega(j)^{2}}(\alpha_{j}(u)\mp\alpha_{-j}(u))-\tfrac{\omega(j)}{(c_{*}\cdot j)^{2}-\omega(j)^{2}}(\alpha_{j}(u)\pm\alpha_{-j}(u))\,.

By (
1.23
) with positive surface tension
\kappa>0
we have

|(c_{*}\cdot j)^{2}-\omega(j)^{2}|\approx|j|^{3}

for any
j\in\Gamma^{\prime}\setminus({\mathcal{V}}\cup\{0\})

and, since
M_{j}\approx|j|^{-\frac{1}{4}}
by (
4.2
),
then

(A.19)

\Big|\frac{(c_{*}\cdot j)\,|j|}{(c_{*}\cdot j)^{2}-\omega(j)^{2}}\Big|\,,\ \Big|\frac{\omega(j)\,M_{j}^{2}\,|j|^{2}}{(c_{*}\cdot j)^{2}-\omega(j)^{2}}\Big|\,,\ \Big|\frac{(c_{*}\cdot j)\,|j|^{2}}{(c_{*}\cdot j)^{2}-\omega(j)^{2}}\Big|\,,\ \Big|\frac{\omega(j)\,M_{j}^{-2}\,|j|}{(c_{*}\cdot j)^{2}-\omega(j)^{2}}\Big|\leq K\,.

In view of (
A.17
), (
A.18
) and (
A.19
) the analytic norms of the components of

Lu=\begin{pmatrix}\eta_{1}\\
\psi_{1}\end{pmatrix}
satisfy

\|\eta_{1}\|^{2}_{H^{\sigma,s}}\leq K^{2}\big(\|\eta\|_{H^{\sigma,s-1}}^{2}+\|\psi\|_{H^{\sigma,s-2}}^{2}\big)\,,\quad\|\psi_{1}\|^{2}_{H^{\sigma,s}}\leq K^{2}\big(\|\eta\|_{H^{\sigma,s-1}}^{2}+\|\psi\|_{H^{\sigma,s-2}}^{2}\big)\,.

By (
A.16
)
the extended continuous operator
L:W\cap Y\to W\cap X

is an inverse of
{\rm d}_{w}{\mathcal{G}}(c_{*},0,0)
.
∎

By the Implicit function theorem there is
r>0
and an analytic function
w:B_{r}(c_{*})\times B_{r}^{V}\to W\cap X
solving

{\mathcal{G}}(c,v,w(c,v))=0
for all

(c,v)\in B_{r}(c_{*})\times B^{V}_{r}

and satisfying

w(c,0)=0
for any
c\in B_{r}(c_{*})

because
{\mathcal{G}}(c,0,0)\equiv 0
. The equivariance properties (
4.15
) follow by
those of
{\cal F}(c,u)
inherited by
(
1.10
), (
1.11
) and
uniqueness. Finally differentiating
0={\mathcal{G}}(c,v,w(c,v))

at
v=0
we obtain, since
w(c,0)=0

0=\underbrace{{\rm d}_{v}{\mathcal{G}}(c,0,0)}_{\Pi_{W\cap Y}{{\mathcal{L}}_{c}}_{|V}=0\ \text{as}\,{\mathcal{L}}_{c}V\subseteq V}+\,{\rm d}_{w}{\mathcal{G}}(c,0,0)\,{\rm d}_{v}w(c,0)={\rm d}_{w}{\mathcal{G}}(c,0,0)\,{\rm d}_{v}w(c,0)\,.

Since
{\rm d}_{w}{\mathcal{G}}(c,0,0)
is invertible we deduce
{\rm d}_{v}w(c,0)=0
.
The proof is concluded.
